===== 0002601_19150522_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
BITS FOR BEGINNERS.

If my readers knew what prolific layers ducks were they would certainly try them. It is: not everybody who can take- them up, but there are many who are admirably situated, and yet do not keep ducks. Ducks' eggs are always in great demand for edible purposes. ' ;Water is not essential for ducks, although it an advantage to have a pond or stream close ai hand for stock ducks. With ducklings that are being fattened for the table swimming should be 'discouraged, so that it is not neces- sary for them to have water except to drink. Geese culture is a very profitable side-line, aid those who can should keep a few geese. The Platter are noted grazers and will clip a patch of grass as clean as any donkey. There is one thing to be said in their favour, and that is that the quality of the grass does not count, for they are "at home" on the poorest land. There are many admirably situated as regards waste land, and yet they do not trouble to take Up geese culture. Geese find the majority of their food, and those who. have waste or common land near at hand with a running stream or a pond should rear a few goslings

now. • It is now that the turkeys intended for the Christmas market' should see light of day. There is good money in turkey-rearing for the Christmas trade, and this side-line should be tried this year by all who have the space and time.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
ATTEND TO YOUNG STOCK.

Every breeder, says the " leathere,l World," will now be busy in the yard, for the young stock needs constant attention from early morn till late at night. The agricultural shows usually put on a class or two for chickens, and they fill well With January-hatched stock, which alio almost up to the size of their parents. Many breeders, who cannot grow chickens fast, rail against these birds and assert them to be over age, whereas if they would only find out how to hatch as soon as the New Year breaks and then td feed properly right the way through, there would be less grumbling. I know one well-known breeder who had made up his mind to produce some chickens for the early summer shows which could win and do him, credit both for quality and size. The birds were not hatched till January, and his man used to feed them with a light every morn- ing and night, and they were kept in a warm shed during the coldest part of the weather. These chickens grew so fast and made up so quickly that when Julie arrived more than one breeder said they were over age, and a promi- nent judge passed them at one show, and yet the time of hatching is correctly stated above. Much depends upon feeding and management whether the stock does well, and what one can do another should be able if only possessed of the same grit and determination.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
START THE CHICKS RIGHT.

There is a lot of meaning for the utility poultry-keeper in the four words "Start, the Chicks right." If they are not started right nothing will be accomplished, they will not live long, and there can be no hopes of them de- veloping into good specimens, because if the chicks are not started -right from the begin- ning they will probably never amount to any- thing, and this will lead to disappointment. In the first place they must be hatched from eggs that will produce good strong chicks. The breeding stock from which they are hatched must be in the pink of condition—they must be fed on food that will produce eggs from which strong, healthy chicks can be hatched. If the breeding stock is not right in "every way dis- appointment' and failure will be sure to over- take you before you are very old.

They must be properly incubated, whether by natural or artificial means, or they will hatch weakly chicks. If incubated by machine you must have Moisture, ventilation and tempera- ture right in the egg chamber. Under natural incubation with the old hen these details will be correct, but with the incubator you must get them as near correct as you can. Most of the incubators sold to-day are good ones, and if given the right care and attention will hatch good, strong, healthy birds. In most cases where the incubator does not hatch good, strong chicks, it is the fault of the operator.— "Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
BLOOD SPOTS IN EGGS.

A speck of blood sometinies is found in a new- laid egg, giving the purchaser the impression that it is stale or partly incubated. As a rule, it is the result of over-stimulation, due either to too generous feeding or to an irritant in the diet. The blood is from a ruptured blood vessel. When the speck is found in the yoke, it is due to the rupture of a blood vessel in the ovary.; when it, is in the white the ruptured vessel is in the oviduct.. As a remedy a little Epsom salts and tincture of iron are useful. To a gallon of water there should be added a teaspoonful of tincture of iron and two table- spoonfuls of Epsom salts.—Middlesex, in "Farm and Home."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
PRODUCE MORE.

It is not easy to recoMmend the continual breeding season in face of present conditions, and yet every one should try and produce more this Year than usual, because of the big demand which must occur when the war is over. I know of some of Our most noted breeders, says a writer in the "Feathered World," who are stopping at half of what they produced last year, partly because they expect a, less demand and partly through the increased cost of food. There will probably be fewer birds sold at twenty pounds than formerly, but for all good utilitY, stock there must be a bigger demand than ever. For one thing, they say the war is not over and may not be for months and prob- ably years, hence the idea of producing more stock is foolish and should not be encouraged. Those who have followed my writings in the past know that such is not in my mind, and the end may come sooner than most of us think. Certain is it that no nation, can go on for long losing men at the rate of the past three months unless it is intended to take the whole manhood of the nations concerned and put them /into the firing line. The past week or two rinay look black, but such must be expected, and we have to steel our,. nerves for the worst.. Thousands of homes will be bereaved and hearts made heavy, but let us consider the sanctity of the cause for which we give the best of our lives, then we shall feel that duty has been done. I often wonder which is the harder, to go out and take an active part in the conflict, or to stay at home to carry on as near usual as possible, waiting for news of those who are near and dear.

DUST BATH.

Where fowls are on range or in large runs

and the ground is broken no provision need be made for a dust bath. If they are kept in small runs, however, a dust bath is essential in order to enable the birds to keep themselves free from parasites. An excellent form, triangular in shape, may be fitted into a corner of the house or run. Where used under cover it only requires to be boarded 6in. high all round, forming a movable box with three equal sides. The cover prevents the birds roosting above. If used outside it should be made with solid sides, leaving the front open above the tetaining boards. One or two sugar boxes, which can be bought for a few pence, contain enough npterial for making an indoor or out- door dusting box, as the case may be.—Board of Agriculture Leaflet.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
KEEP DOWN PARASITES.

Without doubt many a bird which gives an unsatisfactory yield of eggs has had it put down to poor strain when the actual reason has been the neglect to keep down parasites; while again, there is.an enormous mortality amongst chicks from this cause alone year after year. Paraffin is most useful and Cheap, and is splendid for cleaning and spraying interiors Of houses. Its general application to birds is not recommended except for scaly legs. I have found it extremely useful for applying a little on the finger to the heads of young chicks where they are usually attacked. It, however, is an irritant, so must, if used on the birds/except the legs, be employed very sparingly. - Creosote is another splendid thing for poultry- house work, and here again I have seen it used and have used it for scaly legs, whilst if the ends of perches were painted once a fortnight or oftener with creosote the red mite would easily be kept down. Limewash is a useful and cheap disinfectant, and I have found live lime, kerosene, and quassia chips applied hot, a really useful and effective preventive, and I recommend that two or three applications a year be used ong I houses, &c.- -" The Poultry World."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN GUARDIANS.

couili Attitude Re Cases Of Mentally Defective.

Mr. C. Linaker, jun., presided at the fort- nightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians, at Dutton, on Tuesday.

SUBSCRIPTION TO EYE AND EAR HOSPITAL. ,

Mr. Morris proposed that the Board give an annual donation of six guineas to the Liverpool Eye and Ear Hospital. They had a number of cases tested at the hospital every year, and it was half a guinea each case. The Board would soon recover the six guineas if they decided to make the donation Mr

Wright seconded, and the meeting

agreed.

A WOBBLY WALL.

Mr. Morris wanted to know why the wall at Dutton Lodge Children's Home had not been Pulled down. Who 'would be responisible if the wall collapsed and killed or injured any of the children? The landlord had promised to pull the wall down, but he had not yet fulfilled his word.

A letter was read from Mr. C. E. Linaker, stating that he proposed to take down the wall entirely and put up a pale-fence. "One gentleman," said Mr. Morris, "stated that he could push the wall over with one hand." (Laughter.) The Chairman: Yes! Well what I say is: Why the dickens hasn't he done so? It is a bit wobbly, but it's not as bad as that. (Renewed laughter.)

===== 0002601_19150522_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MENTALLY DEFECTIVE.

Dr. Meredith Young, county medical officer, wrote that the committee which had been appointed to carry Out a scheme for the care of the mentally defective persons in the county were prepared to advise the parents or guardians of such persons as to the best means of looking after them, and to arrange for the admission of suitable cases to a special institu- tion. In cases where the circumstances of the family were such that they could not afford to pay for their care in an institution, the com- mittee were prepared to bear the cost of it. In cases where the parents could afford to con- tribute something towards the cost of this special treatment, the committee would ask for a small contribution. The committee had appointed officers to inquire into mentally defective cases and to give medical and general, advice as to the best means of dealing with them.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. ADAMS LEAVING THE ARMY.

Dr. Adams wrote announcing that he would shortly be able to take up whole time duty again as medical officer to the Guardians, his resignation from the Army on account of ill- health having been accepted, with permission to retain his rank and to wear the prescribed uniform.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
INMATES AND VAGRANTS.

It was reported that the number of inmates at present in the House was 129. a like figure to that for the. corresponding week of last year. The vagrants numbered 64, as against 87.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 21, 1915.

STORIES FROM THE TRENCHES

MAJOR CROSFIELD.

Bl'llll', Energetic, And Thought The

World Of.

A Warrington Territorial writing.home, says: Thanks very much for your letter dated the 29th _April. We got your two parcels all right, and I sent a field P.C. to let you know, hoping you got it; because I have come to the con- elusion that you don't get half the letters we write. Of course war time is war time, and it is a proper war just at present. It is getting worse every day. I don't know whether the Germans are making it hot for their last desperate dash or not, but they have something in the wind. ' We are very lucky this time in; although this is the sixth day we have had none killed, and I believe we have to stay in another few days yet, on account of the big battles that have been. taking place. Leave it to me to keep my head down, mam! I myself want to get home again just as much as you want to see, me back, but let us hope for the best and trust to our Saviour to carry our hopes through, because it is a miracle how we get to•the trench. Bullets come like rain from all directions, plenty dropping at our feet; and as for shells, well when they try that game it is a case of stopping where we are and trust- ing that one won't come our way, because if we run we might run straight into it. I tell you who takes my eye. It .is Major Crosfield. He is working day and night, and he is not frightened of doing anything; he gives orders to be done, and if it is very dangerous he will do it himself. He deserves all the praise Warrington people can give him, and as for the chaps here they think the world of him, and would face even death itself if he only told them. They have great confidence in him, and inhere is a mait who I wish gets home to War- rington after this it is Major Crosfield, the pride of the 111th South Lancs. Territorials. He is great, and out here we have a very good name for our work. I am still .thankful that I can say I am keeping in the best of health.

Make Nice Ornaments.

Have you seen the souvenirs Jack has sent home, and did you hear what was in the letter that he sent along with them? I am trying my best to get the same sort of things to!send you. -They are the noses of shells the Germans try to hit us with constantly, they make a nice ornament. I very often see J. Hatton and T. Hughes knocking about, they are looking all right. How did our anniversary go off this time? Did any of you go? I was thinking about it between '6.30 and 8, wishing I was there. Did you get my photos—big ones and little ones? Please don't forget my 21st birth- day, it is killing my pig to think I have to spend it he. but do send me a jolly good parcel for it, so that I can keep it up here and ,you keep it up at home, and although we are far away we _will be thinking of one another and making the best of things.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
LITTLE• TEA-PARTY.

But None 01 The Cernuns Were

Invited !

Writing to Mrs. E. Proud, of 82, Priestley- street, to thank her for a welcome parcel received just when starting out for the trenches, Private George Lewis says :

"If you could only see the envy—just for the time being—when one happens to have a parcel on starting out, you cchildn't help laugh- ing. But we don't keep it all to ourselves, oh, no! We just dive in one with another. . . Why, we had quite a little tea-party . . . not a great way from*the germans, I can assure you. But you can, bet your bottom dollar we didn't invite„ any of them to tea. Not likely! "As I am writing this note I am enjoying one of your fags—which is another luxury to ' Tommy 'in the trench. Your husband is still out here. I am doing champion, thank God; and my only wish is to get back to my wife and kiddies'. . . , If I have the luck to pull through it will make a different man of me. . "

===== 0002601_19150522_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
SHELL.IN THE SOAP.

Warrington Corporal's Luck During

Charge Across Fields.

Corporal Leslie Seymour, of the 4th King's (Liverpool) Regiment, Lahore Brigade, Sirhind Division, writing to his wife at 19, Edward- street, Bewsey—from "somewhere abroad " says':—

"I have gone through. a few experiences here, I can tell you. I ,wouldn't like any of you to see it. I had a narrow squeak last week; we were going to make an attack from our trenches to help the French, and to get at the French trenches we had to charge in the open, across fields. Shrapnel shells and 'Jack Johnsons' were bursting over our heads. One, shrapnel shell burst Eight against the ; a piece of the shell twisted my haversack-buckle, made a big hole in the haversack, and buried itself in my towel and soap with a piece of the buckle around it. • "I got away with just a little scratch on my back—thanks to my haversack being well en my shoulders, instead of being on my side.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0037.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Few Have Gone Under."

"A few have gone under who came out with me. One, a sergeant—poor fellow—:tadn't been in the trenches a half-day when a .shell caught him. My company officer was killed right out, leading us. You see them go out with you; but as .for returning, that is luck I think ! "AI Graves Showing In' Glow."

"What would you think to see 3 church as big as ours on fire—all red, with all the graves showing in the glow? We went by one that the Germans had shelled, and we had to keep, ducking, I can tell you, as the flare was showing us going by. "Our fellows have to put pads over their mouth and nose, as the devils are using gas- fumes from their shells; it overcomes you, and • you are helpless. We have just come out of the trenches, And are going higher up some- where.

"Don't send me anything, only keep writing and let me know how you and the children are getting on; that is all I want. We get plenty of smokes, and if you sent me some cakes they might all be smashed up. . . . Money is no good here. You don't think about beer if you do happen to drop on any; it's like drinking herb beer not sweetened. I had a letter from twist from him. That is acceptable—about an olive of twist every week—as it is all soft tobacco and cigarettes here Keep writing and let me know how you are going on . . .; that's what will cheer me np---know- ing that you and the children are getting on all right . . ." •

===== 0002601_19150522_art0038.txt | included=true | included =====
HAIL OF SHELLS AND BULLETS.

A Rather " Hot " Sunday.

Rifleman H. J. Welch, King's Royal Rifles, who, up to the time of his enlistment was a clerk in the employ of Mr. T. Proud, account- ant, Sankey-street, writing to Mr. Proud on May 12th, says:—

_ "You must excuse me not answering your letter, but the day after I got it we went into action and I can tell you we got it rather hot, too. I call tell you it was tlie worst Sunday I have ever spent in my life. I don't care *here you went—if you weren't being pelted with 'Jack Johnsons ' and shrapnel you were in a hail of bullets from machine guns. Our casualties were none too light. A Brave Sergeant-Major.

"We lost our sergeant-major, and I can safely say there was not a braver and better man ever lived. When we first started advanc- ing he was shot in the foot (in fact he had half his foot blown off), and he was taken back: with some more wounded to the reserve dug- outs. Although he must have been in great pain, he started singing to the others to cheer them up. While be and some others were there a 'coal-box' came and blew them all to smithereens. I got away with a shrapnel bullet through my hut, so I was lucky."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
" 'MIEN THE IMES BREAK."

Telegraph Operators Have Many

Thrilling Experiences.

"I am writing this while on duty at our headquarters on the telephone, and the Ger- mans have just sent a few shells close by. Our artillery' have asked me where the shells.. are dropping, as we depend upon them to retaliate —they tell me they will give them a few in return." So, from helgium, writes Private Jack Litherland to his parents at 9, Winwick-road, Warrington. "I am very pleased to hear that you received the small parcel I sent you," he declares; "so you can realise now what we have to dodge at times. . . . I never felt better, . . . and I am expecting always to keep so. . . Thank you for the parcel. . . . It came . . the day we were going into the trenches, and when I got it there, my mate and I did good justice to the contents—and I can tell you we did enjoy it! Besides that, we carried twelve fresh eggs and other provi- sions, so you may judge it is my intention not to come to much harm as regards food. . . . "I want you to send some plain P.C.'s, two copying pencils, a packet or two of lemonade '.powder, and any other thlngs you think I will need. •

===== 0002601_19150522_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
"The Old Tale."

"Now' I come to the old tale about us being in the trenches again. . . . I have told you that my job out here is that, ot telephone and telegraph operator in the trenches; and we have many thrilling experiences—especially when the wires break and we cannot get into communication with headquarters; and it is most inportant that we should. We get out to find the break, sometimes under rifle-fire and sometimes under shell-fire.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
Piecing Broken Lines.

"This occurred when I was last in the trenches. The Germans' sent one or two of their whizz-bangs over, and broke a line from the trench I was in. So I went out to find the break, and had to craNyl in the open—on my hand and knees—so that I would not be ob- served. At length I found the break, which. was luckily not far from our dug-put. I pieced the break, and got back again; we then got in communication with headquarters again, with just a little delay. "The same thing occurred with one of the trenches; but the line was broken all over the place, and, the fellow pieced as many of the breaks as he could find. But we could not get communication very well until our lines- men went out at night to fix things up. I may mention that theirs is a very trying job— more so when thy have to mend the wires in full view of the enemy, who send bright lights up. - "The Sights We Saw."

"The village where we were billeted was shelled the other day, and a bit of damage was done by the Germans. The sights we saw were enough to make anyone go straight over into the German trenches to settle accounts with them there and then. It was a very pitiable sight,to see the villagers going from the village with their bundles, and little children lagging behind. I have seen pictures of them very often in the papers, but I never thought I should see such sights in reality—or buildOgs knocked to pieces! "On the same day we had the first casualty in our section. One of our chaps—poor fellow —was taking a message on a cycle, when a shell dropped and burst against him; and when he was picked out of the hole that the shell had made he was badly wounded, and has since died. He was a very good and cheerful fellow; we miss him very much. His parents have the deepest sympathy of the whole of our section; and they can rest assured that he died an honourable death, doing his duty for Xing and country. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
"Plenty Knocking Round Town,"

"The sooner some young men of Warrington come to realise what we are up against, the better; and also the sooner will be the end of this great war. I mean the young men who have no real dependents upon them—l know there are plenty knocking about the town. . ."

times in a dozen yards. --I thought I had gone' every time. As we ran trees kept dropping right in our way, big stout trees, too, big enough to kill a dozen men if one fell on them. One branch hit me and knocked me down. I was soon up again and off. I would go a few more yards when a shell would hit the ground almost in front of me and cover me w,ith dirt. At last we got through, and some of us were standing against a rained .house when a shell came over and hit an officer and he (poor fellow) went up in the air. That, was enough for me. I did even time for about 60 yards, , and then I Stopped and started to laugh at another fellow. Almost at the same time the Germans opened fire with their machine guns and I stopped a couple. That did it. I had no field dressing, so I whipped the gas pad off My mouth and made a bandage of it. It made more laughing for them, while I cursed the Kaiser and all the Germans in Flanders for everything. Warrington the Finest Place in Europe.

"Then I.made tracks for the dressing station, and then for us all the way. And they sent me to the Duchess of Westminster's Hospital. It was the loveliest place I have ever seen. It was just like being in a palace. The Duchess came to my bedside and brought me some fags and matches, but I didn't know who she was until after.

"Sorry I cannot write more as I have no paper, so I will now close, hoping to be in the dear old town very soon; it is the finest place in Europe, bar none."

TRENCHES BEFORE SANKEY- STREET.

A Straight Appeal.

Mr. H. Higham, 94, Wilkinson-street, has re- ceived a fine recruiting letter from his brother, Private Walter Higham, R.A.M.C., who formerly resided in Oakland-street

_ "Every available man is wanted here," he says. "I was reading an article in the paper you sent me about the large number of single men walking about in Sankey-street, when their proper place is out here, to give their fellow men a helping hand,as well to defend their country and home. Towns and villages which were once the happy homes of the French and Belgians, are nothing now but a mass of ruins, with thousands of refugees, not knowing where to lay their heads at night, let alone the men, women, and children who have perished beneath the ruins of their homes."

"If It Happened in Warrington."

"Let them picture this scene, and then ask themselves what they would think if it hap- pened in Warrington. A beautiful town one day—a mass of ruins the next. Well, let us hope and trust to God that the likes of what is happening here never reaches our country. But I say this: If we are to live the free life, as we have always done, by the help of our fore- fathers, who fought -before us, to retain our proud nation, then we shall want every than we can possibly get. The longer they delay coming forward, the longer they are going to prolong this war. People who imagine that it is going to be over soon are very greatly mis- taken, as our enemy are pastmasters in what they term 'war,' but which in reality is bar- barity.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
Our Barbarous Foe.

"I doubt' if the heathen Chinese would. stoop .so low in war as the Germans have already done.

"You will see in the paper about them using the asphyxiating gas shells. Not only do they use the shells, but they are using a kind of spray from their trench into ours, which ren- ders our men helpless, and those•who survive are lucky. . We have had hundreds of these cases. Dr. Haldane was here a few days ago examining some of the patients. It is pitiful to see them suffer.

"I was surprised to see in the paper the names of Warrington men, whose regiments are with us. I was sorry when I found that Ser- geant Hamblett, who was killed, belonged to Warrington. I am looking after his mate, SO- geant , who is in hospital. He was with him when he was shot. Sergeant Hamblett was well respected by his men, and they lose a good man. . . . I see that our ' Terriers ' have lost a few more men, but they have been very lucky so far, and I hope that they :will be fortunate to come through without much loss."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILD LABOUR.

ileman C. F. Wilson Tells Of War-Time Scheme for Agricultural Exciting Experiences. Districts.

Rifleman Charles, Frederick Wilson, 26, Clif- ton-street, Warrington, of the King's Royal Rifles, and formerly a moulder at Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Co., was recently wounded as we reported last week, and is now in an English hospital. He sends us a very good description of the exciting experiences he has gone through at the Front.

"I will give you," he says, "a description of what war i like in Flanders. I will kick-off something like this: The first two nights near the firing-line we made a new breast4orks at , with the Germans only 40 'yards in front of us, and none of us knew. When they sent a starshell up we had to drop flat on our stomachs in mud and water. I stood right on. top of the breastworks and made loop-holes with bullets whizzing past my ears like peas. When we finished it we were the first to occupy it: The first heavy bombardment we had there was on the night of March, Ist, at 12.30. Our 'D' Company were to make 'an attack on the enemy's trenches and we thought it was a secret affair, but the Germans knew just as well as us it was coming off.

At a meeting of the Lancashire Education Committee, Mr. J.J. .Cockshott (chairman of the Elementary Education Sub-committee) directed attention to the circular issued by the Board of Education concerning the employ- ment of school children in agriculture during the period of the war, and moved the approval with the view of their adoption .by other dis- trict committees of draft regulations submitted by the Garstanig Local Area Committee. These set forth that before consenting to the absenee of a child from school to help in agricultural work the local authority must be satisfied that adult labour cannot be secured, that adequate wages had been offered for adult labour, and that adequate wages would be 4 given to the child; that other children not required to attend school were not available, and that the attendance at school of a child for whom ap- plication was made had been satisfactory. The regulations further provided that the employ- ment must be suitable, that absence from school in any case would not be allowed for More than six weeks consecutively; no child would be excused frOm attendance for this purpose under the age of twelve years, and a record of each case would be.kept, "stating the names and addresses of the employer and the child, period for which absence had been granted, the weekly wages paid, and the con- ditions generally under which the child had been allowed leave of absence.

Mr. Cockshott said that between February 1 and April 30 last only twenty-five boys and two girls had been withdrawn from the schools for the purpose of assisting in agriculture. In view of the fact that there were between 17,000 and 20,000 boys and girls, twelve years of age and upwards, in the schools in the county area, the withdrawals had been very slight indeed. He hoped it would not be neces- sary during the. summer months to have any extensive withdrawals, especially if the holi- days were arranged during the harvest period and the extra week's holiday was granted. Sir Henry Hibbert said the school managers would arrange the holidays according to the needs of the particular districts. An amendment to the effect that absence from school in any case should not be per- mitted for more than six weeks consecutively nor for more than one period of six weeks in any year, was lost by 15 votes to 9. The scheme outlined by Mr. Cockshott was adopted.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Lost Heavily, But Took The 'Trenches _

"This is how it started. Our bailliy were to fire three shots and then the charpfte,4as to be launched. As soon as 'D' Companf iharged the Germans they mowed them dn like sheep. They went out 90 strong and came back about 19, but they took the trenches all right. That night you couldn't bear yourself speaking. The shells came over like hail- stones. I know someone that night who bayoneted a German officer to the trench (poor devil !). Well, we stayed at about seven weeks. Then we had a bit of a rest. After wards we set out for that well-known place , and I can tell you it is simply murder there. Hill 60, Neuve Chapelle, and Mons are not in it. If-you go out of the trenches they don't snipe at you; they open fire on you with a machine-gun. About a fortnight ago we re- tired a mile and a half to make the British line a bit straighter. We retired to a place called the Chateau; and you get some awful shell-fire there.

"They were shelling all the time and we went to the dug-outs for a little rest. While the 4th Battalion Rifle Brigade dug new trenches they lost pretty fair wounded. The next two days their artillery quietened down,

and then our turn came for the trenches. We landed in about nine o'clock and everything Was very quiet all night. We kept saying to one another: 'There's something in the wind,' and there was, too.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
The German Fire.

"They kicked off about the usual time— eight olclock, meal-time—and they kept up a terrific fire till 9.40, when they started to ad- vance. They advanced about thirty yards and then retired. Then their guns seemed to slow down ',a, bit, but it was only for about ten minutes. It was then they started that ter- rible cross-fire; we got coal-boxes' along the trenches and 'Jack Johnsons' and shrapnel overhead, and gases right in front • of the trench. We were lying in the bottom of the trench all the time; smoking. I was next to our officer, and he was the best fellow I have ever met in my life. We were smoking Wood- bines' and Three Castles' all the time the bombardment was going on. Ile kept saying: Keep your heart up!' and I passed it down to all the boys to keep cool. About 10.15 we got the order to retire through the woods, and we left the fire trench and went through the communication trench. Then we had to cross an open space about forty yards long. It was then that the Germans saw us.

EFFECT OF FOOD ON QUALITY OF MILK. The quality of milk is now known to be 3 factor which is so bound up with the character of the cow that the-effect .of feeding is quite e, secondary one. It must not be denied, hovr- ever, that this influence does exist to some extent, and this the Offerton Hall experiments show, while confirming-at the, same time what is above stated. They are very instructive, and owing to the preliminary trials on the charac- ter of the cows, bring out the significant fact that in most cases they followed their natural propensities in regard to richness of milk, whether they were fed heavily or not.—" Agri; cultural Economist and Horticultural Review.'

IMPROVED FARMING PROSPECTS.

It is no exaggeration to say that the recent downpours have averted immediate disaster and diminished the risk of permanent damage. Many farm crops were in a very critical stage. The winter wheat, with its roots already spread well into the soil, looked fairly well, although backward, but the early spring corn and the later sowings were evidently affected. The insect enemies, always ready to seize their opportunity were active, The grass areas presented an even more dis- heartening appearance. A prolongation of the drought would have involved an immediate and substantial increase in the prices of milk, as well as of meat and vegetables. Producers of these articles may now be able to defer a step which a week ago seemed inevitable.

Needs of the Army.

• Exceptional interest centres in the hay err this year because of the heavy requirements of the military authorities. It is estimated that the needs of the War Office amount to about one-fourteenth of the average yield. In view of this imperative claim farmers were greatly per- turbed by ' the prospect that confronted thew in the middle of the week. Not only were their clovers and mixed seeds backward, but neces' sity had compelled them to draw upon the enclosed areas for the immediate needs of the flocks. Hundreds of acres have ha'd to be hurdled than in happier circumstances would have been mown. ' The value of the deep rooting sainfoin lucerne has again been demonstrated. The. latter is the more prolific but it does not lend itself to rotation farmink, and therefore its cultivation is more restricted than otherwise would be the case; but sainfoin, corning quicker to maturity, is one of the best standbls on light soils, especially where intensive shel3"

IN THE GARDEN.

THE COMMON IVY.

This is the best of all evergreen wall climbers, giving a snug, warm look to any building that ;5 covered with it. It, however, needs attention t° keep it within bounds, otherwise it will find its way into water spouts and gutters, and choke them up. Now is the time to cut it close in, 119 the season of active growth being with us, the young growth soon develops and takes off the bare look caused by the clipping. In addition, by clipping in this way a deal of rubbish that accumulates during the season can be got rid of. A small reaping hook or a pair of shears ;5 the best tool to use for cutting it in. Care should be taken to clear the ivy off the Iva' below the gutters for at least one foot, other- wise it will quickly fill them up and cause obstruction, thus allowing the water to run over and percolate down the wall, rendering it damp. Many people have an idea that causes the wall to be damp, but, says and Home," we have never found this the case' provided that annual cutting is carried out.

VOLUNTEER TRAINING CORPS.

Local Movement I.ndergoing Reorganisation.

In the days of old, Warrington and its environs supplied the settings for many a gory fight. The green fields round about have, we hope, seen the last of this kind of warfare, but warfare of another kind may, at no distant date, deputise for tilos more sanguinary article. There are many men who, debarred by age or physical disability, or possibly by some "good and valid reason .approved by the recruiting officer," from joining the regular forces, are yet quite capable of being made into very use- ful soldiers. There is 4 special organisation in existence for these men, and it is known as the Volunteer Training Corps or Home Defence League. Much has been accomplished by various corps of this now gigantic home army in different parts of the country, but up to the present, for a number of reasons, the Warring- ton corps has not prospered over-much. It has, however, taken on a new lease of life, and all sorts of schemes for making the movement attractive to the man who throws in his lot ' with it, as well as useful to the nation in a possible emergency, are being evolved, amongst them mock battles with other local corps. The success of these plannings, however, is dependent on the amount of support accorded the promulgators by those who are at present outside the movement, but have it in their power to help.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====

To the Editor of "The Examiner."

Sir,—According to your report of the local Town Council meeting of the discussion which took place on the resolution of the Ealing Town Council, asking other municipalities to support a measure for facilities to be given to local authorities to give grants in aid of Volunteer Training Corps, several of the members ap- peared to be labouring under the misapprehen- sion that these corps are not recognised by the Government. Sir A. Conan Doyle has stated that there are at present over 400,000 men wearing the brassard (the War Office recogni- tion). These Men when called upon by the authorities will rank as combatants, and form part of the organised military forces. Already use has been made of these corps. Glasgow sup- plies 60 men on munition work, each man work- ing a six-hour shift at night Staines, in Mid- dlesex, has 65 men on patrol duty, and in places on the East Coast these corps have been called upon to perform military duty. Turning to the financial side of the question. the Humber Conservancy Board have granted £1,500, Liverpool £l,OOO. Grimsby £5OO, Keigh- ley £l5O, Stockton -.£lloo. Having in view the high rates in the town the corps could not expect to receive a• large grant. If one were made it would serve as a nucleus of a fund to be augmented by public subscription. '

On behalf of the corns we cordially invite the entire,: Council to join the movement which would give decided impetus to recruiting. This has been.done in many towns, Bradford in particular.—Yours, &c., P. H. BENNETT, W. ASHTON, Joint Hon. Secs. ' Warrington Volunteer Training Corps. Warrington, May 21, 19115.

MUSKETRY.

•At the Legh-street range an interesting com- petition took place last week, and the following were the prize-winners:— First file: F. W. Wright and J. Norman. Second file: F. Fox and A. Hands. Class 1, A. Bailie; 2, S. Williams; 3, 137 Shake- shaft; 4, C. Hoult; 5, G. Reeves.

"THE RUSSIAN FABLE."

At Last ! The Truth About The Russians.

There are still people who believe the stories of the passing of vast bodies of Russian troops through England and Scotland last autumn. Here is the truth at last, which appears in the 1915 Edition of " Sell's World's Press" (pub- lished at '4. tile Sign of the Sundial;" 1166, Fleet-street, E.G.. price ss. net) in an import- ant article on "The Censorship." "In addition to its duties of preventing in- formation reaching the enemy," says the writer, Mr. H. J. B. Steele, hon. secretary of the Press Representative' Committee at the

Press Bureau, "the Bureau has to check the circulation of unfounded rumours. Unfor- tunately important military movements at the Salt rendered difficult the scotching of what will go down in history as 'the Russian troops myth.' The facts regarding this famous story are simple. "A large number of Russian Gilicdrg visited this country to buy munitions of war, or to join as attaches the staffs of the various/ commands then leaving for the front. Accompanying them was a number of soldier servants, and the bulk travelled from Archangel to Scottish ports. These officers and men were undoubtedly seen by reliable witnesses. The same week-end the War Office decided to change the location of camps of the Territorials then in training. To secure this end, the trains were hand-signalled and moved at night with blinds drawn. The ermine drivers knew nothing of the loads they pulled. Meanwhile, however, some Scottish railway officials had corresponded with southern friends and talked of living a lot of Russians travelling on their line. The rest was easy, and the handful grew to an enormous array?'

;/

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915.

TYE HEALTH OF THE CHILD.

How It Is Looked After In The Schools.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0066.txt | included=true | included =====
PARENTAL DISTRUST DISSIPATED.

Warrington parents, as a whole, evidently look after their children pretty careful.ly, and Warrington children in a mass, apparently, do the maternal and paternal care credit, for the annual report of the School Medical Officer for the County Borough (Dr. G. W. N. Joseph) shows that only 2.2 per cent. of the young folk of the town of school age were discovered to be below the normal in the matter of nutrition. In arrivihg at the conclusion as to the nutri- tion of a child not only are its height and weight taken into consideration, but also all the many other indications of the physical condi- tions of the child. The figures quoted, adds the doctor, certainly indicate that the nutrition of the children of the borough is fairly satisfac- tory.

The ventilation of the majority of the schools has in recent years been improved by the addi- tion of windows that can be opened, and during the past year special attention has been paid to the schools in the borough to see what use was made of the means of ventilation provided. There has been an undoubted improvement on the whole in this matter upon the previous year, but even now, in some instances, says Dr. Joseph, the teachers fail to avail themselves of the benefits to be received from a well-ventilated room. In many instances considerable im- provement has been made in the lighting of the schools and in ten schools the close stove is still made use of for heating. purposes, a fact which the doctor deplores, as the air of the rooms •becomes very dry and detrimental to both teachers and scholars. Cloak-room accom- modation is very frequently ..insuffieient, and the doctor emphasises the importance of. pro- perly ventilating such cloak-rooms as exist. More frequent thorough cleaning of schoolrooms is essential; Dr. Joseph pointing out that such thorough cleaning is not only an. important safe- guard against the spread of infectious disease amongst the children, but is also an object lesson in cleanliness which may have lasting effect.

Before a child who has suffered from scarlet fever or diphtheria is allowed to return to school he is examined by the Medical Officer of Health, and 354 scarlet fever convalescents and 79 diphtheria convalescents were so examined during the year; as against 145 scarlet fever convalescents and 57 diph- theria convalescents in the previous year. Considerable space is given in the report to detailing the routine work of the school doctor, and it is set out that 2,891 cases attended the inspection clinic, suffering from the following ailments :—lmpetigo 946, ringworm 183, eczema 3, scabies 25, conjunctivitis 984, blepharitis 129, other eye diseases 84, aural discharge 129, pedi- culosis 264, abscess 23, verminous clothing 21, and septic sores 100. In connection with these cases 1,526 visits were paid by the school nurses to the homes of the children. A comparative table for the past three years shows that in 1912 the number of children attending the inspection clinic was 1,741, whilst the number of visits paid to their homes by the school nurses was 1,024; in 1913, the figures were 2,430 and 1,199, and in 1914, 2,891 and 1,526 respectively. The head teachers, the report adds, empha- sise the marked improvement in the cleanliness and general condition of the children's under- clothing. Moreover, what is, perhaps, the best sign, is the increase in the interest which the children themselves are taking in their per- sonal appearance. The baths, which the children now enjoy, are resulting in a keener Condition of mind, and the head teachers report "that the children, being cleaner, appear to be brighter, more active, and consequently better able to benefit education- ally."

The physically defective children number 314, showing, as the report point's out, that "there are many children who would greatly benefit by. the establishment of a special school." The following is a summary of the investigations in regard to mentally defective children: Dull, 14; very backward, 18; mentally defective, 25; im- becile, 8. The report also deals with the ventilation and general sanitation of the local kinematograph halls, and records the fact that great improve- ment had been made in these matters, adding that the following regulations have been adopted : Children apparently. under 14 years of age shall not he allowed to enter into or be in the licensed premises after the hour of nine p.m., unless accompanied by one of their parents or an adult person. In the event of an epidemic of infectious disease arising, and the Medical Officer of Health advising that it is in the interests of public health, the Watch Committee may, for such period as they think fit, ,direct that all children apparently under the-age of 14 shall be excluded, and after such direction has been notified to the licensees, no child apparently under 14 years shall be permitted to enter into or be in the licensed premises at any time during the period named. A FINE RECORD OF REMEDIAL WORK. Mr. Holman Kingdon On The School Medical

How The Clinic Helps the Attendance Officer. It was pleasing to note that the Medical Officer stated that in his opinion the increase in the staff of the school clinic was fully justi- fied, 13.7 attendances having been saved, as compared with previous years, in each case treated at the clinic. That part of the report referring to the spray baths which had been introduced into the Bolton Council and Evelyn- street Council Schools was very interesting. It was the first time that the committee had really had an opportunity of dealing with the matter in detail, and it would appear from the informa- tion gathered that everybody concerned seemed to be unanimously of opinion that the baths had had a marked effect on the health and general condition of the children affected, and that increased personal cleanliness had resulted from their use. As to the suggestion Which had been made that the introduction of .the spray baths was another step in the direc- tion of relieving the parents of their responsi- bility, he had always held strongly the view that people who talked like that had not studied the question. The whole of the policy of the committee had been to enforce the parental responsibility. Enforcing Parental Responsibility.

They often heard it said that if they did things for people, there was a probability that people would not do those things for them- selves, but he would point out that in every- thing that committee did—whether it was in the matter of the attendance of the children at school or at the clinic, or in the matter of seeing that they got treatment for such diseases as they-might have—the whole tendency of their policy was to bring .home to the parents what parental responsibility really did mean and, so far as they reasonably could, to enforce it by such means as were within their power. After referring to the fact that there was a large increase in the number of cases of conjuncti- vitis during the year and remarking that the chief causes of that complaint were dirt, care- lessness and overcrowding, Mr. Kingdoin went on to talk of defective vision, and said that the test to which the eyes of the children were submitted was not a drastic one, and that the committee rather erred on the side of leniency, if anything, with the result that the figures given as to defective vision were certainly no better, and might even be taken as not so good as they seemed: A great deal of difficulty was experienced in getting the children to wear the spectacles prescribed for them, and to see that they did so what might be termed a spectacle- register had been suggested for each school. I nterference.With The Liberty Of The Subject? Another difficulty experienced had to do with getting the parents to understand why their children should have spectacles; they did not have spectacles when they went to school, and they could not see why their children should. He did not think that in the matter of getting the parents to realise that what was being done was for the good of the child Warring- ton was worse than any other town, and the higher the standard of education they could maintain the greater in the future would be the appreciation of all those remedial and other measures which were looked upon with a. certain amount of annoyance and disgust, as if the committee were taking away what some people were pleased to call the liberty of the subject—a phrase he disliked altogether, as it generally meant licence. Dealing with the number of cases treated in the school clinic, Mr. Kingdon pointed out that in the case of nearly every disease except conjunctivitis—of which there was an epidemie—there was a con- siderable reduction in the time taken to effect a cure, the result being that the number of attendances lost by the children affected by the ailments which came within the scope of the work of the clinic had been greatly decreased, the figures for last year being 27.2 attendances lost as compared with 33.3 attendances in the year before. and "38.8 in 1912. The dental clinic Mr. Kingdon referred to as a pet of his, and said that whilst it was still a very small baby it was growing in strength daily. They had lost the services of Mr. Ifedley Taylor, under circumstances well known to the members of the committee, and they were asked to vote him an honorarium of 20 guineas in special recognition not of his services, but for additional work done by him during the time lie held office. It was common knowledge that although not engaged to give a certain number of hours per week he nearly always put in extra hours, and the interest and enthusiasm which he put into the work constituted a model which it was hoped his successors might be able to copy.

A Tribute to the Ex-School Dentist.

The honorarium was intended not as a measure of the committee's appreciation, but as a practical souvenir of that appreciation. The tone of Mr. Taylor's report showed that not only did he realise that the step which the com- mittee had decided upon in the appointment of a whole-time dental officer was necessary, but that they parted good friends, and that they would always have Mr. Taylor's hearty co-opera- tion in any work in a dental direction which they might feel called upon to do.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
Officer's Report.

The School Medical Officer's report was laid before the members of the Education Com- mittee at their monthly meeting on Monday evening, and in drawing their attention to it Mr. HOLMAN KIM:MON, the chairman of the Medical Inspection Sub-Committee during the past year, said he knew these 'Were busy times, but at the same time he could honestly say that any member would be amply repaid for the time he spent in perusing the .report, 'which constituted a fine record of remedial work done amongst the children. He did not think anybody could read the report without feeling that the committee had entered upon a course of action in which all with the interests of the children at heart would wish them God-speed.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
Hastening Slowly—But Surely.

When they started clinic work in Warring- ton they made it, a point, rather, to hasten slowly and to take no step unless they were sure of their ground. He thought the wisdom of that policy was borne out by the results. Mr. Kingdon went on to point out salient items in the report. As to the sanitary condi- tion of the schools, there were, he said, various difficulties that had to be encountered, as, for instance, old structures and badly-designed buildings. In this connection one could not help sympathising with the managers in not wishing to spend more than they could reason- ably afford and he must say that, on the whole, the managers had met them very fairly and in several instances improvements recom- mended by the committee were slowly but surely, being carried out. Mention was made in the report to the matter of heating by stoves in many of the schools, the School Medical Officer taking the view that no school could be adequately heated by a closed stove. He (the speaker) did not doubt the doctor's authority, but he would like to point out that in many countries ,open fire-places were alto- gether unknown. He also thought that in some instances it might be possible, without making drastic alterations, to bring the stoves ta such a state of efficiency that the air would not be too dry, nor would poisonous fumes escape.

Children and Cinema Shows.

Mr. Kingdon next referred to the measures taken to prevent the spread of infectious disease among school children, and iaid that the report showed the advantage of the early ex- clusion of unprotected • children—those who had not had a previous attack, and were ex- cluded from the schools. With regard to thp question of cinematograph shows and the chil- dren, he thought the committee were taking steps in the right direction. Still, he did think that the arrangements made by the Watch Committee might have been a little more drastic, but he was advised that there was a question as to whether that committee had a legal right to take steps that he would con- sider adequate. Ile was of opinion that, so fir as they reasonably could, they ought to take steps to prevent children Suffering from any disease going to .the picture exhibitions. Alsf, he took' the view, though. it might not meet with the approval, of everyone present, that children under.. a certain, age would be better away from the picture shows altogether. The: statistics as to nutritioii -were,—as the doctor said, fairly satisfactory.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
A Plea for a Free Hand.

"I am now going to make an appeal to you and it may be for the last time," said Mr. King- don,„ in conclusion. "I do feel very strongly about this matter, in every friendly way, and my appeal to you is, if you are satisfied with the work of the Medical Inspection Sub-com- mittee, if you think they have controlled the work as they should, and if you think they take an intelligent interest in the work, that you should give us a free hand . . . and not

fetter us by asking us to 'accept an appointment made by another committee," adding that lie did not care to remain in the position he occu- pied on the Medical Inspection Sub-committee if they were going to press the matter of a joint committee with the Health Committee as re- gards the appointment of officers whose work had solely to do with the schools: Mr. Kingdon also thanked his fellow committeemen for the support they had given him, and Dr. G. W. ist. Joseph and the other officials, remarking that one could not have nicer officials to work with. He moved the adoption of the report.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
CLINIC CURES QUICKER THAN HOME CURES.

Two Ways in Which the Child Benefits. Mr. C. E. PARKER spconded, and said that he would like to associate himself with what Mr. Kingdon looked upon as his swan-song. He would be very sorry if, through any circum- stances, the energy, wisdom and knowledge which Mr. Kingdon threw into A° work of medical inspection should be lost to the town, and he (the speaker) backed him up in the matter to which he had alluded, because he be- lieved him worthy of backing up. There were one or two points in the report which it might be as well to emphasise, and one of them dealt with the difference in the time that was taken up in curing the children when they were treated at home and when they attended at the clinic regularly and irregularly. The statement to which he referred showed that the treatment given to the children at the clinic got them better more quickly, especially in cases where their attendance was regular. Undoubtedly, that question of getting the children better as quickly as possible had its important financial aspect, for it appreciably affected attendance at school, and by so doing was not only better for the child physically, buN also educationally. He would like to endorse what Mr. Kingdon had said about the services cf Mr. liedley Taylor. It was due very largely to his kindly treatment and intelligent manner with the par- ents that the distrust and suspicion which used to exist "five years ago was totally dissipated; indeed,_the manner in which he had gone about his work had made the clinic really popular.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
ALL THE WORK AT A COST OF ABOUT

£250.

Mr. KINGDON remarked that the school medi- cal work cost about £750 a year, and the Government grant earned in respect of it last year came to £363, or roughly half of the tozital cost. Then, owing to the children getting proper treatment and being able to go back to school earlier, they were-earning more in the shape of grants than would be the case, but

for the clinic, so that, taking everything to- gether, they got the somewhat astounding foot that the whole of the fine medical work done in the schools was not costing the town much more than £260 a year. He thought that was really remarkable, and there were few centres of. work in the town which could show such good results as could the clinic for so small an expenditure.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
ALDERMAN BENNETT'S HOPE.

AldermAn BENNETT congratulated Mr. King- don and his committee on the re- sults of their good work, and hoped

that Mr. Kingdoci would not run away from the good work which he had been doing, because he might not be able to get quite all his own way. (Laughter.) Perhaps, he (the speaker) might be a little stupid, but when he found that things had not been going quite as he would have liked, he always pegged away at it with greater desperation and felt his efforts to be more important than ever. He hoped that Messrs. Kingdon and Parker would stick to their guns and hope for better times, and keep plodding on. Mr. KINGDON I have been doing that for five years.

Alderman BENNETT : That's not much in the life of a man. I hope you will do it for a great many more years, until your hair Is grey. Mr. KINGDON : But I haven't got any. (Laugh- ter.)

Alderman BENNETT : I'll give you the benefit of the doubt. . . . I think this is a splen- did report and justifies both the work and the expenditure. I would like to congratulate the doctor not only on his report, but also, if not too late, on his marriage. The CHAIRMAN '(Councillor Roberts) : That is not on the minutes.

Alderman BENNETT went on to add that he felt very pleased about the result of the free baths, adding that they were his "own particular child," and hoped that the committee would not hesitate to introduce them into new schools. In fact, he would like to go a step further and see swimming baths.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
ANIMALS BETTER FED THAN SOME CHILDREN.

Councillor PEMBERTON thought the report a very fine one, and added, with reference to what Mr. Kingclon had ,said about resigning his chairmanship, that-surely if a man was doing his best in the matter of public life he ought to bow, to the majority. One must go on and do the best he could under the best conditions they could get, whether they thought those conditions were right or not. Anyone who studied the education question knew that many children from infancy were handiCapped, owing to the poor food they got. Sometimes there was more attention paid to the feeding of animals than there was to children; it was very sad, but it was so! Largely that condition of things came about through that want .of thought about which Mr. Kingdon had so often told them, the parents being so ignorant in a great many cases. But in thousands of cases throughout the country it was a question of means, and in that fact was found the greatest difficulty—the inability of the parent to be able to earn wages sufficient to buy what was necessary for fee4ing his children.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PHYSICALLY DEFECTIVE.

Mr. PLINSTON commented on the fact that one of the 'host marked improvements during the last twelve months was noticeable in connection with the cleanliness of the children, and went on to say that he thought nothing was more important than to endeavour to find accommodation at a special school or schools for the physically defective children.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
MOST SATISFACTORY WORK THE COMMITTEE HAS DONE.

Mr. J. Ursom thought :that the work re- ferred to in the report was, perhaps, the most satisfactory' that had been accomplished by the education authority. It resulted in the raising of the physical condition of the children and, nowadays, there was little or no resentment on the part of parents against what used to be considered the interference of the school doctor or the school medical officer, a fact which was largely due to the tact and wisdom of the officials responsible for the work. Indeed, there was now so fine an appreciation of the work of the officials that it was somewhat difficult to restrain parents from sending their children to the clinic. It seemed as if the parents had more confidence in the officials of that com- mittee than they had in the ordinary profes- sional man.

A VOICE: It's cheap Mr. UPSON : It may be, but where, at first there was strong resentment, there is now appreciation. Mr. Upson added that the work of the Medical Inspection Committee was only just beginning, and went on to indicate direc- tions in which progress would have to be made. Speaking as one who saw at first hand the work of the Medical Inspection Committee and its results, he said that it was perhaps the most satisfactory work that the Education Com- mittee had done, and was of very substantial service to the rising generation. He thought it would be calamitous if through any obstinacy of spirit / the work of the man who was largely responsible for the accomplish- ments of the committee should be lost to the children; if the people of Warrington should lose, through the operation of forces which ought not to be brought into play, the services of a man who had served them with an immense amount of energy, erithusiasm, skill and wis- dom.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ECONOMICALLY SOUND POLICY.

The CHAIRMAN said he was delighted to`associ- ate himself entirely with every <cord of l‘ppre- elation that had been uttered concerning the report, and of the committee, uho had done splendid work. He thought he might claim that a policy which made for the physical, mental and moral improvement of the children was economically sound, and, in the long run, would be financially sound, The report was then adopted.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
FEVER IN THE RUNCORN

RURAL AREA.

It was stated at Tuesday's meeting of the Runcorn Board of .Guardians that the number of eases of infectious disease in the locality was rather alarming. Mr. Woodward raised the subject by remark- ing that he knew of a case of infectious disease concerning which a complaint had been m'ade with regard to the disinfection of the van. It seemed to him that the efficiency of the hospital as a means of restricting the run of infectious disease was becoming impaired. It was surely most important that they should see to it that every part of the infectious disease work should be done thoroughly. There were at the present time far more cases of infectious disease than there ought to be. If Dr. Adams had been present that day, he (Mr. Woodward) would have asked him a few questions; for one thing, he would have asked about a case at a house in his own parish, where a patient was out and about six or eight weeks after the case was notified. Two children from the same house had been attending school all the time. He was told that there was no possibility of satisfactory isolation in the house. The hospital might as well be shut up. They spent a lot of money in building the place and a lot more in maintaining it; and it was all more or less wasted unless they saw, to it that the medical officer and all the men connected with the work did their tasks thoroughly. He hoped the Board would tackle the matter in a busi- nesslike manner.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
ANOTHER COMPLAINT.

Mr. Whittle said that he also knew of a case that possessed most undesirable features, and he supported the remarks of Mr. Woodward. The Chairman (Mr. C. Linaker, jun.) said he thought it was a pity there was no one present representing the doctor, so that questions could be asked. - .

Mr. Woodward said be did not know what arrangement Dr. Adams came to with his locum tenens.

The Chairman added that he felt that when Dr. Adams was away he might make arrange- ments for his locum tenens to attend at least once a month at a Board meeting. If such a system were atlopted, every member would be able to nut any question that Was in his mind.

The Clerk stated that he had been informed by Dr. Adams that he would be back and ready for duty by the end of the week.

ATTACKED A SOLDIER.

Ear lestown Man's Strange Conduct

On Railway Platform.

James Cooper, of 33, Nelson-street, Barks- town; was charged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday with being drunk and dis- orderly and assaulting Private Charles Yorn, of the South Lancashire Regiment, on Bank Quay Station, on Saturday. Evidence was given by Private Yorn, who said that prisoner, who was drunk, came up to him and knocked him down, kicking him before he could rise.

This was supported by the testimony of an officer of the company, Inspector Morley, who sent for a constable and did his best to main- tain peace in the interim before the latter's arrival.

Cooper said he was sorry ad did not know how he came to do it.

A fine of 20s. was imposed

REN 'LING TIIE GERMANS.

Two Men Fined For Attempting To

Create Disturbances.

"We must put this sort of thing down," said the Chairman of the Borough Bench (Mr. S. Wright) on Monday Morning, when James O'Grady, a labourer, of no settled abode, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in the Market-place on Saturday evening.

The ev-idence of Detecti-ve-Sergeatnt Armstrong was to the effect that prismker was trying to incite a crowd to violence by reviling the Ger- mans. When arrested, he was very trouble- some, and assistance was .necessary to get him to the Police Station.

The Chief Constable said the man's conduct in trying to rouse the crowd to attack certain premises in the Market-place might have re- sulted in hundreds of pounds' worth of damage. It was enough to cause,a riot. If he exerted his energies in the trenches instead of acting as he did on Saturday night, ,it would be a great deal more to his. credit.

Prisoner was sentfmeed to one month's bard labour.

Why He "Came from Liverpool."

William Edward Cox, of 166, Liverpool-road, Warrington, a labourer, was charged with a similar offence in Horsemarket-street on the same evening.

He was calling on a crowd to go and wreck a German shop, when he was arrested. He told the' crowd he had come from Liverpool for that purpose.

- The Chief Constable mentioned that there had been rumours about the town that there might be a demonstration against local Ger- mans.

A like sentence to that imposed in the former case was inflicted.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
FALSE INFORMATION.

Serious View Taken Of Ching Wrong

Names.

Several convictions have taken place at the Borough Police Court, during the past week, -under the new 'Order in Council of the Aliens Act, which says that all persons, of whatever nationality, shall sign a form that will be presented to them at any place where they may go to lodge, either for a night or a longer period. There seems to be a practice, more or less common, amongst the people who fre- quent common lodging-houses, of giving any name but their own. This, in normal times, may be harmless enough in nine cases out of ten, but, under the new Order, it is a serious matter to give false information. All a lodger has to do, providing he is a British subject, is to give name and nationality on the form provided. It is a simple enough matter and the lodger who prefers the outside of a prison to the inside will not "monkey with the truth." .

Found Out By Getting Drunk.

Sentence of a month's imprisonment was imposed in the case of John Graham, who was charged at the Borough Court on Monday with giving false information at a lodging-house situate at 19, Barbauld-street, Warrington. He was further charged with being drunk and disorderly in Ship-yard, although it was pointed out that he was not drunk at the time he gave the false information, which consisted in giving the name of Daniel Garnee instead of his own. He was locked up for being drunk and disorderly, and it was subse- quently discovered that he had been lodging at the house in Barbauld-street under a false name.

A fine of 9.. was inflicted for the minor offence.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
THREE IIEN IN A BAR.

How Two Manchester Men Parted

Monks And His Mope.).

Two charges were preferred against John McGuire and John Fynan, both of Manchester. at the Borough Police Court on Saturday, before Mr. R. T. Fairclough, Alderman Tinnion, Councillor Locker, and Messrs. John Upson, T. Pemberton and A. J: Jolley. The first was one of obtaining 10s. from b. Warrington labourer named Thomas Monks, by means of a trick, at the Central Hotel, Warrington, on May 7th; and the second was that they gave false information by signing incorrect names whilst lodging at a boarding-house, to wit, 57, Horsemarket-street, Warrington. McGuire's address is 14, Lower Byrom-street, Manchester, and Fynan's, 20, Hewitt-street, Manchester. Mr. Jenkins appeared for Fynan.

"Come and Have a Drink !"

The Chief Constable at the outset sketched the history of the case. On Friday evening, the 7th May, Thomas Monks met the two prisoners in Winwick-street and was asked by one of them if he could tell them the address of a man who lived in Church-street. Monks said he could not, and was then asked to go and have a drink. He conformed, and had a glass of beer with' the prisoners, who drank mineral waters. They asked him if he was willing to part with 4alf a sovereign, telling him that by doing so he oould share in £6. Ile declined at first, but they pressed him and eventually succeeded in persuading him to part With the money. In exchange they gave him a bookmaker's ticket, although Monks did not know what it was at the time. All three men left the hotel and walked along the street. The two prisoners then went away together. They stopped out- side the Fire Station and began to argue: The police had got wind of the matter, and had followed the men, one of whom was arrested outside the Fire Station, Pynan escaping and going to Manchester, where he was subsequently apprehended and found to be in possession of the half-sovereign.

A Sleeping Partner?

Mr. Jenkins said he had been instructed by Fynan to give a different version of the story. Fynan was a bookmaker's clerk, and had been acting, in that capacity at Ripon, afterwards going on to Chester, where he ,NN as engaged bv his principal. He was not going tc -say that 1, ,client had done nothing wrong, but he sug- gested that his .part in the affair was a passive. rather than an active one. He had been foolish in being in the company of McGuire, while the latter was persuading Monks to part. with his money. ,Fynan had a good home in Manchester, and there was no object for his res•orting.to the kind of in'actiee mentioned in the charge. The Chief Constable said Oat so far from

===== 0002601_19150522_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
GED 85. WELL 7 YEARS.

WHEN the organs begin to weaken, whether early or late in life, the hardworking kidneys usually tire out first. Failing eye- sight, stiff, achy joints, rheumatic pains, backache, and distressing urina- tion are often due only to weak kidneys.

Fynan being the "sleeping partner," the boot was on the other foot. lie was the one who did the talking and he took the money and absconded to Manchester with it.

McGuire pleaded guilty and had nothing to say.

Giving False Information.

The Chief Constable said that the second charge was preferred under the Aliens Act, according to the specifications of the new Order in Council of 13th April, 1915, which made it compulsory for even a British subject to sign up a certain form when staying at an inn, hotel, or lodging-house. Name and nationality had to be given by a Britisher. The prisdners went to the British Workman's Cocoa Rooms and spent the night there. Next morning they were called on to sign forms, and did so, McGuire giving the name of Harry Watson, and Fynan signing him- self as Frank Jones. The authorities (added the Chief Constable) were taking a serious view of this kind of offence.

On July 22nd, 1907, Mr. W. Fedarb, of 16, Park-street, Folkestone, said: —" Doan's Backache Kidney Pills have been a great comfort to me in my old age. My trouble was lumbago and kidney complajnt. I used to get frightful pains in my sides, loins and back, and could only get about with the aid of sticks: I fell down helpless once. "But the change brought about by Doan's Pills is simply wonderful, for I can walk out in ease and comfort now, and have no sign of kidney trouble. _ _ (Signed) "W. FEDARB."

_ On January 2nd, 1915—over seven years later—Mr. Fedarb said :—" I was eighty-five in October, but I'm sure I should not be as well as I am to-day if it hadn't been for Doan's Pills. They are my greatest bless- ing." All dealers or 819 a box, 6 boxes 1619 ; from Foster_ McClellan 'Co., 8, Wells-st., Oxford-et., London, W. Be sure you get the same Pills as Mr. Fedarb had.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
Any Old• Name !

When the prisoners were asked why they gave wrong names, McGuire replied "Well, sir, when you go into lodgings for the night you give any name." (Laughter.) Mr. Jenkins maintained that it was not at all an unheard-of practice for men of far bigher social standing than the prisoners to give a false name when they were staying at a hotel for a night. Eight previous appearances in the dock were proved against McGuire—for drunkenness, neg- lect of family, obtaining .0.5 by false pretences, &c: _

===== 0002601_19150522_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
Not an " Innocent Abroad."

Fynan was not (said Mr. Nicholls) the "inno- cent abroad" that Mr. Jenkins had tried to paint him. Mr. Jenkins objected to the suggestion that he had tried to make out Fynan to be an " inno- cent abroad."

The Chief Constable went on to proVe convic- tions against Fynan for burglary, thefts of shirts, trousers and newspapers, and fraud on the railway. The Bench imposed a sentence of three months' imprisoinment on both prisoners for each offence. Prisoners were also ordered to pay the stolen 10s. back to Monks and to meet the witnesses' expenses.

Stripped To The Waist And Covered

AA•it, Blood.
