ALL THROUGH A DRINK!

At Thursday's Police Court, Gunner Thomas Nixon and Driver Chas. Day, of the 69th Brigade (Ammunition Column) of the Royal Field Artillery, stationed in Surrey, were charged with being absentees.—The evidence was to the effect that the men were found by P.C. Horne trying to scale a wall at the rear of Bank Quay Station, and that they admitted to him that they were members of a draft of Artillery who were going to Ireland, and 'added that, at Waterloo Station, they ,missed their train, having asked the officer Whether they could go out and get a drink, and being per- mitted to do so, with the result that when they returned to the platform the train had gone. Further questioned, they admitted that they left the draft for the purpose of absenting themselves so that Nixon could come to War- rington to see his wife, and so that Tait could proceed to Liverpool to see his parents.— Prisoners were ordered to be handed over to an escort.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
DANGEROUS MOTORING.

'A Swerve Round The Cemetery

Entrance.

Before the Borough Magistrates on Thursday morning, Edwin Pennington, a youth, residing at 80, Mersey-street, .was summoned for driving a motor-cycle to the danger of the 'public in Manchester-road, on Sunday, May 2nd. _ _ _ _ _

A resident in Manchester-road said that at 12.10 he was opposite the end of Marsh-street and saw defendant ride past in the direction of Manchester, at a speed of not less than 20 miles per hour. At the time a man was breaking in a horse and turning out of Padgate-lane, drew up opposite the tramcar, apparently with the object of getting the animal used to the cars. Without checking his speed, defendant crossed to the off-side of the road, opposite the Cemetery gates. He nearly knocked over a perambulator containing a child, and completed the semi- circle in the Cemetery Gateway by crossing over the pavement.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
Knocked An Old Man Over.

He then pulled up and witness was informed that he had struck an old man and had knocked him on to his elbow on the pavement. Witness considered that defendant had taken a very dangerous step by driving in the manner he did. The congregation were just leaving the Parish Church at the time.

Defendant denied that he was driving at the speed alleged, or that he knocked the child over, though he nearly did so. He did not, he said, touch the old man, but just `` skimmed his elbow" by a matter of three inches. He rode on the pavement to avoid the man.

Mr. W. Roberts (one of the magistrates): Yon gave him a fright?

Defendant: I jumped off to see that the old man was not hurt, and then rode on, The Chief Constable said that it was but fair to the defendant to say that one of the witnesses who had been summoned stated that when defendant saw the horse he lost his head and swerved round.

The Bench imposed a fine of 205., with 10s. 6d. for witnesses' fees, and was warned by the Chairman.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915,

THE HEALTH OF THE CHILDREN.

On the authority of Mr. JOHN UPSON, the representative of the teaching profession on the Education Committee, and a man of long, wide and observant experience of children, we learn (from the debate which took place on Monday night at the monthly meeting of the committee) that a marvellous change has been effected in the physical well-being of the children of the borough since the Medical Inspection Sub-committee began its labours under powers set up by recent legislation. It was assumed that, given a fair chance, the public supervision of the health of children would do a great deal of good, but there was the great obstacle looming ahead represented by the feeling that the " liberty of the sub- ject w and "parental responsibility" were to be impinged upon. By the earnestness of the committee and the tact and wisdom of the officials who have had to administer the Act, all opposition to this friendly and bene- ficial work has been successfully combated; and Mr. lIPsoN went so far as to say that so great a confidence have parents in the Clinic that they now consult it in preference to consulting the ordinary medical men. Several members of the committee made the sugges- tion that it was because they got the attend- ance cheap, to which Mr. UPsox's reply was that at first, irrespective of cost, there was great resentment against inspection, a resent- ment which has now vanished. Mr. HOLMAN KINGDON gave one powerful reason for this when he said the committee, instead of under- mining responsibility, had been primarily in- terested in making parental responsibility real by showing parents how they coula pro- perly and usefully fulfil their duty by their children. That there has been a great im- provement in the physical health and personal cleanliness of the children of the borough is established beyond all doubt by the report of Dr. G. W. N. JOSEPH, the school medical officer.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GROUND COVERED.

Although Mr. UPSON said the work of this committee was only just beginning, there has already been made a considerable amount of progress in many and varied directions. The promptness with which infectious diseases are handled, the inspections for defective con- ditions of any kind, the closer attention to bodily cleanliness, the provision of baths in some of the schools—with promise of more baths to come—advice as to clothing, are only a few of the methods instituted for the better development of child life. The prac- tical effect of the work of the department may be seen from a useful table in the report which shows the number of children excluded from school for a period suffering from ring- worm, impetigo, conjunctivitis, ear discharge, vermin, debility and other ailments. Three years ago the total number so excluded was 1,490, who lost attendances averaging 38.8. Last year 2,185 children were so excluded, but they lost only 27.2 attendances. In other words, more children are being handled and the work is being so well done that cures are more quickly effected and so lost time is re- duced. Not only are the afflicted children being benefited, but others who are entitled to protection, must also benefit. Filthy con- ditions in some children are bad for the teachers and the other scholars. Perhaps most progress has been made in the dental section of this school doctoring; and Mr. HEDLEY TAYLOR is to be complimented, as he often was.on Monday night, upon having brought the department up to such a state of perfection that the demand for dental treat- ment has justified the appointment of in all- time dentist. Decayed teeth are at the root of many evils, mental and physical, and it is quite possible that tte education of parents upon the wisdom of keeping their children's teeth in order may in itself revolutionise the health and stamina of the people in the near future.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
NEARLY SELF-SUPPORTING.

Those who are inclined to carp at the expense connected with these new duties undertaken by the State will find their answer in smile figures produced by Mr. KLNGDON. The immediate suffering that is being saved and the good that is being done to the race are incalculable; but, as a ihere matter of £ s. d., the medical inspection of school children is nearly self-supporting. The ex- penditure for the year was £750. Against ;his the committee received grants amount- ng to £363, and ,the grants saved through hildren being cured more quickly than formerly, so swelling the average attendances, saved grants previously lost amounting to £l5O. So there was a debit balance of only £247 on the year.

WHY TANTALISE THE COMMITTEE? At the conclusion of Mr. KINGDON'S account of his stewardship as chairman of this very active and useful committee, he uttered what Mr. C. E. PARKER said might be looked upon as his "swan song." That is, he appealed to the Town Council and Education Committee to show their appreciation of the work of the Medical Inspection Sub-committee, if they were satisfied with results, by allowing him and his colleagues to proceed without being hampered by the overlordship of the Health Committeo, who, having claimed the right to appoint the officials whom the sub-committee need, have reduced their claim to that of the right to representatives on a joint committee

ti

===== 0002601_19150522_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1015.

to make such appointments. If they are not I to be free, Mr. KINGDON, as he threatened some time ago, will resign. Mr- PARKER appears to be of the same opinion, and the strong appeals ,made to them to go on with their good work in spite of obdurateness in other directions met with no response. It would be calamitous, said Mr. UPsoN, if the skill, wisdom and enthusiasm tnat have been shown in accomplishing the most satisfactory work that has been done by the Education Committee since its formation, are lost because of some perverseness elsewhere. Mr. KINGDON'S position is a strong one, and, though we hope he will not deprive the town of services it can ill afford, we fully sympa- thise with him in his effort to regularise matters so that he shall have power, as well as responsibility, in his own department.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLIER CLOSING OF SHOPS.

A correspondent, signing himself "Retailer," who represents a most important section of the shops in Warrington, draws attention to one way in which greater effi- ciency could undoubtedly be effected, while at the same time considerable economies could be secured, both in men and material. Why, at a time when We are relying upon silver even more than leaden bullets to save the nation, should our shopkeepers be forced to keep open their establishments in the waste- ful fashion that grew up in our days of peace and luxury? The earlier closing of shops could be practised even in peace time to our universal advantage; how much more would a reform benefit us now! The Post Office and other places under public control, or where opposition does not interfere with freedom of action, have curtailed the hours of business because labour of variou's kinds has been withdrawn for the purposes of the war. More men for the war are now demanded from the shops. Women, it is said, can take the place of men behind the counter—but can they? How many women would willingly undertake to wait upon customers, /many not of the most forbearing dispositions, from nine o'clock till nearly midnight on Saturday? And if they would consent, would it be fair to ask them? Is it fair to ask men to do it? No wonder, as our correspondent states, there is a great and growing difficulty in getting suitable assist- ants nowadays. The assistants ask for shorter hours, and here we have a representa- tive of the masters also asking for greater consideration for his class. It is, we think, useless to appeal to the class of people who purchase at the last minute to mend their ways. The only effective method would be early closing; and the early closing of shops is impossible while the Market is kept open. The way out, therefore, appears to be a joint conference between the tradesmen of the town and the Markets Committee to formulate a scheme under which the lives of the workers in our shops and at the stalls may be made tolerable, while at the same time substantial all-round economies are effected. The urgent need for some action along these lines is apparent on national,'no less than personal, grounds; and we hope the MAYOR will see his way to take steps that will lead to the formulation of a workable scheme.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PEACE, NO FOOTBALL.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Football Club, held on Thursday evening, re- flected the earnestness with which the nation regards the present situation. The audience was so thin that it could scarcely be seen in the big room, which, a year ago, was crowded to overflowing; and Councillor OSBORNE, the president, announced., amid approving applause, that the Warrington 'Committee had resolved that no matter what' steps the Northern Union take, they will not be a party to professional football being played during the continuance of the war. So that, if the war continues, Wilderspool will be re- served for play by those who are too young to enlist—the schoolboys and the Intermediate League players. Councillor OSBORNE says there is a private consensus of opinion in Northern Union circles that it would be only right, fair and honest towards comrades in the fighting line if professional football ,tvere suspended till war ceases. The general ques- tion will be decided early next month, but the mind of Warrington is already made up. If there is no football next winter .at Wilderspool, certain charges will have to be met, but they can easily be covered, as the probable deficit would amount to only £5O.

MEMORIAL HYMN

For Those Who Have Fallen In War.

Lord of the Nations, round whose throne The angels spread their cov'ring wings, Ottr safety lies in Thee alone, The Lord of lords and King of kings. Silent, amid a nation's tears, The path of sorrow, Lord, we tread; Bowed by the burden of our fears, We yield to Thee our hallowed dead.

Faithful to death, at Honour's call, They died a lasting peace to gain; 0 'Thou that heeds the sparrows' fall, Receive and guard our gallant slain 1

They met the foeman, undismayed. The light of battle in their eyes, Their steadfast souls on Thee they stayed, And gave their lives a sacrifice.

Though buried in an alien land, Where foe meets foe in deadly strife, Their spirits stand at Thy right hand, Crowned with the fadeless crown of life

Lord of all life, to Thee we bow, Thy hand hath stilled the fleeting breath; But peace divine enfolds them now, Beyond the mystery of death.

Though here we mourn our heroes dead, In safety gathered near Thy side, Grant us Thy strength still to maintain The sacred cause for which they died.

Lord of the nation, in whose hand Our sure defence and safety lie. Help us for King and Fatherland To fight, to conquer, or to die.

ALFRED CORNET'.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
The Mendicant's Mistake.

"I thought he looked like a man to help a fellow with three ha-pence," said a man who was charged at the Warrington Police Court on Monday, with begging. The confession was one of failure, for the man with the benevolent demeanour was a plain clothes cons-table, who when asked by the mendicant for three ha'pence, revealed his identity, took the plausible one in tow and piloted him to the Police Station, where he was •put into the dock the next morning.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
UNITED IN INFAMY.

Germany's National Responsibility For Lapse To Barbarism.

WOULD SELL HER SOUL FOR VICTORY.

BY C. E. PARKER.

At the beginning of the war it was believed that the German nation had been led unwil- lingly into war by the Kaiser and the military caste by which he is surrounded, and that we were fighting not Germany but Prussian mili- tarism. Subsequent events have led to a re- vision of this idea, and we are now conscious that we are in conflict with Germany nation- ally united in thought, purpose and effort. I do not mean that there may not he a minority, possibU an appreciable one, who have not been carried away by what we consider a mad national ambition. But in view of the facts which have come to light, we are forced to realise that the war has the German nation at its back. The valour, universally shown, the sacrifices that have, and are being made, afford ample evidence of the fact that we are at war with the German people, and not alone with a bureaucracy and military caste. Under no other supposition can we explain the in- dividual sacrifices that are made by men, women and children all over Germany. Think what Germany has done! For nine montlis she has held at bay three of the strongest European nations. Granted she was pre- pared, while the others were not, ready to the last button. But even this initial advantage would not have been sufficiently effective un- less the whole German nation was of one mind and strong in its " will to conquer."

National Depravity. And in regard to the atrocities, both on land and sea, deeds which have made the name of German synonymous with vileness, this seems not a sectional depravity but a national one. It were absurd to conceive that both officers and men from all parts of the 'German Empire have perpetrated hideous and revolting crimes merely as automatons under oiders from a higher source. 'No doubt i4i general terms the individual commanders have had instructions to conduct this war with great severity, but it is clear from the different ways in which the several units in command have acted both upon land and sea, that there has been ample scope for individual action. The orders were general, not particular, a great deal was left to personal initiative, and the acts of cruelty, outrage and excess are largely personal acts. The Prussian Government certainly gave orders for the scuttling of merchant ships and fishing boats but it was left to the officer in command whether he gave warning and time for the crews 'to escape before torpedoing their vessels. There is, a curious note in one of the reports to the German Admiralty when this time was given.

"We are afraid," says the report, "that the German people will view with disfavour such acts of cleme-, -v." Here plainly is the reference made not to naval instructions, but to the view the writer thinks the German people are likely to take of such acts of clemency. No doubt the rulers are the authors of this policy, but it is clear the in- dividual methods of carrying it out are left to the officers and the men, and the more cruel they are the more they appear to be supported and encouraged by the majority of the Ger- man people. A similar diversity of individual conduct may be noted in connection with the treatment of British prisoners in Germany. The idea then that we are fighting only a ruling caste in Germany should be abandoned, and the sooner the better. We are up against the German nation, the -German people, against German cruelty, German lust and Ger- man Kultur. It is futile to sneer at the last term; it represents a reality and means national efficiency, and has little or no relation to our own term culture. For good or evil, the German people are nationally efficient, and especially for war purposes. Economic Exhaustion.

The expectation entertained that there would be a reaction in Germany from within against the ruling caste on account of the declaration of war, and especially the manner in which it is being conducted, must, at any rate for the present, be put aside. What the future has in store in regard to disruption in thought and action from Germ(my within the gods alone know, but for the time being it would be futile to place any reliance on the war coining to an end as a result of the dis- integration of German unity. The war can only come to an end by the defeat of German power, accelerated as this undoubtedly will be by the economic position which is surely, if slowly, crippling Germany. No country which has for years relied in her economic develop- ment upon international trading can suddenly become self-sustained, self-supporting, with- out involving a tremendous drain of wealth. Great as are the internal resources of Ger- many, and husbanded as they are by un- paralleled sacrifices, no country can continue indefinitely to sustain its inhabitants when so large a proportion of its energy is absorbed in unproductive employment necessary to war. The period of economic exhaustion may be further off than many of us expected, but it is silently and surely getting nearer, and no amount of boasting, no amount of sacrifices can indefinitely keep it back. it may be con- tended that the same forces tend towards the exhaustion of the Allies. True, but with this difference: that their resources, their reserves are incomparably greater, and the drain upon these is relatively lehs. Time is on the side of the Allies, and this holds good in the mili- tary sphere as surely as in the economic. Germany may during the next month or two score some points in this grim tragedy, but she has reached her limit, already she has about called up her full fighting strength. Further to deplete the numbers of workers at home by calling up men who from age or physique are of very second-rate fighting quality, would not be of much assistance. To transfer these ineffectives from the industrial spheres where they are at present so much needed, into fighting factors, would be a doubtful advantage, for it would increase the economic strain. The time is now or never for Germany; unless she can within the next month or so accomplish upon the battle area something really effective, something so sub- stantial that it would place her in a position to enforce a Peace on the basis of a draw in her favour, then it seems to me the fate of Germany becomes sealed.

Our Humana Handicap. But the end is not yet. Germany is still unbeaten, still powerful, still united. She started the war with a prepared military force probably stronger than the combined military forces of the Allies. The latter had, in a large measure, to be created. In naval strength, except for guarding our shores and transporting troops, England's strongest arm is out of action. Then we are seriously handi- capped by our higher form of civilisation, our respect for the rules of international law and humane usage. We are not prepared to sacrifice our honour for a temporary advan- tage, while Germany is prepared to sacrifice anything and everything—to sell her soul for victory. Whether this difference will in the long run prove a real handicap is more than doubtful, but for the time being our deter- mination to fight fairly is a handicap. Before wil can achieve our purpose we may have to wade through rivers of blood, we may have to suffer losses far greater than we have so far endured. There are signs that the nation is at length realising the seriousness of the task before it, and recent events have brought this home to many who had not so far appreciated the gravity and peril of the hour. Hence the obligation on everyone who loves his country, his kind, who values the freedom and progress of the world, to fight, to work and to save. The task before us is a heavy one, but if eaeit one does his or her part there is no doubt about the final issue.

"THE TIME HAS COME/

Recruiting Meetings In Warrington How The Men Are Joining.

COLONEL FAIRCLOUGH'S LETTER.

Recruiting proceeds apace in Warrington. The local Territorial recruiting officials have held two meetings during the past week, with encouraging results. The following letter from Colonel Fairclough, in command of the 4th South Lancashire Regiment in France, was read by Lieut. Williamson, the head of the local Ter- ritorial recruiting department, to whom it was sent, at a meeting at the Park Gates on Tuesday evening : .

"will you please push on with the' recruit- ing of men for the services, and especially for the South Lancashire Regiment, Regulars and Territorials. This job is not going to be finished without men—and a good many men, too ! The more men there are at work, the sooner the end will be, and if people at home would only realise that—if everybody would do their best for the cause—the war would soon come to an end. But those out here and in other parts where opera- tions are being conducted, must he efficiently backed up by those at home, either by personal service, maximum output or contribution. "The Battalion is doing well. I am glad to say that our casualties are no greater than those in other battalions, but still we get them, in spite of precautions. "The last two days have been wet and cold, and things are consequently not very comfort- able.

"Quint and Shorter have joined, and it makes things much brighter, as we were getting very short. I don't care how soon the others come back, as we miss them terribly."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
IF THE WAR ENDED NEXT WEEK.

Lieutenant Williamson said at the outset that more men were wanted to take the places of those who had already received the necessary training and were ready for the Front. The time had come now when every able-bodied man who was not engaged on Government con- tract work was needed in the Army, and it was the duty of every man up to the age of forty, and forty-five in the case of those who had previously seen service, to offer himself as a recruit. Having read the letter printed at the head of this column, Lieutenant Williamson added that he had also had a letter from Major Crosfield, who said: "We must have all the men we can raise. If the wars were to be over next week, we want the men just the same, so that we shall be in a position to talk—so that we can make our own terms of settlement." Those who wanted to join that night, Lieutenant Williamson proceeded, could go to the Drill Hall, be examined and sworn in and have their uniforms at once, if they weren't too big; he had no uniforms left in stock for big men. A meeting that was held the other night brought in fifty recruits, and that night's meeting would, he hoped, have an equally satisfactory result. Councillor Broadhurst alluded to the prophecies of Major Crosfield, Captain Town- roe, himself and others amongst his fellow. working men, that Germany would ride rough- shod over this country if the Young men did not rise to the occasion. "I have served 19 years (the sneaker said)—not only with the Warring- ton Battalion—and I say this, that I stand here a better man for the service I have given." There was not a man in the town who would not benefit by joining the Coloars. He was proud to say that it was entirely owing to the education he had received from the officers and N.C.O.'s of the Volunteers that he stood to-day as a representative of St. Austin's Ward. • Will The Working Man Be Better Off?

He had just been told by a man he had met that no working man would be any better off if the war were brought to a successful con- clusion. That was false. In five years' time, unless the 'Germans were defeated, they would be in England, and the British people would have tc pay an indemnity for the whole of the lives Germany had lost, and for the whole of the munitions she had used. Necessities would be taxed to the uttermost farthing. If any man, five years ago, had said that to-day our Terri- torials would be fighting in tne trenches against the most dastardly military foe the world had ever seen, fighting for dear life against the greatest murderers who ever donned uniform, he would have been met with the retort "Pre- posterous," as they did when it was said that Germany had warlike intentions towards Eng- land. The local Volunteers were met witn jeers and sneers, and called "England's last hope," "Saturday afternoon soldiers" and so on, but they might thank God that the Terri- torials had shown themselves to be made of such splendid stuff. In conclusion, Councillor Broadhurst said that there was one thing in our favour, at least, 'and that was that truth was our stand- ard. Our cause was holy, and we would fight for it even unto death. Our cause was right, and right was might. Let them, therefore, all acquit themselves like men and verily Britain must win in the fight. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19150522_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
IF GERMANY WON.

Lieutenant Burgess, adjutant of the 3rd/4th Battalion South Lancashires, spoke a word of warning as to the conditions that would in all probability prevail in the event of Germany's winning. He knew the conditions in Ger- many, as he had lived there. The working class, shortly before the war, were in a very discontented state, due entirely to the mili- tarism, the officialdom and the restrictions on their liberty. If the German people them- selves were not contented under their military rule, what would the Mt of Britain be? ,He went on to quote the allowances made to men joining the 3rd/4th Battalion. ' While in War- rington: Single. men, billeted at home, received 3s. a day, while married men were allowed 4s. For every recruit obtained, an allowance of is. was made with one day's leave. The battalion was shortly going to camp, and, if desired, he would undertake as far as possible to see that friends joining together should live in the same tent, and that if drafts were sent to the 2nd,l4th or the lst/4th Battalions, these men should go together. (Applause.) - The band—of which the 3rd/4th Battalion may well be Koud—struck up a. spirited tune at the conclusion of the meeting, and led the way to the Drill Hall, where' a number of recruits "signed on."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
HURRY UP, YOUNG MAN

Lard Kitchener Wants Your Name On His List.

Another largely attended recruiting meeting was held outside the Market Hall on Thursday evening, the South Lancs. Band being in attend- ance and playing patriotic and popular airs before the meeting, whilst afterwards they headed a torchlight procession, which paraded the main streets.

Lieutenant Williamson addressed the meet- ing, urging the importance of still more men coming forward and pointing out that when the terms of peace were being discussed this country wanted a powerful body of men behind it, so that the terms would be their terms and not the Germans' terms. Since the present series of recruiting meetings had started they had had close upon a hundred recruits, and they ought to have at least another hundred before the week was out; indeed, it was the duty of every member of a family present to get one of their menfolk, if not more, to join. All those around knew how the Belgians had been treated, and if the Germans got authority over this country they would treat the people here even worse than they had treated the Belgians. The whole of Germany's hatred was directed against the English—not against the French, Russians or Belgians, and it would be a terrible thing for the British women and children if the Huns happened to win. (Hear, hear.)

LOCAL INTELLIGENCE.

Lymni Butchers' Decision.

Last night's meeting decided a week on Mon- day to start closing: Monday., 1 o'clock; Tt,les- day, 2 o'clock; Wednesday, 1 o'clock; and Thursday at 6—until further notice. The resolution was unanimous, and applies also to Heatley.

Market Post Office: Curtailment of Hours.

On and after Tuesday the hours of business at Market-place Branch Office will be cur- tailed during the period of the war. The office will be open from 9.30 a.m. to 5 p.m. The alteration in the hours has been rendered necessary owing to the depletion of staff through the war.

Warrington Market Prices. On Wednesday wheat was firmer, Bs. 10d. to 9s. per bushel of 701 b.; oats firm, at late rates. viz., 4s. 6d. to 4s. 10d. per bushel of 451 b.; wheat straw, 365. per ton; oat straw, 365. to 375. 6d. per ton on rail; clover hay, £4 ss. to .24 10s. per ton; meadow hay, £3 15s. to £4 per ton. Minimum Wage of Li for Teachers.

The Finance Sub-committee reported to the Education Committee that applications from uncertificated and supplementary teachers for increases of salary having been considered, they had resolved that no teacher who shall have completed ten years' service under the War- rington Education Authority shall, after the Ist May, 1915, be paid a salary less than £52 per annum.—The Education Committee ac- quiesced.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
Banks and Working Class Savings.

At all the offices of the Union Bank of Man- chester the staffs are now on duty, and will be for the duration of the war, every Friday evening from seven o'clock to eight o'clock, for savings bank business only. This innovation is for the convenience of workers and others engaged in the daytime in the production of Army supplies and for the relief of the bank work during the daytime, the staffs having been largely reduced by mem- bers having joined the Forces.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Postponed Through The War.

The Finance Sub-committee reported to the Warrington Education Committee at their monthly meeting on Monday evening that let- ters had been received from the Local Govern- ment Board with reference to the purchase of additional land in Oliver-street, stating that the Board were unable to comply with the application at the present time, but that they saw no reason why the loan should not be sanctioned when the situation permits. The committee therefore decided that a further re- quest be preferred to the Local Government Board in connection with this loan, a decision which the Education Committee endorsed.

St. Peter's Sunday School Anniversary

The Sunday school anniversary in connection with St. Peter's Church, was celebrated on Sunday. The special preachers were the Rev. L. J.T. Darwall, vicar of Walton, and the Rev. E. E. Chamberlain, of Padgate, both or wnom made urgent appeals for support for the Sun- day school and the church. Special hymns were sung at all the services by the church choir and Sunday school scholars, who had been very carefully trained by the choirmaster, Mr. E. A. Padmore. At the morning service the anthem "When I Consider Thy Heavens" was beautifully rendered by the choir, the Misses N. Grounds and M. Percival effectively rendering the duet portion which is a leading feature of the composition. The anthem "Sun of lify Soul" was sung at the evening service, when the soloist was Mr. H. Sudlow. In the afternoon a service of song entitled "Little Meg's Children" was given by the Young Women's Bible Class. The connective readings were given by Mrs. Percival. A Hint To Teachers.

At the monthly meeting of the Education Committee, on Monday evening, the Rev. Canon Stevenson moved the confirmation of the fol- lowing minute which appeared in the report of the School Management Committee:—" It was resolved that, if a teacher already serving in one of the schools under the committee ap- plies for, and is appointed to, a position in another school under the committee, the com- mencing salary for that appointment shall be the salary the teacher is already receiving, or £5 less than that salary, as the committee may determine."—The Canon explained that the only object the School Management Committee had in view was the checking of indiscrim- inate movement of teachers from one school to the other without due and reasonable cause. Constant resignations were very bad for schools and the committee would be very sorry to see a spirit of unrest amongst the teachers. On the other hand, the committee were very glad to further the interests of any teacher who desired to move for some good and reasonable cause.—The Rev. Father Wilson seconded, and the minutes were passed.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Leather Trade Vagaries.

The trade presents very few new features of interest just now (writes the Warrington cor- respondent of the "Leather,. Trades Review "). Those who have contracts placed are pressing for deliveries, but, apart 'from this, the interest in further business is rather slow, and the whole of it centres in heavy goods. There is no lack of inquiry in this direction, but, on the other hand; the response is small, for with practically everything in process already sold, tanner's offers are limited by the number of suitable hides he can find, which unfortunately are very few. With the probable closing of the Italian market the quantity is likely to diminish considerably. Prices are rising, for tanners have become alarmed by the unprece- dented rise in hide values, and are very chary about selling anything except such stock as may be on the floor, and as tie demand for leather at the moment is scaroely strong enough to carry any substantial advance, I rather look to see business of a hand-to-month character for a while, and in the meantime would not be surprised to see some reduction of Output.

FIFTY RECRUITS FROM ONE MEETING.

A recruiting meeting was held in the Victoria Park on Friday night of last week, when a stirring appeal was made by Alderman Tinnion. The band of the 3rd/4th South Lancs. was in attendance, and played inspiritingly. The meeting was also addressed by Lieutenant Williamson, who urged the importance of obtaining as many men as possible. The eloquence of the two speakers was not lost, some fifty recruits being obtained as a result of the meeting.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
NO OFFICIAL WALKING-

DAY.

Last night at the Town Hall the Mayor pre- sided over a representative meeting of all local churches and Sunday schools concerned in. the Walking Day processions. After long and private discussion it was resolved: "That the usual Walking Day processions be abandoned, and that it be left to the individual schools to make their own arrangements as to the giving of a treat to the scholars."

No public processions will therefore be held from the Town Hall, nor will the schools close for the Friday. For the holding of any treats no day is yet fixed; probably it will be a Satur- day. The tradesmen may now decide to keep open.

_ The Mayor will not now call a public wet" ing to discuss the matter.

Closing Day At The Park. Bank Park will be closed to the public on Monday next from 6 p.m.

Help For Prisoners!

An appeal is thade on behalf of the British prisoners of war interned in Germany. The following articles are very urgently required: Cast-off boots, underclothing, dry food-stuffs (loose) and bread, tobacco and cigarettes. Gifts will be gratefully received at the Parr Hall, where names of prisoners will be suP" plied to those desirous of undertaking the dispatch regularly of parcels to one or more prisoners.

The Great National Need.

PRIVATE TOM EYRES.

Shot Through'llead In The Early

Morning.

I beg to enclose personal effects of your son —who, I very much regret to say, was killed in action on the 11th instant. I have no doubt his company officer will have given you all details. No words of mine will heal the Wound; but you will know that he died fight- !n9 for a great cause, and that he nobly did his duty. We have laid him to rest with others who have fallen in this terrible struggle. Please accept my deepest sympathy in your loss.— ,Yours faithfully, G. H. Ingram, Quarter- Master-Sergeant. s This letter—which reached Warrington on rraomi'day morning—was the first news that Mrs. th t yres, of 14, Birchall-street, had had of vi't,:leath of her husband, who was fighting Iv: the Warrington Territorials. With it a:,re enclosed Dr. Law's certificate of the death, ler the deceased's pocket-book, pipe, comb, tele, money, &c.

Brother Attended His Burial.

„vlLhe same morning, Mr. and Mrs. Wm. Eyres, Vere-street—parents of the dead private— :raard from the son James (also serving with the 4. 14th South Lancs.) His letter, which was aated Tuesday May 11th, said ,„ • • . I am sorry to inform you that our l'oln has been shot right through the head; and he died almost ,immediately, without any pain whatever. They broke the news to me this afternoon, Whilst I was on guard; they told me that , he had been hit in the head. I shall have to take things hard, and still keep my heart up. , When they brought him out of the trench To the dressing station” I got permission to see him; and I have, got permission to go to his funeral. He will be buried in a little Village churchyard—where we go to spend our rest when out of the trench. You can bet that he will have a good funeral, and that I and a few pals will look after • his grave, and care for it as much as pos- sible. Hoping you will take things as light as Possible—as he died for a good cause whilst serving his country.—Your loving son, Jim.

Dear Mrs. Ayres,—lt is with deep sorrow that I write to tell you the sad news that your husband, Private T. Ayres was shot in the head with a rifle-bullet in the early hours of yesterday morning. you •see all the tales in the papers, which are quite correct, and we get the local sheets up here when we are out of the trenches.

"Shells come over our side pretty often, but our artillkry is far smarter, about a mile on our right. We have a fresh signal for axtil- lery, much quicker than telephone. I have, also, a German bullet and a piece of shrapnel which the shell contains. If I can get it through I will send a large one next time."

PRIVATE JOHN CHUAILEY.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0140.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN CHUMLEY.

First Of Cable Company's Men To

===== 0002601_19150522_art0141.txt | included=true | included =====
Lose His Life.

The first to be killed of the gallant coterie who at the call to arms left the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Company's Works to do their little " bit " at the Front is Private John Churnley, who was serving with the Warrington Territorials, and news of whose death was received on Friday morning. A single man of 24 years of age, he resided with his father at 273, Liverpool-road, and he had been in the employ of the Cable Company a matter of about five years, having, latterly, been an assistant in the power-house. He was a diligent and trustworthy worker, and the Works Managei. (Mr. E. S. Seymour) says that by his death the firm have lost one of the most reliable men that he has• ever had under him; "a man who never lost a minute in the mornings or any other time." It was Mr. Seymour who first discovered the deceased's excellent qualities as a worker, and who was instrumental in having him promoted to the position he held, at the time of volunteering his services, as an assistant in the power- house.-

===== 0002601_19150522_art0142.txt | included=true | included =====
Enthusiastic About His Soldiering.

Just before he went away for his training he paid Mr. Seymour a call, and then appeared to be quite as enthusiastic about his soldier- ing as he was about his work. "I appreciated him as a worker and as a man," Mr. Seymour told our representative, "and I deeply regret his loss. He was absolutely one of the best- respected lads in the shop." The news that Private Chumley had given his life for his country was first made known to his father through the following letter, re- ceived from Captain Reginald Smith, and dated May 18th :

Fatal Bullet In The Head.

"It is my sad duty to inform you that your son, Private John Chumley, was killed last night when going into the trenches. He received a bullet wound in the head and his death was painless and instantaneous. Your son was a member of my company for some years and so I knew him well and was able to appreciate his worth. He had an abso- lutely clean record, and his death has greatly upset me. It may be some consolation to you that he died doing his duty to his King and country. Will you and your family plea..e accept the deepest sympathy of myself and his comrades, in the great loss you have sus- tained P'

===== 0002601_19150522_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
A Last Letter.

A day before his death the. private wrote as follows to a friend, Mr. T. Cawley, of Pick- mere-street, Warrington : "Thanks very much for the parcel and letter which I received all right. Sorry I could not write sooner, because we have had a very busy time and a long while in the trenches, the second battalion being called away from us to charge Hill 60. In it they did fine, but I am sorry to say they lost all the officers but one, and a company of men, leaving us to hold our line for thirteen days, with about thirty-six casualties. When you read the local papers next week you will see one of the casualties mentioned—Private Salt. I was next but one .to him when he got hit. He just came up to the aim when a bullet struck him through the head and he died instantly. Well, Tom, I am enjoying the parcel you sent; I was not ex- pecting one quite so big I suppose

cal officer's servant, serving with the Terri- torials, who wrote as follows : "Just a line at the request of your son, James, of B Company, to let you know that he has been hit in the neck. lam glad to say, however, that the wound is very slight. He received every attention immediately he had been hit best of spirits. I remained with him for some time after the doctor had finished dressing him, and I can assure you that he is quite all right. You might have an op- portunity of seeing him at home in a few days. He was wounded on May 10th."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
Clever With His Pencil.

The deceased soldier was a keen footballer and was in the St. Helens Cable Company's team the year they won the cup in the Works' Competition, but did not play in the team which actually lifted the trophy. He was clever with his pencil, displaying no mean artistic powers at black-and-white drawing, of which he was intensely fond. He was edu- cated at the Wycliffe Day School. Whilst training at Tunbridge Wells he played football for his company's team, Sergeant Skelhorne being inqluded in the eleven. He attended the Wycliffe Brotherhood services and was extremely popu- lar with his fellow-workers and chiefs. Close upon 70 employes of the- Cable Co. are now serving in the Army.

Great Day In Local Catholic History.

His Eminence, Cardinal Bourne, has promised to attend the solemn opening of the new St. Benedict's Roman Catholic Church, in July. He will be the first Cardinal to officiate at a local church.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
the base hospital in France, and embarked-- along with fifteen hundred more *ounded. Upon arrival in this country he was conveyed to Aintree, passing through Warrington at midnight on Sunday. At Aintree he only stayed a few hours, and on Monday morning he arrived at Southport, where he now lies. The two " lots " the injured man was with had been relieving each other in the trenches for twenty-eight days without a wash cr shave —and, indeed, could only get food by means of parcels sent from home, which were brought to them if there came a lull in the fighting The Saxon-Germans, adds Private Griffiths, are gentlemen to fight With, but the Prussians —if they once take a trench they do nothing but fire all the while.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
HIS STORY.

Son of Mr. and Mrs. Joseph Griffiths, the Rifleman was born in Warrington nineteen years ago, being educated at St. Benedict's School, Orford-lane. At the age of thirteen ha went to work with Mr. Reid, grocer—who was then in business by the Central Station. Here he remained for two years, then going as a wire-roper to Messrs. Rylands Bros., which position he 'retained until 2nd September. It was then that he enlisted in the 4th Rifle Brigade, going into training and in turn visiting, during the ensuing seven months, Winchester, Sheerness, Isle of Sheppey, and Eastminster. Finally—on the last day of March—he crossed over to the Front, where he has been serving until wounded. Each week he has written home, and only a fortnight ago a letter from him said he was fighting only forty yards off the Prussian Guards!

riffi

In peace, times, Rifleman _.-as attends Benedict's Church. He has taken a great in- terest in gymnastics, with other workers, at the Rylands's recreation ground, and was thus- in fine form at the time of his enlistment. He has three cousins in the British Army, another cousin with the New Zealanders, and two uncles also with the colours. His father is employed by Messrs. Winstanley as a painter.

THE . EXAMINER, S ATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915.

PRIVATE JOHN HAYES.

News Of His Death In A Neighbour's

Letter.

News reached Warrington yesterday through the medium of a letter written by Private Stanley Hill, whose wife lives in Regent-street, that Private John Hayes, whose home is also in Regent-street—at No. 11—had been killed, the writer adding that the fatality occurred on Sunday last, death being instantaneous. Official intimation has not, however, yet come to hand.

The deceased was married, and leaves a wife and four children. He was an old Volunteer and, altogether, had had about ten years' ex- perience of civilian-soldiering. He was in B Company of the Territorials, and was the son of Mr. and Mrs. Wm. Hayes, of Forster-street. He received his education at the Silver-street School and, at the time of his rejoining the Territorials, and offering himself for active service, was employed by Messrs. Winstanley, painters and decorators. A number of his cousins are serving in the Army. The deceased's father played football for Warrington.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0149.txt | included=true | included =====
WOUNDED,

PRIVATE MIES NAYLOR.

"Doing Fine" Atter Being Hit In The

Neck.

Private James Naylor, 19, Vere-street, has been wounded, and is at present in the Nor- thern Hospital, Leicester. The intimation was conveyed to the wounded man's mother by Private B. Watson, a medi-

===== 0002601_19150522_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JAMES NAYLOR Wounded.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
Shelled Out of Their Billets.

Writing home at the end of last month, the wounded man said: "You cannot realise what it is like out here. For the last three days [April 26th, 27th and 28th] the Germans have shelled our billets so much that we have,had to move out of them Yesterday

[April 27th] the Germans shelled us and one of their shells hit Peter Denton and blew him into a hundred pieces. J. Clough was next to him when it happened, but he was not touched. This is the second time Jack has just missed being hit; last time he had his rifle blown out of his hands and his tackle stripped off him J. Clough and S. Ratcliffe are in the same platoon as myself and they send their best regards."

Ha'pennies As Souvenirs For Young Brothers.

The following day he sent another letter home in which he enclosed two half-pennies, with the request that they should be given to two of his young brothers as souvenirs "should any- thing happen." The next heard frau him was in the shape of a postcard, which read :--" Just a line to let you know that I am on my way to England; I do not know, to what part." Following that curt message came a letter, dated from the lst/sth Northern Hospital, Leicester, in which the gallant ' Terrier ' says I am doing fine here, and ray wounds are healing up," and adds a request that "The Examiner" may be for- warded to him.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0152.txt | included=true | included =====
Father Also Serving

Private Naylor's father is serving in the National Reserve, being stationed in the South of England. Both father and son received their education at Silver-street School, under Mr. John •Upson's supervision, and there are four of the 'family—a rather large one—still attending that school. Private Naylor, who is 21 years of age, was . employed by Messrs. Rylands' Bros., and was one of the ", Race'" footballers. He belonged to the second team for two seasons and was a very promising for- ward. He played in the St. Helens District League.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0153.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOSEPH HOWARTh.

Hit In The Elbow Whilst Taking

Rations To Trenches.

-News was received on Saturday morning that Private Joseph Howarth, of A Company, V4th (Territorial) Battalion South Lancs., had been wounded in action. The news was conveyed to Mrs. Joseph Howarth, at Long-row, Orford, in a letter, dated May 13th, written by Private Howarth himself. It ran as follows :

So Many Different Hospitals.

Just a few lines to let you know that I got wounded on Tuesday night, and I am now in hospital. We came out of the trenches on Monday night, after being nine days in them, and one of our other com- panies took our place. Of course, we had to take rations down to them, and it was while on the return journey that I got hit. It is a bullet wound in the left elbow, and it is very painful.

I did -not receive any of the parcels you sent me, because I was in the trenches, and I do not suppose I shall get any now, for there are so many, different hospitals here, that they will not know which one I am in.

Doing Fine! Later, Diits. Howarth received the following letter from her husband: "I am doing fine. You do not need to upset yourself about me, for I think I shall be all right. I am living in hopes of being in England before long. They have not taken the bullet out of my arm yet. It is very nice in the hospital here. We get good food, and the doctors and sisters are very

"May 15th,—I ani sorry to let you know that your husband, Private John Henshall, B Com- pany, has been wounded this day, but I am sure he will soon be all right again, as the wound is only slight. He got hit in the shoulder and was brought into our dressing station this evening. Dr. Law did everything he possibly could. He will leave here to-night in the ambulance for the clearing station, and then he will be dispatched to the base hospital, and probably you will have a chance to see him in a few days. I was very sorry when I saw it was Jack, as we have been bosom friends for a considerable time. I can assure you he is all right, and you have no need to worry about him. I dare say his officer will let you know and write you as soon as his company comes out of the trenches."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0154.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE JOHN HENSHALL Wounded.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0155.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE WALTER CASWELL.

A Correction.

Last week we published a letter received from Private Walter Caswell, of the 20th Hussars, who is wounded and staying at the 3rd Southern Hospital, Oxford, having lost an eye, through shell fire. We regret to say that we erroneously stated that Private Caswell was a single man. He is married and has one child. His wife is stay- ing temporarily, with his mother at 13, Percy-street. The letter from him was addressed to his wife, not to his mother.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0156.txt | included=true | included =====
SERGEANT KENWRIGHT.

Sergeant Kenwright, who was reported in our last issue wounded, lives with his father Ind mother at 237, Knutsford-road, not with hie widowed mother, as stated.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0157.txt | included=true | included =====
LANCE-CORPORAL J. IL KNOWLES.

Education Committee Sympathise With Him In His Injury.

Sympathetic reference was made at Monday night's meeting of the Warrington Education Committee to the fact that Lance-Corporal J. H. Knowles, of the 4th South Lanca.shires, and one of the school attendance officers for the borough, had been wounded, Mr. B. Plinston, the chairman of the School Attendance Sub- committee, in proposing that a letter of Sym- pathy be sent to the injured man and his wife, remarked that the gallant lance-corporal was the first man wounded among the coterie who had left municipal work in Warrington. He was sure the committee hoped that Lance- Corporal Knowles would be speedily restored to full health.

Mr. G. Walker seconded, and the proposition was carried, the Chairman (Councillor H. Roberts) remarking that he was sure the feel- ing of the Committee on a matter like that would be unanimous.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0158.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. FOX'S BROTHER-IN-L\ 1 KILLED.

Captain Wm. A. Taylor, of the St. Helens Territorials (the sth South Lancashires) has died of wounds received in action. He was wounded in the back of the neck by a shrap- nel bullet, which divided the spinal cord and completely paralysed him. He was taken to Boulogne Hospital, where he died before his wife, who had been summoned by wire, could reach him. The deceased captain was interred at Boulogne with jnilitary honours. Captain Taylor carried on an engineering works at Rainford Junction and. resided at the Gap, Rainford. He was 37 years of age. The son .f Mr. Thomas Taylor, colliery proprietor, Parbold, he was educated at Ushaw College. .In 1902 he received his commission in the sth South Lancashires. In 1910 he married Mary, eldest daughter of the late Dr. Fox, J.P., of Warrington and New Brighton, and sister of Dr. Fox, of Warrington. He went to the Front in February. He leaves three young children. Captain Taylor was a very keen and energetic officer and was particularly in- terested, in signalling. Up to a year or two ago he was the divisional signalling officer.

When the war broke out, Lieutenant Fulton- Smith—then holding an appointment at the works of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, brewers—relinquished civilikn life and went to Edinburgh to re-join his old regiment the 9th Royal Scots, which was formed in 1900 for the Boer War. He re-joined as a private, but was later promoted to a second lieutenancy.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0159.txt | included=true | included =====
LIEUT. IVAN FULTON-SMITH. Died of Wounds.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0160.txt | included=true | included =====
Belonged to Local Volunteers.

Educated, in the first place, at the High School, Edinburgh, Lieutenant Fultoii-Smith subsequently attended the Warrington Gram- mar School. As a youth, he was a keen sports- man and a good shot. He was at one time associated with the local Territorials.

Lieutenant Fulton-Smith enjoyed the respect of both officers and men with whom te came into contact in the 9th Royal Scots Regiment. Much sympathy is felt for his father, who has done splendid work for the local Volunteer Training Corps, of ' which he is chairman.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0161.txt | included=true | included =====
RIFLEMAN E. LUNT.

Injuries In The Right Thigh And The

Left Arm.

Wounded in two places, Rifleman Elijah Lunt, of the 2nd Battalion Rifle Brigade, who up till September, when he enlisted, lived with his mother, Mrs. E. Lunt, at 42, Rowley-lane, Warrington, now lies in hospital at Manches- ter.

Rifleman Lunt, who was in the famous battle of Neuve Chapelle, was wounded on May 9th in the right thigh and the left arm, and three days later he was in hospital in Manchester. From there he despatched a post-card to his mother, and that was the first intimation of his injury received at home.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0162.txt | included=true | included =====
A Good Bowler.

Twenty-seven years of age, Rifleman Lunt is a good bowler. He is a member of the Bull's Head Bowling Club. As a boy, he attended the Hamilton-street School, and on leaving there he entered the service of the Warrington Tanning Company, in whose employ he con- tinued till September Ist, when he enlisted in Kitchener's Army. He departed for the Front on the 27th of January. Rifleman Lunt has a younger brother in the Warrington Territorials, who has been at the Front some time.

The late Mr. Edward Lunt, father of the

RIFLEMAN LITNT. Wounded.

PRIVATE JOHN MURPHY. Gassed.

taken to hospital. through the effects of gas— that poisonous stuff the Germans sent into the air on May 2nd. I am now getting on very nicely, so don't worry about me. But it was awful to see the rest of the men fighting for their breath and the rest of them dropping on the field unable to go any further, just as if they were shot. It was a shocking sight to see. "I received the parcel you sent me—lags, cakes, ham and sweets. • Send me some fags at once, as we can't get any in this camp'. I should like Susie to tell Emmy Jackson that I met one of her brothers—the one in the A.S.C. —the other week, and he looks very well. Tell Mrs. Mitchell, Pig Hill, that I met her oldest son and he looks very well."

Private Murphy, who was employed by Mr. Melbourne, Wilderspool Ironworks, enlisted last August and has boon in France about six months. He had only recently written to say he was enjoying the best of health—that he had never enjoyed better.

MIL I ISON'S NEPHEW

ruseallied By

Germans, But Laid

Aside By Rheumatism.

Amongst the sick and wounded soldiers brought to Warrington on Sunday night for the Lord Derby Hospital at Winwiek,' In- spector Williamson discovered Private Henry Upson, the son of Councillor A. Upson, J.P., ex-Mayor of Maidenhead, and nephew of Mr. John Upson. Private H. Upson joined the Seaforth Highlanders at the outbreak of war, and as he was an excellent shot, was sent to the Front within six weeks of joining. He had been in the trenches since November sth, took part ii the prolonged fighting at La Basse, and was in the thick of the battle at Neuve Chapelle. Beyond a slight wound in the foot at La Besse, which did not incapacitate him, he came " through his prolonged work in the trenches unscathed by the enemy, but the long winter's exposure to the wet and cold of the trenches has brought on acute rheumatism of the spine and legs. He has been in hospital at Rouen and Boulogne, and is now transferred to Winwick.

IFLEII ILLI All (dUFFITHS.

Fought For(% Yards From Prussian

Guards.

At his home, 57, Lairs-street, arrived on Thursday of last week a field postcard from Rifleman Wm. Griffiths, No. 319, 4th Rifle Brigade, telling his mother—with whom he makes his home—that he was wounded, and had been admitted into hospital. 'On Monday afternoon this was followed by a further card, which showed that he was under the care of the British Red Cross Society, and had arrived at the Homcepathic Cottages Hos- pital, Southport. Accordingly on Tuesday after- noon his mother and sister paid him a visit.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
Shot Through Head, and " Gassed."

The Rifleman's sister is Mrs. Gilgrist, of 53, Laira-etreet.

It was found that the Rifleman had not only been shot through the head, but also slightly "gassed.". Nevertheless, he is progressing very satisfactorily, and was quite cheered by the visitors' call. He explained that when he was wounded his " lot " had just relieved the first batch in the trenches, and he started writing a letter to a friend. Suddenly "a stunned sensation" came over him without a moment's warning, and he remembers no more until finding himself in the dressing room. On the Thursday night he was brought from

PRIVATE JOHN MANSON.

Member Of Borough Constabulary Reported Killed.

It has been reported in the local papers at his ,home in Caithness-shire that Constable John Manson, of the Warrington Police,, has been killed in action in France. P.C. Manson, a native of Caithness, joined the local force in. March, 1911. He had served in a Volunteer company of the Seaforth Highlanders in the South African War, and on sth February last he re-enlisted in his old regiment for active' service, being drafted to the 3rd Battalion at Cromarty, where he remained until the 20th April, when he left for the Front to join the Seaforths there.

Confirmation of the News Still Lacking.

Letters have been received at Wick, Ciiit ness, by relatives of men serving with the 2nd' Battalion Seaforth Highlanders, and in one of them it is mentioned' that Private John Manson- had fallen in action. Immediately this news reached Warrington the Chief Constable com- municated with the War Office, and also with the depot of Manson's regiment, at Fort George, Scotland, but up to going to press no confirma- tion of the report has been received. It will be recellected that already 29 members of the local police are serving in 11. M. Forces, and ut) to now there has only been one killed, viz., Constable Grubb, of the Royal Berkshire Regiment, who fell at Gheluvelt, on October 26th, 1914. Much anxiety is naturally being felt by P.C. Manson's comrades, who are hoping the report may not be true.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
PEARSON AND KNOWLES' LABOURER KILLED.

Rifleman Thomas Darey, 4th Rifle Brigade, was killed in action on May 4th, and official news reached his father, Mr. Thomas Dacey, Corporation Gas Works employe, 24, Bluecoat- street, this week.

Rifleman Dacey, who enlisted last September, was a labourer at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles' Ironworks, and would have been nineteen years

of age on the Sunday after he was killed. As a boy he attended St. Benedict's School. Mr. Dacey has another son serving in the second battalion of the local Territorials.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
FEARNHEAD MAN WOUNDED

In The Fierce Attack Upon The

Dardanelles.

Private N. Scott Ward, of the 13th Battalion of Australian Infantry, on Sunday was re-. ceived into the Moseley-road Hospital, Man- chester, suffering from wounds sustained in the attack upon the Dardanelles. Private Ward is the son of Mr. T. Ward, of Fearn- head. He is 27 years of age. About six years ago he 'went out to New Zealand to relatives engaged in farming in that colony, but before that time he was well known in the Fearnhead district, having received his education at Pad- gate Schdol and People's College, and having been actively connected with Risley Methodist Chapel and the Padgate Tent of Rechabites.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
Landed in Face of Strong Resistance.

Soon after the commencement of the war he enlisted in the 2nd Australian Expeditionary Force, and, with them, was in training several weeks in Egypt. Along with the other Austra- lian and New Zealand soldiers he took part in the assault upon the Turks in the Gallipoli Peninsula. Having landed in the face of strong resistance, he had been in the firing-line barely twenty-four hours when he received two rifle wounds, one bullet passing tlqough a knee and another entering the left wrist, which was taken out about a week after between the elbow and the shoulder. The knee wound has healed rapidly, but it is expected that several weeks will elapse before the arm can be freely used.

One of our corresp mionts hearing he was wounded paid him a visit, ar.i on asking bow he felt, he replied " Oh, all right.' The soldiers, including Mr. Ward, were busily engaged in a game of hockey with a soft ball, and it was very evident they were thoroughly enjoying themselves and making as light as possible of their wounds. During conversation he said that the fighting was very severe, and that the great difficulty was in finding out where the Turks were situated. Just before he was wounded he had been digging some earth: out of the trench, and at the time he was shot was just enjoying a few minutes well-earned rest. He thought the bullets which struck him , had been fired from behind their trenches, as ' the Turkish snipers seemed to be situated all round them and bullets were coming from all directions. After being shot he waited for some time for the stretcher-bearers, but think- ing that in all probability the Turks might rush the trenches he got up and, though suffering from severe pain in both leg and arm, he hobbled down to the beach himself. He was taken to the hospital in Alexandria, and has now been sent to Fallowfield. _ _

He is walking about and is quite cheerful, and says that in all probability if he had been actually sitting in the trench he had made he might have been shot through the head. As many of the wounded from Fallow&ld have been removed to Winwick, there is a proba- bility that he may eventually be sent there also.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
ORFORD MAN WOUNDED.

Private John Clougher, who previous to his enlisting in the Rifle Brigade, was employed at Orford Tannery, has been wounded in the arm, and is in hospital at Carlisle. Private Clougher lives in Sandy-lane with his parents. He went to the Front the first week in March.

Female War Labour.—This Week has been introduced into the Lymm district female war labour—a lady being installed in the railway booking office at Heatley.

Volunteer Drill.—A Battalion Drill of the Cheshire Volunteer Regiment—including the Lymm section—is to take place at Rostherne on Monday. More Belgians Leave.—Yesterday afternoon another batch of Belgian refugees bade good- bye to " Beechwood "—their Lymm; home. The reason is not stated.

"Badly Cut Up In Charge."—Writing home on the 10th, a Lymm " Terrier " said: "One of our companies Made a charge on Friday Morning. They got badly cut up, and lost very nearly all the officers. . ."

• Church Parade At F ront —Private Jack • Moston, of the "Terriers," writes from the Front: We are having lovely weather; we have just been to a church parade and heard a fine sermon. .. . I wish I was coming home for Whit Sunday; it would be nice. . . ."

Soldier's Sad Leave.—Sergeant Robert Jones— who, a few weeks ago, was over at Lymm on sick leave from Sevenoaks—has. just had the misfortune to lose his youngest child, aged four months. To enable him to attentl the funeral, the sergeant was granted 48 hours' leave of absence.

"Master Thurston" Cake At Front —Writing to his wife early in the week Sergeant Clare says: "I have received a parcel from the Rev. G. B. Thurston, containing some of Master John's christening cake, and have given all our men a little, and all were very delighted with it. It was very nice."

Butchers' Hours.—At the Spread Eagle Hotel on Tuesday evening, the Lymm butchers met to consider the curtailment—or otherwise—of their shop hours during the present crisis. After much discussion they adjourned their decision until a meeting held last night. The result appears in o-ir "Local Intelligence," page 5.

"Terriers" Entertain Friends.—Private Jas. Gilbert writes: "We had Peter Percival on our ground the other day—having tea with us —and he does look well. We had another man, named Wibberley, from Statham. . , . Peter is doing well, and he would have liked to have stated with us for good; but they would not let him V'

Did Their Duty.—At the Lymm Petty Ses- sions, held at the Police Station on Wednesday morning, there were on the Bench: Messrs. A. Watkin (in the chair), S. Davies, R. H. Goto- bed, and Holden; and the Hon. J. E. Cross.— Mr. Gotobed, having just been elected to the Council chairmanship, was duly sworn in as a magistrate.

Promoted Corporal.—Private George Drink- water, of the Bth South Lancashires—whose home is at Rush Green, Lymm, and who is now training in the South—has just been Pro- moted to the rank of Corporal. He also, by the way, has two brothers in the South Lanca- shires.

Cookery Mistress' Appointment.—Miss Mary Harrison, cookery mistress at Lymm under the Cheshire Education Committee---which position she has held for a year or two with all-round satisfaction—has been appointed to a similar position at Hinley, near Atherton. Yesterday Miss Harrison left Lymm to take up her new duties.

Where Has The Cake Gone?—Private P. Percival writes to 3, West Hyde, New&lds, to thank his wife for a parcel: "I got your par- cel last night, but there was no cake in it [as had been sent]. There was some tobacco and matches in it, and somebody else's parcel tied up in it!" It is rather a pity about the cake, for it was a capital piece of confectionery— and the postage, too, was a trifle heavy. The private has, however, received safely parcels from one or two other relatives.

One Man: Two Offences.—Thomas Coppock, of Chapel-street, Stockport, was summoned at Wednesday's Sessions for having, on the high- way_ at Lymm on Friday, May 7th, driven a motor cycle and side-car without a rear light; and also for failing to produce his licence when requested by a constable.—To both offences he pleaded guilty.—A constable said, that at 10.30 p.m. on Penny Hill he whistled to the driver, who stopped his machine. He said "the rear light had been a proper nuisance. It was connected with the front lamp, and seemed to go low, and it would jog out." Asked for his licence he said he had one, but then 'found he had left it in another suit when changing, previous to leaving home. He sent it on, however, three lays later.—Defendant said a Lancashire constable told him the rear light bye-law was not operative in Cheshire.— The Chairman: Well, at any rate the light was' out. You will be fined half-a-crown, in- cluding costs, in each case.

n

Handicapped School Attendance.—John Hil- ton, labourer, of The Green, Partington, appeared at Wednesday's Lymm Petty Sessions in respect of his daughter Annie (13) not attending Cadishead School.—Mr. C. E. Hill (attendance officer for Sale district) said the girl, having passed Standard V., had made five attendances out of a possible 115 during 13 weeks. She came under the Cheshire bye- laws. In the previous year she attended four times from September 16th.—It transpired that the father- had been ill, whitst the mother, a cripple, needed her daughter's help at home. The earnings of the girl's brother and sister were needed to help to keep the house, and so the latter could not stay at home in Annie's stead.—The father said he did not know he would have to apply to the committee. He could not afford to pay for domestic help.— The Chairman (Mr. A. Watkin) said defendant was taking advantage of the committee's pre- vious leniency in similar cases, and he would be fined half-a-crown, including costs. If the girl did not attend regularly in future the man would be severely punished.

Why Child Did Not Attend.—Edward Black- burn, of Moss-brow, Warburton, was sum- moned at Wednesday's Petty Sessions in re- spect of his son Stanley (51) not yet attending school.—He said the boy's health was not good, and then when he had been going to send him sickness broke out at the Partington School, and "he thought he had better keep the boy away for a bit."-The Chairman: And then you took the law into your own hands!— Mr. Mercer (registrar): This man seems set on defiance, and in spite of warnings sent out by the Chairman and the Clerk is determined not to send the child to school. We ask you to assist us in these matters. The case has been adjourned four times.—Defendant: I am on Government work, and my wife was not fit to attend; she has not been well all this year. I leave home at 6.30 a.m.•and don't get back till 10 at night—The Chairman: But you ,have only two children. Surely you could send him. You are. a .very foolish man; why didn't you appear before the committeeP—Defendant : I have only seen one letter.—Mr. Mercer: Well, I have sent the letters addressed to him.—ln fining the man 2s. 6d., including costs, the Chairman said that the next time the case came up he would be severely dealt with.

"Two Manchester Girls."—ln our last issue we gave a _poem on "The Death of the First Lymm Warrior"—written by Mr. James H. Appleton (17), of Statham. This week he has received a letter which says: " . . . We

wish to congratulate you on your splendid poem, which we found extremely interesting to read in 'The Warrington Examiner.' We are two Manchester girls, but we hdve many friends in Warrington, so have 'The Examiner' forwarded on to us every week by one of our friends. . . . Sorry to hear of your chum (Private Leigh) being killed in action; but . . . he gave his life for his King and country. . . . If it should be anyone's luck to capture Kaiser Wil- liam, we hope it will be the Russians—because if the British got hold of him he would have an easier life of it. . . .—We remain, yours *lily, Sybil Worthington and Florence Field- ing."—Mr. Appleton has written another capital poem on "The Loss of the Lusitania."

POOR RATE INCREASE.

Sanctioned By The Justices.

At the Police Court on Wednesday, the justices sanctioned the levying of a poor rate of is. Bd. in the 5.; on buildings and 10d. in the £ on land: Last year's rates were is. 6d. and 9d. respectively.

MOTOR ACCIDENT.

Three Lymm People Involved At

Warrington.

When Mr. Edward Gilberts, of " Newfield "- was coming from his work on Wednesday night at about a quarter to- six, and was proceeding along Thelwall-lane, Warrington, the motor- cars of Mr. Marsh (of Bradley Hall, Cherry- lane, Lymm) and Mr. J. Houghton (of West- lea, Lymm), approached in the opposite direc- tions and attempted to pass each other. At that particular spot the road is narrow. Mr. Gilberts was knocked down and fell, striking his head on the ground. For a short time rendered unconscious, he was carried into an adjacent cottage, where he was attended to. He was then conveyed home in one of the motors, and is now progressing well—under the care of Dr. Be Jong. •

===== 0002601_19150522_art0182.txt | included=true | included =====
FIRE AT STATHAM.

Considerable Damage Covered By

Insurance.

At "The Croft," Statham—the residence of Mr. Jones-Parry—a fire broke out at 2.30 on Wednesday afternoon. The outbreak occurred near the roof, where some painters were engaged on the outside of the facie spouting. It is, of course, not known who caused it. The fire bell was rung at the Lymm Station ten minutes later, and the brigade turned out under the superintendence of Mr. Wm. Kirk- patrick. On arrival at the house, they found the roof on fire; one of the attic bedrooms, with its furniture and contents, was also ablaze. The flames were findlly extinguished after six busy and exciting hour, and by order of Mr. Melbourne (agent for the property), the fire- fighters cleared all the broken slates, Sze., from the roof and the floors.

Before their departure Messrs. Melbourne and Jones-Parry complimented the brigade on their smart work.

The Damage. The damage is estimated at between £5O and £6O. It is covered by insurance.

"RAID ON GERMAN."

Rumoured Event And Police Court

Sequel.

John Blackledge, jun., whose address was that of a farm at Agden, was summoned at the Petty Sessions on Wednesday for annoying street passengers by the use of obscene language at Lymm on Saturday night.—He pleaded guilty. Sergeant Davies said that at 10.30 p.m. he was in Grammar School-lane, when Blackledge came up and addressed a group of young men who were there—using obscene language. He (the sergeant) went across and asked him why, and defendant at first denied the offence.

Superintendent Sutherland: Is it a fact that on this night the house of a naturalised Ger- man was to be attacked?—The Sergeant: Yes; I was there in consequence. Defendant had nothing to say except that he was not drunk.

The Sergeaut: He had had a little, I should say!

"There For That Purpose."

The Superintendent: It is really a serious matter. The rumour had got about that this house was going to be attacked, and there is no doubt that this man and some others were there for that purpose; but we were there before them. .

Defendant: I didn't go up tb.ere with any intention of that kind at all.

Superintendent Sutherland said Blackledge had not been before the Court before.

"If You Want To Show Your Feelings-" The Chairman (Mr. A. Watkin) to defendant : If you want to show your feelings towards the Germans you had better join the Army. You seem to be the right sort of man for it. It is very fine to come here and talk about Ger- mans in this way, but you had better go and fight them. Defendant was fined 55., including costs.
