TIRED TERRITORIALS.

Feeling A Bit Worn-Out.

Yesterday morning Mrs. G. Davies received a letter from her son, Private Percy Clare: "It is rumoured around here that we are going to have a rest. . . . There is not one of us but feels run down. We always feel tired—what with the hard work at night and when we are supposed to be haring our few days' rest from the trenches, and when we are in the trenches, and with all the noise of the battlefield I can tell you we do feel like a good rest. We have had rather a stiff three months; but never mind! . . . We keep on going, for it can't go on for ever. . . . On the 10th he wrote: "We are at present in the midst of heavy fighting, and are con- tinually in the trenches. This makes the eleventh day 1n the fire-trench and supports. ." In word dispatched on the 12th, he said:— "About three o'clock this morning we arrived at the fields from the trenches. . . .

Harry Cadman has got back to us again. . .

Tith EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915.

LYIVIM TERRITORIAL ROLL OF HONOUR.

MORE KILLED AND WOUNDED.

The Lymm "Terriers" at the Front came out of the trenches on Tuesday, May 11th, after serving in them for ten days. The weather was very good. Those ten days were rather xciting, and the Lymm men sustained the following casualties:

KILLED. Captain B. S. Walker (Southport)

WOUNDED. Private Stanley Gibbs. Private Cecil Acton (High Legh)

Private J. Clare writes: "Cecil -Act9n and S. Gibbs have got wounded, but not serious." Considerable anxiety has arisen in the/ cases :

-- • Private Henry Brookes and Private H. Cadman (previously wounded), who are reported wounded by the War Office— for no other news of these cases has reached Lymm! Others of the Lymm "Terriers" have served different periods with different sections. Sergeant Harold Clare has been at the base hospital for a day or two, suffering from neu- ralgia, but by now will probably be back in the firing-line. Writing to his wife a day or two ago he said he wished to be back with thel "lads." This latest periett in the trenches is described by Private P. Percival: "We have had a rough do' of 4, I can tell you. I dare say you will see about it in the papers. It is awful—that gas they are using. We saw fellows come staggering along as if they were drunk; and men lying all over the place, waiting to be buried. But lam all right so far. Our battalion has been in the trenches for over, a three-week, and now we have come out for a few days' rest. . ." Yesterday morning a note from Private H. Cadman, dated May 15th, ran: "Our com- pany had rather a long stay in the trenches this last time, for they have been in the sup- ports and in the trenches for abolit sixteen or seventeen days. .. ." Private J. Read's communication of the 11th, ran: "I expect we are being relieved to-night from the trenches—after being four days in the firing line. We shall be glad of a short rest, I can tell you Just before being Wounded, Private S. Gibbs wrote: "Some of the other reinforcements have joined us, but not very many. Up here on top of the Hill we have a fine view of the German trenches, and we can see them at work. This is the first time I have seen a live German. We have seen plenty of dead through glasses." Writing five days after this, Private James Gilbert said: "We are hoping to have another day or two out yet." On Saturday, May 15th—the latest news—the men were still " resting " out of the trenches. PriVate Henry Cadman, writing during a respite after two. days in the trenches, , says: "We are now having a rest, and the town which we were in when I left them is an awful sight now. The Germans are shelling every jay with 17in. shells, and I can tell you they have made it a heap of ruins; it is blazing with fire at night. We had used to be billeted in the town, but now we have to go and live in the fields—or anywhere we can get. I am sure there must be heaps of dead in the place; and no one has to go anywhere nearer the town,' for the shells are dropping all round the place., . _

- Lance-Corporal S. Linley and Private Arthur Hadden, having been home on a few days' fur- lough after being wounded and accidentally hurt respectively, have this week re-joined an- other section of their regiment in a Cheshire town.

The former is hoping to visit home again to- day, to stay the weck-entl. Lance-Corporal Edwin Blease (wounded) ar- rived home on Friday evening, returning to his depot on Thursday afternoon. Private James Taylor—who was the first Lymm "Terrier" to be wounded—remains convalescent in the Grantham Red Cross Hos- pital. His left /arm is still useless; and in a despondent letter home he remarks: "I don't think I shall ever get back the use of my arm."

Further silly rumours from Lymm have reached the " lads " in the trenches. Private Henry Brookes writes: "There is not the slightest bit of truth about Joe Brazendale being killed. Where such rumours get afloat I don't know. It is very curious. . ..."

Yesterday Private Percy Clare wrote: "The weather has broken over here. We are having some jery wet weather, and it is not so nice living in fields. . . . Everybody is up to the boot tops in mud. But we can put up with it"

this job was over, as we are all badly wanting a rest. . ." This word arrived on Monday. Private Brookes' wife is staying at South- port. On hearing the news site was greatly upset; but wrote: "I have had a .letter this morning from Harry. It was written on Fri4lay, so_l think there must be some mistake —and it must be some other soldier of the same name. Harry once told me there were several with the name of Brookes in their battalion."

PRIVATE S. GIBBS. Wounded.

shall post this when I get to Southampton. I don't know where and to which hospital in England lam going. I will let you know later. Don't worry. . . ." Officer's Tribute.

Svond-Lieutenant T. B. Morgan writes on May 13th:—" Dear Mrs. Gibbs,—Just a line to say how sorry I am that your husband has been wounded in the left hand. It is not at all serious. He has done some excellent work [as a range-finder] in locating stores, guns, tkc., be- hind the German lines."

Private Percy Clare—who has been acting at the Front as Private Gibbs' reserve, and now succeeds him jn the duties, and who subscribes himself as "Stan's pal," also drops a line to the wife of the wounded private: "Stanley has got wounded; but don't upset yourself, as he is quite all right. . . . I left him last night [the 12th] and he was in the best of spirits. He told me that he was right for what we call blighty '—that means England; so I hope it will not be long before he gets home. lam sorry to lose him, as we have ben together all along. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0189.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPTAIN B. S. WALKER.

"'Terriers'' Very Good Friend" Killed By Sniper.

. It is with deep regret that we announce the death of Captain B. S. Walker, of the sth Cheshires—who has been shot in the firing-line by a Thiper. Only three weeks ago he had been promoted from the rank of lieutenant. The Captain was not a Lymm man; his home is at Southport. Along with Captain Hartley, he was, however, in charge of the „local "Terriers "4,-by whom he was most deeply re- spected and honoured. From these sad soldiers many magnificent tributes come to hand.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0190.txt | included=true | included =====
" No One Could Realise It."

Private S. Gibbs (who has himself been wounded) writes: "I am sorry to say that our officer, Lieutenant B. S. Walker, was killed by a sniper. Not five minutes before it happened he paid me a visit. He and another officer had beem on the top of Hill 60 all day observing. The last words the officer said to me were 'Good news, Gibbs, keep your head down, and good luck!'

- "The next thing a sergeant came to the bottom of the hill to say that the officer had been killed. No one in the trenches could realise it, as he was one of the best of officers, and every man liked him. We have lost a very good friend. Captain Hartley is very upset."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0191.txt | included=true | included =====
" Our Dear Platoon Officer."

Private Joseph Clare's word home runs : "I am very sorry to say that we have lost our dear platoon officer, Captain Walker. . . It upset us very much, because he was one that we could follow anywhere; and if he had ever said: 'Come on over the trench and at 'ern!' we should have gone. He was .really a very good and featless officer; but it must have been God's will. ."

Private Ernest Hadden says: "He was a great favourite amongst the Lymm lads, and will be missed very much...." Private Percy Clare says: "We have sus- tained a great loss by the death of our officer. He was a good, brave man, and . . . a gen- tleman. He was sniped. . . . It has cast a deep gloom all over our company."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0192.txt | included=true | included =====
"A Nice Funeral."

"He looked after his lads as though we were his own brothers. We have put him to rest this afternoon [the 12th]. I was one of the carriers. It was a nice funeral; all the Bat- talion lined up in the field, and we marched between them."

PRIVATE STANLEY GIBBS.

In Coast Hospital, Wounded In Left

Arm.

The first news of Private Stanley Gibbs hav- ing beeii wounded to reach Lymm, arrived on Saturday - morning—whbn Mrs. Gibbs, a Henry-street, received the following word from Private J. Read '(another Lymm com- rade), written ta, the 11th : Dear Mrs. Gib am writing to inform you of your husband, Stanley, being wounded to-day in his left hand by a bullet. He has been admitted into hospital, and is going on well. He will soon be coming to see you at Lymm, I hope. Your husband asked me if I would write to you. I must also inform you of another Lymm chap getting wounded on the same date, Cecil Acton, who was shot through the shoulder by a bullet. He is going on very well. They have both gone into .hospital.

The wounded Private himself wrote the news as an addition to a letter he had written to his wife just before the casualty:—" This letter was written just as I got wounded. I am well. I got shot through the left arm. I am in a hospital train going to Boulogne. I hope to be in England soon." On a card dated the 14th, and written at Rouen—en route for the homeland—Private Gibbs stated: "I am leaving here to-night, and

===== 0002601_19150522_art0196.txt | included=true | included =====
Visited By His Wife.

The wounded " Terrier " now lies, satis- factorily progressing, at the Liverpool 112rs- pital ; and on Wednesday he was viSited by his wife, who found him most cheerful and con- tented.

The bullet has passed through the palm of his left hand—fracturing two fingers and the knuckle of his middle finger. He says he will probably be eight weeks recovering.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0197.txt | included=true | included =====
His Career.

Born twenty-three years ago, Private Gibbs is a native of Aden. Leaving the boiungton School at the age of 14, he went to work as a wheelwright for Mr. Clarke—with whom he remained for two or three years. On similar work he then joined the firm of Messrs. Hampton, az Thelwall; but latterly had been in the employ of Mr. W. Kirkpatrick, Bridgewater-street, Lymm. He had belonged to the Territorials for five years, and had attended their several camps. At the outbreak of hostilities he was called up, and, after training in different parts of the country, went in February to the Front as a "range-finder." His letters home "The Examiner" has from time to time published. He is a worshipper at Lymm Church. Before his marriage he was prominently connected with the church at Oughtringtoh.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0198.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE CECIL ACTON.

High Legh Man Slightly Wounded In

Shoulder.

Mrs. S. Gibbs was the first person in Lymm to hear that Private Cecil Acton had been slightly wounded in the shoulder whilst at the Front with the sth Cheshires. She learned it

PRIVATE CECIL ACTON

Wounded.

through the medium of a letter from Private Jack Read, which is given above.

A Native of Crowley.

Aged 22 and unmarried, the gallant Private was born at Crowley, near Appleton; but he has for many years made his home at, High Legh. In times of peace he is employed as a gardener, at Dean's Green, by Colonel Gibves. He joined the Terri4urial Forces abcst three years ago, and had attended all the annual camps before the outbreak of war. Then he was called up with the other Lymm "lads," and with them went out to the war zone after the customary homeland training.

The Private belong to the Church of England

PRIVATE HENRY CADMAN.

AVounded Again Two Days Wei.

Return To 'Trenches?

Mrs. H. Cadman, of Booth's Hill, has received from the War Office the following notification of her ,stepson's second woundffig

He had been wounded in the ehest. His Cor- rect age also was given. Last week; as we have previously stated, Private Cadman had recovered from his first wounds, and returned to the trenches. Having been there with his Lymm comrades for two days, he wrote to his sister, Mrs. G. Rixon, of Pepper-street : "I am in the best of health, and glad to say that I am better and have joined my regiment again. I .have done two days in the trenches— which the Company have been in for sixteen or seventeen days. We are now having a rest." This letter WitA written on the 12th.

Yesterday morning- Mrs. Rixon received further word, written by the Private, on the 15th: "I am in the best of health. . . I have not had a letter since I got wounded. [Several have been sent—every week]. . . . They did not find me. . . . lam just going on guard. . . ." Than this no further report of the wounding —except rumour—has reached the township.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0200.txt | included=true | included =====
F. 11 I. NS AS GOOD SOLDIERS AS ENGLISH.

"As for the war ending soon," writes Private James Gilbert, of the sth Cheshires at the Front, to his friend, Mr. Fred Vernon (who also lives at Newfield, Lymm), "it never will; and if they do not come to tertns they will never finish it under two years or more. For nobody knows what it is like, only those that are over here—and they can hardly tell! "They used to tell us in England that the Germans could not shoot; but can't they! They are quite as good as us in everything from what I canesee."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0201.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE MAJOR FELL SICK."

mna "Terrier's" Happy Stay In Pretty French Village.

Private Ernest Had'den (C Company sth Cheshires) writes from the Front to his motker at Pepper-street:— "You will be surprised to hear that I have been away from the regiment for about seven or eight days. The Major fell sick, so I went down the•line with him. We stayed at a big house In a very pretty French village. We had glorious weather with brilliant sunshine, and the country around was quite a picture—and so quiet and peaceful it did so remind me of home!

"It came to an end yesterday. He was ordered farther down the line, and officers' servants can only go a certain distance with them. So I had to return to the company. I am once again with the Lymm lads. I expect the next time they go in the trenches I shall be with them; so it will be a little more ex- perience for me. Things are a lithe quieter at present. . . ."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0202.txt | included=true | included =====
SCOTS GUARD'S LUCK.

Private B. Elliott's Two Narrow "Shaves" In Big Battle.

Private Ben Elliott, of the Scots Guards— who lives with his parents at. Canterbury Cot- tage, Lymm—writes a graphic letter to his sister, Miss Millie Elliott, at Altrincham. . . . Since I wrote you last we have been in a big battle, and I have had two very narrow shaves. . . . As we were coming out of the trenches a bullet went through my pack (on my back). cornerways. There is a hole right through, but I don't mind. Then on Saturday I was out in my dug-out when I felt something hit my leg, and it was a piece of shrapnel from a shell that burst in front of our trench.

"I have got that. . . . I have got quite a lot of little relics like that, and shall stick to them. . . . A tale is attached to every one. . . .

===== 0002601_19150522_art0203.txt | included=true | included =====
So Near and Yet so Far.

" You have no idea what we boys are going through, but I trust to God to give us courage.

. . : Since I came out I have /Teen in Flan- ders, and Will [his brother] is in this battle. . . . I cannot come across him, but have no doubt I shall find him soon. . . . They are about two miles behind the trenches, and have a better chance than us, as they only have to watch where the shells are bursting, and have sa chance of getting out of the way. We have the bullets and shell-fire to watch--so that we are bobbing up and down all the time. That is why we have to keep coming ouf of the trenches—to get our nerves cool again.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0204.txt | included=true | included =====
Eating " That " Cake.

"Well, I think that cake is lovely, and you would have thought I had never seen a cake in my life if you had seen me sitting in a big field at three o'clock this morning eating it, with shells bursting all about me. I just kept bobbing my head up and down. . . ,"

===== 0002601_19150522_art0206.txt | included=true | included =====
BATCH OF WOUNDED

Arrive At "Brookfield "In Fine Spirits.

The hatch of wounded warriors—whose arrival at "Brookfield," the Lymm Red Cross Hospi- tal, has been expected for a week or two, as we have before mentioned—" landed" at that in- stitution on Wednesday afternoon. _

They occupy all the Wards and the operating- room—which have been beautifully re-decorated —including the new wards. Apart from their wounds, they appear in fine health, and their spirits are first rate. They progress satisfactorily under the care of Sister Colburn (the matron) and her well-trained staff of nurses.

The new arrivals number eighteen, so that there are now twenty-four wounded men in ",Brookfield:" The new-comers include: Gunner Arnold, Royal Field Artillery. Private W. Sutton, Black Watch. Private George Beattie, 2nd Black Watch. Private Wm. Birtwistle, King's Own Royal Lancasters. Private Foley, 2nd Munsters. Private S. Derry, 2nd Lancashire Fusiliers. Private Wm. Grand, Ist Northamptons. Lance-Corporal Dodson, Anglesey Royal Engi- neers.. Private Howard, Ist Sherwood Foresters. Private John Gorst, Ist Loyal North Lama- shires. Private Wm. Roffie, 2nd Lincolnshires. Private James Robertson, let Argyle Suther- land Highlanders. Private Melville, Ist Black Watch. Private McGillicuddy, 2nd Munsters. Rifleman John May, 2nd Rifle 'Brigade. And three others.

Poor Father.

Over the shop door of a pork butcher in a village in the Eastern .counties may be seen a signboard representing a man in a black coat, brandishing a hatchet, with the inscription: "John Smith kills pigs like his father."

REPAIRING 'HIE ROADS.

In the absence of the Chairman (Mr. Angus) owing to the illness of his wife, the Vice-Chairman (Mr. W. Harrison) presided at the monthly meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council, at Dutton, on Tuesday. Before taking his seat, the Vice-chairman proposed that a vote of sympathy be passed. The suggestion was adopted.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0210.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW LIGHTING RATES PENDING.

The Warrington Town Council wrote giving twelve months' notice to terminate the agree- ments for the lighting of Walton Inferior, Latchford Without, ra ppc nhall, Walton Superior and Appleton. The remark was passed that it was to be presumed the Corporation meant to raise the prices. The Clerk: Yes.

It was observed that the Corporation were only lighting the different places for a few hours nightly, and only on alternate nights in some instances. The question was asked whether the Council were going to get anything back.

The Clerk replied that reductions were being made.

RING OUT THE NEW: RING IN THE OLD. Mr. Pilling rose to move a resolution iii regard to the future meetings of the Council. He thought a great mistake was made by the Council when they decided to hold the meet- ings monthly instead of fortnightly as origin- ally. They met for business every fortnight, and he saw no reason why a general meeting should not be held every fortnight. In fact,* there was every reason why they should revert to the old system. It would expedite business considerably. He moved that the change be made. _ _ _

Mr. Woodward seconded. lie was perfectly satisfied that he was wrong when he thought that the system of meeting monthly would be an improvement, and he now felt that the best thing, the Council could do was to revert to the hitherto prevailing custom. The proposition was carried.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0211.txt | included=true | included =====
A REPRESENTATION RUSE.

The Clerk to the Comity Education Com- mittee wrote stating that t hat authority had agreed to the inclusion of Mr. Jas. Spruce in their number, as representative of the . Rural Council.

Mr. Pilling proposed that Mr. Spruce be appointed by the Council their representative on the Education Committee.

Mr. Woodward, however, pointed out that if the Council elected Mr. Spruce their repre- sentative, the area would lose in representa- tion. Mr. Spruce was already an independent member, and if the Council appointed another of their number they would virtually have two representatives on the committee.

Ur. Spruce said he thought it would be better for 'the Council to appoint someone else.

and made a_proposition to -that-effect.

_ Mr. Woodward seconded, and the Council concurred and appointed Mr. Baxter,

A Dominant Position

:rttrrs to the otator

COST 01."I'llE MUDGE.

Sir,—l saw in "The Examiner" that gr.-

Alderman Smethurst said "that many state- ments had been made with regard to the prob- able cost of the bridge, and not a few of them. were extremely wide of the mark "; and that the actual cost was £19,962 19s. 10d. This• amount he also refers to as the total cost. Is not the wortily Alderman in his total cost very far from the mark himself? Surely such items as purchase of extra land and property. alteration of tram lines, electric and gas mains, paving and other necessary work must all be included in the real price the ratepayers have to pay for the bridge. The conundrum still is, what has it actually cost? Can anybody tell? A RATEPAYER.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0217.txt | included=true | included =====
RESTRICTIONS ON FEMALE

L 11301.11.

Sir,—At the meeting held at the Town Hall last night, certain traders were asked to con- sider the question of a rearrangement of their staffs, with a view to estimating what men could be spared for war purposes. The chief suggestion made was that, wherever possible, female labour might be substituted. I tied to point out that, to help forward this arrange- ment, traders should have some assurance that they would not be interfered with unduly bY inspectors accustomed to a maximum use of red tape. Not being accustomed to speaking in public, I did not make my point understood. Aly reason for mentioning the matter at all as our experience at the small factory with which lam connected. We have, as far as possible, substituted girl labour on work- formerly done by youths. In doing so, we. worked two sets of girls, one set starting at, 6 a.m. and finishing at 1 p.m., and the second set starting at 1 p.m. and working until 8 p.lO. By this arrangement we were just about able to get as good an output as when we had youths working from 6 a.m. to 5.30 p.m.; the girls hod nice, easy hours (about 37 a week); we era- ployed more girls; and we were releasing eligibles for the Army or for Government work.. The Factory Inspector, however, made us droP the two shifts method. The result was our out- put was cut down by three hours a day; we had to discharge one set of girls; the remaining girls work four hours a day longer than they did formerly (and the regulations we were fringing were framed for the protection 0I female workers); and we are not now able to execute, all the orders we had on hand for an article previously imported from Germany. This kind of thing, as an instance, points to the necessity of traders having some assurance that, temporarily, of course, the ordinary re- strictions will be made more adjustable, and' that the officials concerned will work in such s way as to assist traders to make their rearrange- ments work as efficiently as possible. J. S. TILLING.

Bewsey-street, Warrington, .19th May, 1915.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0218.txt | included=true | included =====
RECRUITING AND EARLIER

t :LOSING.

Sir,—No doubt a report of the meeting of retailers and distributors convened by the Mayor, and held on Tuesday night, will appear in your columns; but I should be grateful if YOU will allow me an opportunity or emphasising one of the difficulties of replacing male bY female labour in shops touched upon by two speakers at the meeting, viz., the unduly long hours.

These hours are very trying, and the source of much unrest among ,the trained assistants, and must prove more so to the untrained hafl whom it is proposed to introduce. In manY large towns retailers have combined and agreed on earlier closing during the war, as they find it impossible to obtain assistants owing to the long, hours, and from many quarters we hear 01 assistants who have joined His Majesty's Fore& declare they will never return to the trying oen- ditions and late hours of shop life. Long before the war tradespeople found an increasing diffi- culty in getting suitable assistants for this very reason. Young people naturally prefer going te the works and offices because of the shorter hours there. The great purchasing public are getting used' , to the unusual conditions of these abnornin' times, and I feel sure we may rely on theul doing what they can by shopping earlier t° relieve a worthy class of people whose present hours leave no time for recreation. But more than this is necessary. Cannot some influence be brought to bear on the Markets Committee to close the Markets earlier? The shops wilt gladly follow suit. I hope your columns will be open to more letters on this subject, an" that some definite action may be taken ill Consequence. RETAILER..

RURAL ROUTlNE.—Continued.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915.

THE SHOP ASSISTANTS' CHANCE.

E implorers Pledge Themselves to Help Recruiting.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0221.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERY AVAILABLE lIMAII TO BE RELEASED.

That this meeting, representing the wholesale and retail distribution trades of Warrington, pledges itself to do everything in its power to help recruiting, and will, so far as possible, release every available man to serve with the colours and reinstate him on his return.

The resolution quoted above was unanimously passed at a conference of representatives of the wholesale and retail distributing trades, held in the Council Chamber of the ToAkn Hall on Tues- day evening.. The meeting, at the request of Lord Kitchener and the Home Office, was called by the .11fayor (Alderman Peter Peacock), • and it was addressed by Mr. F. W. Goldstone, M.P., a Labour member, and One of the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee, who 'made a compelling appeal, in the course of which he emphasisell the fact that, in the .matter of getting recruits, "the kind of cempiasied we want is the benevo- lent compulsion of a. generous encouragement." In addition to the Mayor and the principal speaker there were present: Aldermen John Evans, A. Bennett and D. Tinnion, Colonel Meggridge, Lieutenant-Colonel Mocatta, Major Pole, Lieutenant Williamson, Mr. A. T. Halla- way •(deputy town clerk), Mr. T. H. Spinks (assistant town clerk), the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls), Mr. J. M. Murray (director of education), and the following representatives of local firms: Warrington Co-operative Society, Mr: J. Jarman; J. Banks_ (outfitter), Mr.' J. Banks; W. Hodgkinson, Ltd., Messrs. W. and A. Hodgkinson ; _Mr. T. Domville, pawnbroker; Warrington Rural District Council, Mr. B. S. Newcombe; Carter and Son, Ltd., Mr. Stanley Carter; Henry Milling and Co., -Ltd., Mr. Henry Milling; J. Critchley (tailor), Mr. James Critch- ley ; Mr. E. A.-Sanders, Liberal agent; J. Povey and Son, •Ltd., - Mr. James Povey ; Edwin Richardson and Son, Ltd., Mr. John E. Richard- son. and Mr. John Jackson; Tilling, Gray and Co., Ltd., Mr. J. S. Tilling; Mr. Jas. Bate, fruit merchant;. Mr. George C. Miller, fruit mer- chant; S. N. Green and Co., Ltd., Mr. Sam N. Green; Greenall, Whitley and Co., Mr. Joseph H. Clarke; -Mr. Thomas Partridge, imperial Hotel; Mr. Harry Rogers, Friars' Gate; J. B. Isaac and Co., Mr. B. Bennett and .Mr. A. Pendlebury ; G. Davey-Smith, King-street, Mr. G. Davey Smith; the Home and Colonial Stores, Ltd., Mr. W. Bew ; Public Benefit Boot. Co., Mr. A. Price; Stead and Simpson, Ltd., Mr. T. H. Tretwell ; A. Mynekyme, Mr. F. J. Myne-. kyme ; J. Nelson and Son, Mr. P. S. Ward; and Shop Assistants' -Union (Warrington Branch), Mr. E. Thomas.

COUNTRY CALLING LOUDLY FOR MEN. By way of prelnde to the meeting, the Mayor said that, whilst he did not think that any particular coullflaint could be laid at the door of. any trade, the War Office and the Country were calling loudly for More men, and he was afraid that the call would not be stopped until some additional power was given ,to the country, or until the war was settled. The circular calling the meeting had been addressed to the shopkeepers and business gentlemen who were in the distributing trade, and he felt sure that whatever effort they could put forth in reference t,o this great war they would do so cheerfully, as WarringtonianS always did. The appeal would not fall on deaf ears, and, at any cost to themselves, they would do what they could to help on the recruiting Iflove7 ment, a matter in which the distributing trades of Warrington would not be behind .the .dis- tributing trades in any other place. (Hear, hear, and applause.)

300,000 MORE MEN WANTED—AT ONCE! Mr. Goldstone prefaced a lengthy address by saying that those present were already con- vinced of the greatness of the issue, and in. order to carry the war through, as soon as pos- sible, to a completely successful termination, the Government, acting through the various departments, were attempting to raise more men. Only that •day Lord Kitchener, in the House of Lords, had made an appeal for 300,000 more men, in addition to those who had already enlisted. It was hoped td get them by taking stock of the existing trades and indus- tries and, thus finding out the Sources of -un- tapped material.. .If that attempt failed, it seemed to him that no matter what their views might be, from an academic standpoint, respecting voluntaryism or conscription, the main issue would be borne in upon them, and that was that they had to win this war at all costs, and although, perhaps, .the majority of those present: hoped that the necessary men would be forthcoming voluntary, he thought all of them were prepared to face the sinking of individual views as to the method of raising men if it were a question of the issue hanging in the ,balance. At Present the view of the Government seemed to' be that the various trades and industries should be approached through local channels to. find .out what men there were who were still available and who, by taking the 'proper steps, might become members of his Majesty's forces. •

face a little. less celevity than the shop assist- ants had been accustomed to give; it was the sacrifice that the customer was called upon to make! The feeling of the women in this coun- try was so keen about the war that those who Offered their services were fully determined so to qualify themselves that, to the best of their ability, the labour they could give would be no whit behind that of the men whom they re-- leased. The alternative, and- to some extent the corollary, was the employment of men of non-military age; and he thought they had got to the stage where, when a man of military ago went to the Front, they were justified in imlvertising for another of non-military age. So they might centre their thoughts around the possibility of releasing men and the method whereby the places of the men .could be filled. The kind of compulsion flint was wanted in the present case was the benevolent compulsion of a generous encouragement; not, at present, the direct compulsion of telling a man that be must gb, -and not even going to the extent of enter- ing his name on the list which employers would be asked to fill in—a list on which they would be required to state the number of men of military age Whom they still employed and the number of those whom they estimated could be released.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0222.txt | included=true | included =====
Trade That Has Done Its Share.

He hoped those present would not assume that because they were called together that evening that there was any intention of suggesting that the distributing and retail trades had not done their duty. So far froth that being the case, shop assistants, commercial travellers, clerks and warehousemen had, probably, done so, well that no other trade had surpassed them. No fewer than 260,000 shop assistants had enlisted —“cheers)—but it was estimated that 220,000 of military age still remained, eligible or outside, or, possibly, were so tied up that they could not be spared. It was estimated that some- thing like 430,000 men from the particular trade- to which he referred—shop assistants, warehousemen, clerks and the rest—had enlisted, leaving behind an estimated number of 360,000. ~The problem resolved itself into this: Could they by taking thought and counsl together absolve a considerable portion of those Men from their ordinary duties, so .that they might have the opportunity of serving with the Forces? The _question which faced them that

night was coula they do anything to re-arrange their various businesses so as to free a con- siderable number of men? What could be done? So. far as part of the retail trade was concerned they could recruit a coniderable number of women to take the places of the men who were at present employed by them. To do that the. Board of Trade, through the Labour Exchanges, were 'compiling .tt register of women who were prepared to take industrial duty so that the men Might be free for the pur- poses of the War.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0223.txt | included=true | included =====
Female Labour Still To Be Utilised.

In this connection he believed that, if active efforts were made now, the short list already_ in existence at the Labour Exchange would be very considerably lengthened; for there was A considerable body of female labour still to be utilised. - In this time of the country's need, the speaker contitined, Customers must be pre- pared, and, he believed, would be prepared, to

===== 0002601_19150522_art0226.txt | included=true | included =====
Patriotic Shop Assistants.

They would be asked whether they would re- quire substitutes—women, or men of non-mili- tary age—and also whether they were willing to guarantee reinstatement, if it was desired, to enlisted men? It had come to the knowledge of the Parliamentary Recruiting Committee, from the, householders' returns, that no less than 22,000 shop assistants had expressed their wil- lingness to enlist if a guarantee could be given them of reinstatement after the -sar was over. Ile suggested to those present that possibly they could help to. the extent of giving that guarantee. and if they did so they would relieve the difficulty of the position for a number of men who were not slackers, but who were, rather, looking at ,the responsibilities they had at home, and who were just wondering—and lie could not blame Them—what was to happen to those totally dependent upon .them if they came back to find the place they used to fill was filled by someone else.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0227.txt | included=true | included =====
Sacrifice Demanded from Everyone.

He thought every one of them was convinced of the rightness of the. British cause; every one was deeper in earnest to sacrifice everything that they could, so that the country might go through and emerge successfully from the war, not only from the point pf view of national interests, but also from the standpoint of a higher interest—the interest of the preservation for the world of civilisation as against the brute force which the enemy were exhibiting in the most callous forms.. If that were their inten- tion, it seemed to him that each one of them had to face some amount of sacrifice in excess of that already faced, and, the sacrifice which might be called for from those in the particular trade to which they belonged might very well be that of so reorganising their forces- as ,to free that. fraction of the 360,1)00 men that was required, and that was represented in War- rington. (Applause.) Mr. J. E. Richardson said it had been sug- gested that female--guards might take the place of male guards on the tramways, but the trade unions had said " No," and had refused to work under , such conditions. Might not the same thing obtain with the shopkeepers if they adopted a similar course? Mr. E. Thomas of .the Shop Assistants' Union, said that &his 'Union ,would have no objection whatever on the question of female labour, provided the woman was paid three- quarters of the wage received by the man, whose, place she was taking, and that. the men returned to their places after the war.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0228.txt | included=true | included =====
AN ECONOMIC DIFFICULTY.

Mr. .Jarman said that the members of. the local branch •of the Co-op Employes' Union, expressed no objection whatever to replacing men by women, and no stipulation had been Made" with regard ..to wages. • Perhaps, the reason for that was that the Co-oP Society already had .a scale in operation for .female labour, but though he was positive that there was no rate for women higher that the Co- operative Society's present rate, it would not work Out at three-fourths of a man's wages, be- cause a man of 21 in the employment of the Co- operative Society received 28s. a week, whilst the rate for a girl of 20 was 17s. per week.. It was a- case in which there should be give and take, and he believed that, for 'a time at any rate, it would be necessary to replace one man with two women. In that event it would mean a loss instead of gain to the employer, so far as wages were concerned, because they knew that much of the work could not be done as readily by women as by men. If they paid two women a guinea each per week to replace a man who had been earning 28s: per week, business could not go .u.n. Tie suggested that the matter should, within reasonable limits, be left for the shopkeepers to arrange, but a minimum should perhaps be fixed.

- Mt. 11. Milling said that if the shopping hours were shortened it would be easier for the trades- people to release more men for military service.

THE DILATORY SHOPPER.

Air. Jarman: What you want is a town's meet- ing that will get hold of the people who will come late shopping and tell them just exactly what you think of them. Our hours at the Co- op Stoges are less than anybody's in town, hut there are people who will conic in just as the door is closing.

Mr. J. Critchley said that there were posi- tions in the shops and warehouses which ,no woman could take up, and in that case the only alternative would be the employment of Men who were over military age. Mr— Tilling said that with -regard to the employment of women, the traders would want Some guarantee that they would not be " hum- bugged about." by factory inspectors. lie knew of cases in which girls had taken the place of Youths and the working liJurs had had to be reduced three hours per. day. 4 A questioner. asked Mr. Goldstone what objection he had to women being paid full wages if they did the work of a man? -

===== 0002601_19150522_art0229.txt | included=true | included =====
A DIFFICULTY THAT OCCURS.

Mr. Goldstone replied that be, personally, had no objection. On the other hand he would like to see them so paid, but he was looking at it from the point of view of the men to whom he was appealing. "If," he added, "- they have to pay women at the same rate as men, and at the seine time grant the pen 'something to eke out the, separation allowance, it may make all the difference between being able to carry on or having to close up. I want to see them carry on. No one can .feel more acutely than I do the desirability of safeguarding the trade union position, but I have not yet heard of an employer who is seek;ng permanently to alter the • standard Which has beep attained. For the moment we have to approach the prob- lem from the standpoint of national security, while obtaining Incidentally the best conditions for the men who go." As to the suggestion re the earlier closing of the markets, Mr. Gold- stone added that he believed that, if the diffi- culty were at all widespread, representations to the Government would be sympathetically con- sidered.

DIFFICULTY OF SAYING " YOU MUST GO OR GET OUT."

Mr. Jarman then proposed the resolution already, quoted and, in doing so, said that a number of the employes of the Co-operative Society had enlisted, but he ciould say, without hesitation, that a number of others might do so if they chose. The meeting could well under- stand the position of a large employer who Was not prepared to say to his men "You must go or get out." The fact that the firms would now have ,to supply: figures as to eligible men Might help the Government in taking sonic 'steps in the matter which they would otherwise not see their way to take. The Co-operative Society undertook that any eMploye Who joined the Forces should, on his return, receive at least the same rate Of wages he bad previously

been paid, although they could not guarantee that he should have._exactly the same Position. They were prepared to do all they possibly could with women labour if they could- not

ibtain men over military age. There might .be i,ome difficulty in adjusting the question of A-ages, but he did not think it would be in- aiperable. The Society also made up the

difference between tile Government allowance and the wages of employes who had dependents.

OLD MEN IN KHAKI-SLACKERS STILL WEARING "CIVVY'S" CLOTHES.

Mr. Richardson seconded, and, in doing so, said that if he had any difficulty in the matter at all, it was the feeling he had at the back of his mind that the object of the meeting was that the screw should be put on by the employer, while the nation still adhered to the voluntary system. Though he was disappointed with the response made by, the young men at his own establishment, lie did not feel that lie wanted. to call them up and appeal to them a second time. The position was such that it ought to appeal to them without anything being done by the employer. If there was one thing that made him sad, it was to see men approaching 45 or 50 years of fine marching through the streets in khaki, when lie knew of the number Of "slackers" about the town. He had a, feel- ing that the Government were indirectly ask- ing employers to do something which they them- selves had failed to do—to tell the young men that they must go. II is firm, in filling vacan- cies, would ask' that the men engaged should be over the military age. Mr. Richardson added that if the employers had to employ two women to take the place of every man and pay them on the hard and fast lines mentioned by the Shop Assistants' Union, it would be driving a hail. into the coffin of the proposals made that night, because it would make the carrying on of busi- ness an absolute, impossibility. The proposition- was then put and carried unanimously.

-WHERE THE BEST FIRMS GO FOR HANDS.

The Mayor, in reply to a remark which had ,een made by Mr. Hiehardson—to the effect that

when he asked .a lady who was desirous of working whether she had registered her name at the Labour Exchange; he got the reply " No! None of the good firms go there !"—said : "You can take it from Me that that lady evi- dently did not know anything about it. . The best firms do go there and get satisfied every day."

UNUSUAL BUSINESS-NOT "BUSINESS AS USUAL."

Colonel Moggridge proposed a, vote .of thanks to the Mayor and to Mr. Goldstone, and in doing so said that he had been busy for nine: months trying—and succeeding very well—to get men to enlist. The one great bar to securing a greater response seemed to him to be the cry that was started at the beginning of the war, and which was still . heard, "Business as usual." He • did not see how anybody could expect business to be as usual in these abso- lutely abnormal times. That cry had pre- vented people understanding. as they should the very critical times through which we were passing. People were -trying to carry on as usual, and it could not be done. He was Cer- tain those present Would help in every way to substitute female, for male labour. It was only temporary measure; it was a thing which he did not suppose would ever be asked again. A very large number of shop assistants were of the right age and physically' fit, and the mili- tary 'authorities Would like them to come in. Alderman Tinnion, in seconding, referred to the great interest the Mayor had taken in re- cruiting work, and added that he felt sure that the object the .Government had in view, would be attained so far as Warrington was concerned. The proposition was carried with applause.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0232.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GOVERNMENT'S INTENTION.

In reply Mr. Goldstone said that so far as he was coOerned there was no suggestion that employers should apply the screw. He gathered . .

that the intention- Of. the Government- was to exhaust all possible means of preserving the voluntary system. If the efforts in the various trades and industries failed to bring the men who were absolutely necessary, it seemed to him that no Government could afford to go on unless they were prepared to take the utmost means to get the men required to see the war through to the end they all desired. (Applause.) , The Mayor also expressed his thanks, saying that he felt that at a time like that they could not do enough, and adding that if ever he could do anything to help recruiting, and assist the Government in the hour of trial he felt that he ought to do it, and that he deserved no thanks for doing it.

WILL U.S.A. REMAIN ON THE FENCE?

Warringlonian's Views On America's

\Vat. Allitude.

' Mr. William Broadhurst, of 77, Priest- ley-street, Warrington, • has received an interesting letter from his son-in-law, Mr. R. Purslow, who, with his wife, is at present residing at Rokbury, Alass.,.and who describes the effect of the war on American trade, and also throws some sidelights on (minion in the U.S.A. " Work here," writes Mr. Purslow, is very bad, there being scarcely anything doing I should like you to see some of the cartoons of the Kaiser published in the American Press. . . One 9f our judges remarked that he would ,like to see a dozen bombs fall on the head of the Kaiser and his advisers._

===== 0002601_19150522_art0234.txt | included=true | included =====
Germans' Petition to President.

The Germans here went nearly mad over the judge's remark and petitioned the President to remove the offending justice from the Bench. Fancy removing a judge just to please a handful of Huns. lam glad to say that Woodrow Wilson told the Germans that he could not listen to them. . The less England tampers with American trade the bet- ter it will be for them over here. The real Americans are all right, but the Hibernians, the Germans and the Austrians have a lot of influence, and all over this great country they are working and planning to.bff-set England's plans."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0235.txt | included=true | included =====
How the Huns' Submarines Get Their

Information.

The writer goes on to refer to the recent visit of a German cruiser to Newport News, and opines that the object of the visit was to get in touch with German agents in the States, with a view to arranging for the export of coal and provisions, and also to ascertain the time and date of sailing of any. English or French ships, and to discover the precise courses they were to take, in order that they might communicate the information to the three German commerce 'raiders who were scouring the Atlantic. "The same sort of thing I am sure," the writer adds, "is taking place round the British Isles, the work of ascertain- ing the information &c. necessary being obtained by Germans from America, who speak good English, and pass as either Englishmen or Irishmen, and who pose. as fishermen, but are in reality runners for the German sub- marines, letting them .know what boats are leaving Liverpool, Queenstown, &c. .. . lam really afraid you people over there are about a hundred years behind America and Germany in some respects.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0236.txt | included=true | included =====
America Peculiarly Situated.

"Bothithese countries can run you off the face of the earth in the matter of electrical equip- meats, and they are always trying to improve on them. . . You think America will soon be involved in the war. I do not think so. America is peculiarly fixed and there are many 'big reasons why she should not implicate herself but should remain strictly neutral, . • . Work 16 very poor here. There are thousands of men loafing around starving. . . . (hood luck to Our gallant boys at the Front, and bad luck to the crack-brained Kaiser, who I hope soon to see at St. Helena."

BOOKS AND MAGAZINES.

Life and Writings of Alfred-Lord Tennyson. By Arthur Turnbo. Creat writers' series. Critical studies of the world's - greatest authors. London: The Walter Scott Publishing Co., Ltd. is. net.

The author of this little book sets out for the. reader an admirable epitome of the _

several 'lives of the great Laureate of the Vic- torian era, written by Hallam, the present Lord Tennyson, and other distinguished authors—the leading facts in the career of the poet gathered from these authentic sources, being set forth concisely and in an orderly arrangement, which for the student furnishes a comprehensive history of the various stages of development by which the apostle of poetic culture attained the height of his fame.

Mr. Turnbull also gives many excerpts from well-known writers who have criticised the poet's style and methods, and binds the whole :together with comments of his own on many of the principal poems. - There is a copious index, and a chronology of the poems. The get-up of the book does great credit to the publishers.

Man and Woman. By Havelock Ellis.

London: The Walter Scott Publishing Co.; ___

Ltd. 6s. net

This is a new edition of Mr. Havelock Ellis's well-known work. The book is revised and greatly enlarged. In it the author gives us the results of many years' research into the differences between the sexes, disclosing facts of immense social import. Reports of inveS- tigations made in various countries by eminent scientists throw light on many ques- tions much to the fore lately. Peculiarly in- teresting are the results of investigations made. with a view to discovering the mental and physical differences between boys and girls of various ages.

Mr. Havelock Ellis quickly disposes of dis- cussion on the " alleged inferiority of women " by pointing out the futility of such !discussion, and he rightly declines to bar the way of future progress by pre-conceived notions of the fitness of women for particular spheres of activity. " We are not at liberty," he ;says, " to introduce any artificial sexual barrier into social concerns. The respective fitness of men and of women for any kind of work or any kind of privilege\ can only be ascertained by actual open experiment; and as the 'conditions for such experiment are never twice the same, it can never be posi- tively affirmed that anything has been settled. once and for all. When such experiment is successful, so much the•better for the race; when it is unsuccessful, the minority who have broken natural law alone suffer. An exaggerated anxiety lest natural law be over- thrown is misplaced. The world is not so in- securely poised. We may preserve an atti- tude of entire equanimity in the face of social readjustment. Such readjustment is either the outcome of wholesome natural instinct, in which case our social structure will be strengthened and broadened, or it is not ;. and if not, it is unlikely to become organically in- grained in the species." _

The book mas- be 'cordially commended to all who are seriously interested in the social problems of our times. It is a mine Of in- formation bearing on these great questions, presented in that calm and judicial manner it is the special pride of the scientist to Pre- serve amid the conflicts of jarxing factions.

GREECE AND THE ENTENTE POWERS.

Of pro-German feeling in Greece there is practically none. The case of the 'Allies has been well presented, and from the 'senti- mental point of view the nation is without doubt, in favour of England and France. Turkey is the hereditary enemy, and the. thrust of Austria towards Salonika has long been a menace. All that caq be said on the other side is that to Germany itself there is no. actual ill-feeling, and that some personal gratitude is felt to the Kaiser for the part he played in crying " Han& off" to Austria at the end of the second Balkan War. It is when we come to the question of self-interest that ,things are not so simple. Greece has already passed through two trying struggles within less than three years. One of its principal industries is its growing mercantile marine, with a tonnage of about 400,000. With Austria put out of action, and English ttnd French steamers largely used for war purposes, the carrying trade of the Mediter- ranean is passing into the hands of Italy and Greece. It is a temptation for the Greek commercial classes to ,welcome this chance of recouping themselves for the sacrifices 9f the Balkan Wars.—Professor Ronald. Burrows in the " Contemporary Review " for May.

WELSH WOMEN ROUSED.

- An interesting interview with. Mrs. Lloyd George appears in " Great Thoughts" for May (Smith's Publishing Co., 6d.)—the first number of a new volume. Speaking of Welsh women and the war the wife of the Chancel- lor says: "The treatment of Belgium by the Germans has roused the Welsh women indeed. Then, too, they are not now afraid of being left a burden to others. or the parish, as they were at The start of the war. The Govern- men't's generous arrangements have freed them from that fear. In some villages I know, where men at first hung back, after the women saw what good money came regu-: larly to the families of the few who had gone, when they grasped that the wives and chil- dren left behind were actually better off, financially; than. they themselves whose men- •folk were still working at home these women soon began—it makes me laugh to think of it, but we Welsh people do like such a good joke !—these women soon began to urge their

own husbands and sons to join the Forces too! And to-day there is hardly a man left in those villages who could possibly be accepted a- soldier, sailor, or combatant of some kind!" Discussingi the drink problem Mrs. Lloyd George says: " I am not sure that restric- tion of hours of sale will do much to solve the problem. lam told by good authorities such a course only means that those who want drink will buy it in bottles, so as to have it when the ' pubs ' are closed. lam more in- clined to suPport lessening• by far the num- ber of licensed houses. A man or woman will not generally walk miles just to get a drink."

WHY NOT ADVERTISE CYCLING?

• The glory of the open road is realised by every wheelman and wheelwoman.. But. the advantages of club cycling are not so aliparent. There is a great opportunity either to be taken or left, by cycling clubs throughout the country, after the war is Over. The thousands of men who have for the first time learnt to appreciate the real pleasure of open-air life, and who, will return to this country then, should be encouraged to join some club, so that they will have an opportunity of keeping up to a certain extent, the good they have received from living in the 'open. The pastime of cycling does not advertise itself. Why not? is a common question asked. I believe it would be productive of much good, if, when the war is finished, all clubs would advertise the fact that they exist, 'so as to -draw into the net those whom they want to benefit..._ Those who will return to their former occupations will suffer if they do not continue to find out of door exercise and recreation. The: only way to get hold of the greater 'number of these fellows is to advertise for them. •

===== 0002601_19150522_art0246.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BY-WAYS.

For many years' past, but particularly during the last three or four seasons, I have been addicted to traversing the by-ways rather than the highways. This coming season will, I know, but strengthen me in my resolve to leave the main roads severely alone, as- far as possible, and take to the capillary roads with which this country abounds. I have two,. objections to find with the principal thorough- fares. In the first place, they are overrun by motor-cyclists and motor-cars; in the second the surfaces are far from good. I have suc- ceeded in the last two years in getting the secre- tary of the club to which I belong to plan his fixture list, so as to include the less frequented tracks, and I notice. from the published lists of various clubs in London, that they are now following our lead. The 1)Y-roads very often run parallel to the main roads, but they take in villages and hamlets which are teeming with inthest and delightfully old-worldish. Nearly always they are missed by the average rider. To do a hundred miles a day is nothing; to enjoy every moment spent awheel is the main object for all cyclists. • Try the side tracks and judge for yourself. THE LAW AGAINST US.

We are generally told that the laws are made for the benefit of the majority. This may be true in certain directions, but it is not true in all. A case was recently brought to the Courts by a cyclist who was badly bitten by a a dog. Every rider runs the risk of being in-- jured in the same way. It is not only the vicious beasts that bite, but playful and stupid dogs very often attempt to do the same. The law on the subject is to the effect that unless the injured person is able to prove that the owner was aware of the dog's propensity to bite he has no redress. The action for damages was eventually settled out of Court. The cyclist had had to endure pain and suffer monetary loss, and, if it had not been for, the generosity of the dog owner, he would 'undoubtedly have received no. compensation. The owner, however, was generous and he gave the cyclist £5. This is only one instance of the law being

TRUTH ABOUT RUSSIAN

PROHIBITION.

The exact nature and extent of the liquor prohibition in Russia is not, it seems, quite understood in this country. Many conflicting statements have appeared recently in the Press, some to the effect that the prohibition in Russia concerns only vodka, the. national Rus- sian spirit, and does not ban Wines and beers, while other statements have implied that the new law extends only to the rural districts, the towns and cities being free from any re- strictions.

In view of these misconceptions it is .inter- esting to note a leading article in the "- Outro RoSsii," of Moscow, which deals with the sub- ject and states clearly the real position. The article, refers to a recent semi-official confer- ence in Petrograd, at which it was decided, to recommend the Government to allow the sale of beers and light wines. "This resolution," says the Moscow journal, "created a widespread anxiety which has now been allayed by the Tsar himself. On hearing of' the threatened re-opening of the beer houses the Temperance Society of Nizhni-Novgorod sent up, through its president, the Grand Duke Constantine Constantinovitch, an urgent peti- tion to the Tsar, in which he was urged not to abate the beneficial law of prohibition by allowing the sale of beers and wines. ~ "At the foot of the telegrani handed to him by the Grand Duke the Tsar inscribed with his own hand the following words: " There is no ground for anxiety. As long as the war lasts wines and beers will not be sold gener- ally." •

The last word seenis.to leave •a loophole, a s it might imply that beers and wines may be allowed to be sold locally in the near future, but it is .quite clear that at present strict prohibition extending to all districts and all forms of alcohol is in force throughout Russia, as it has been for the past nine months.

SECOND-HAND MOTOR-CYCLES.

There appears to be a very good demand this season for second-hand motor-cycles, and a large number of machines are changing hands. It is difficult for those who have never owned such a mount before to be able to judge the value and know how much should be paid. AN IMPROVEMENT WANTED.

The majority of motor-cyclists agree that one improvement that is badly wanted on their machines is better rear-springing. I have had a number of letters recently. On this subject, the writer in each case mentioning this point. One correspondent . says, "My 1915 machine would be ideal if instead of being rigid the rear Was sprung." 'lt is a subject that has, entered the minds of many riders in the past, but evidently the awful condition of our principal roads has made their drawback more apparent this. season. Pot-holes, instead of being an occasional nuisance, are now a continual night- mare. One difficulty that. manufacturers 'are up against in springing the rear of the cycle to Make a satisfactory and economical job of it, is that such springing must require little or no running attention, and must be free from wear. To design a suitable spring frame embodying these .features is far from easy. If we are patient, however, I am eonvincedt a practical form of frame will be built some ddy.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0249.txt | included=true | included =====
PETROL-PRODUCING PROCESSES.

, The problem of increasing the world's fuel supply is daily becoming a matter of more importance. The withqawal of horses, •on account of the war, has compelled the indus- trial community to take to motor traction at an earlier date than was expected. This has increased ,the demand. Again, the war calls for a tremendous quantity, a demand which must be supplied first of all. Chemists and mining engineers all over the .world are work- ing hard to discover new and cheap sources from which motor spirit may lib obtained. A number of new processes. have been discovered, but the difficulty in time past has been to pass the laboratory stage and to place production on a commercial basis: It is a big step between the two. An important question is that of re- ducing co-st. From what I can gather the main object is to treat the raw material by improved processes, so that it will yield -a greater quan- tity of motor spirit. One new" process is that by which it is possible to obtain petrol from synthetic crude oil. Paraffin, vaseline, rodwax and other natural hydrocarbons are reconverted into. crude oil and the, spirit obtained there- from. The Rittman cracking process is, claimed to give a larger percentage of spirit froms petroleum than , obtainable before. Cheaper petrol is a thing which appeals to all of us.

CHEAPER CARS. ,

People who buy cars want them to last as many seasons as possible. 31a nufacturers have therefore kept this point in view in designing their engines, chassis and body- work. They have not accepted the •Ameri- can idea that "just good enough" is good enough for them. 'They, have erred to a cer- tain extent on the other side. They have devoted their skill 'hi the pyoduction of a limited number of light cars f tip-top quality. Designers would do well to remember one thing, namely, that flue demand to-day is from the million who want a car at a reasonable price, which is trustworthy and built to last. These are directions :in which the cast of pro- duction could be reduced, thus extending the market. The all-British car to sell at ..1100 is still a thing of the future. We can not arrive at it all at once, but makers can take a step in the right direction. This is all the more important now, since the cost of labour and material has increased, and the tendency is for the price of the finished :produce to rise. This increase in price will still farther restrict the Market.

Tijurrli Arius.

SINS OF THE YOUNG MAN.

(Attire Instead Of Cricket ; Books Instead Of 'Bacca.

The sins of the young man were cried from the pulpit of Cairo-street Church on Sunday evening. .He spends his money on tobacco -in- stead of books; he talks cricket instead of cultivating his mind; he goes to. picture palaces when he would be better employed at night school; and he grows up a dissatisfied cynic; so at any rate, says the Rev. Chas. Roper, :of West Kirby, who preached the Sunday school anniversary sermons. He contended that it should be the great aim of the teacher and the parent to alter this state of affairs by influenc- ing the children to take a delight in culture for its own sake.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0258.txt | included=true | included =====
Education and Religion.

Taking as his text: " The fear of the Lord is the beginning - of knowledge: but fools despise wisdom and instruction." (Proverbs, i., 7). • The rev, gentleman, at the outset, pointed to /the importance of education in religion. True religion was true life. The man who claimed to be religious muSt show himself to be a good man, and the man whose intellectual pavers were developed according to the. opportunities that came his way was better equipped to lead a good life than him whose education had been neglected. Education helped to create a more enobling conception of God and human duty. The advance of civilisation had been propor- tionate to the advance in human knowledge. The reverend gentleman went on to deplore the smallness of the amount spent by our nation on education. We were, he alleged, being beaten commercially and industrially, by nations that valued education more highly than we did.. .

===== 0002601_19150522_art0259.txt | included=true | included =====
An Army of Grumblers.

The majority of the young men of seventeen and . eighteen who were educated at our colleges and secondary schools thought far too much of cricket and football and far too little of intellectual pursuits. And when they at- tained to man's estate they formed a large army of grumblers. They stood in the way of truth and progress, and took no interest in any of the things that mattered. They denied that there was a good God ruling in the universe, just because He hadn't strewn their particular paths .with roses—because He hadn't suspended the time-old law that whatsoever a man sows he will reap. If the young man would spend at night school the time he spent now in picture palaces; if he would spend on books the money he now spent on tobacco and cigarettes, it would be far better. The modern young man seemed to care for nothing so long as he had enough ,money to keep himself smartly dressed and indulge in the empty pleasures alluded to.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0260.txt | included=true | included =====
What Teachers and Parents Can Do.

It should be the great aim of tho teacher and the parent to help the children to steer clear of becoming cynical, by warning them of the sophistries of the world and "against its deceptions. In the long run, the educated man-- and he was using the word in its widest sense -- had the best of life, even from a materialistic .point of view. Parents and 'teachers should do their utmost to inculcate into children a love of education for its own sake. in this way, so much could be done to make the rising genera- tion better than the present.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0261.txt | included=true | included =====
Good Attendances.

In the afternoon an address was given by the Rev, T. T. James, of the -Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church.. All three services were well attended, and good collections- were taken for the Sunday school funds. The beautiful singing of the children was a prominent feature of the gatherings. Amongst the music rendered in the afternoon were " The Rose Is Queen," God Bless The Little Chil- .dren " (composed by the organist, Mr. .3. M. Muir), "Evening Song" and a solo and chorus "Children's Voices" (solo by Mrs. F. Monks). In the morning the choir gave "There's a Friend For Little Children" (Watson), solo by Miss Mellor. At the evening service the anthem "Teach Me, 0 Lore" (Attwood) was rendered.

THE MALEDICTORY PSALMS.

Vicar Of Buriontiood Thinks They Might Be "Sub-Ediied."

In the current issue of his Parish Magazine the Vicar (Rev. A. M. Mitchell) writes of "Church Maledictions," and asks: "What im- pression is left in the minds of thoughtful and devout worshippers by such lines these: Let them be as dust before the wind. Let their way be dark and slippery, and let the Angel of the Lord -persecute them. Let a sudden destruction come upon him un- awares. Let his day's be few. Let Satan stand at his right hand. Let them fall from one wickedness to another. Let them be wiped out of the book of the living. When he shall be judged let him be condemned. Let his children be fatherless, and his wife a widow. Let his children be vagabonds and beg their bread. Let there be no man to pity him. ..

The Vicar adds: "How far these particular psalms, or part psalms, are responsible for the harsh, vindictive, unforgiving spirit of so many nominal Christians, is an interesting but inex- plicable point. The effect cannot be helpful; to the thoughtful, it must be harmful, bow harm- ful who can say? Imperceptibly, as the result of familiarity through constant repetition of the imprecations of psalms, the Mind may be permeated with the spirit of malediction.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0263.txt | included=true | included =====
Plea for More Judicious Use of the Psalter.

"The revision of the psalter is not in question, but a-more judicious use of it in public wor- ship is called for. The imprecatory portions may, with much advantage every way, be de- leted from Morning and Evening Prayer. Such deletion would afford intense satisfaction to mul- titudes of simple folk . . . who are pained by being expected to join in the maledictions of ages past—a time when man was still much of the savage and almost whblly.- ignorant of civil- isation and culture."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0264.txt | included=true | included =====
"DEEPEST DEPTHS OF DIABOLISM."

'Vicar Of Burtonwood On "Incarnate

Fiends " From Germany.

Writing under the heading "The Devils!" in the current issue of his Parish Magazine, the Vicar of Burtonwood (the 'Rev. A. M. Mitchell) says:— "That, contrary to all usages of civilised war- fare and in spite of pledges recently given, asphyxiating gas has been used by our German foes in the long-drawn and still raging battle round Ypres, there is now not the shadow of a doubt. The doers of a deed of this kind, what• ever they may style themselves, Chriwitians or Mahomedans, Free-thinkers or Atheists, are not men but fiends incarnate. Let them hence- forth be known and called by the nanle they have so fitly earned by their malignity—the devils. Devils, we presume, can IN in.carnato; they can take to themselves the likehtess of men: Diabolism assumes many forms, resorts to many devices, practises many enormities, but .it is difficult to imagine a deeper depth of diabolism than the use of poisonous gas for the overthrow and destruction of an unsus- pecting and honourable foe. The Blood of the Britisher Fired.

" Alas! this does not stand alone; the cruel, vindictive, outrageous treatment of British prisoners in German hands is proved on un- deniable evidence to be only too true—too pain- fully true. The blood of every Britisher is up, is at boiling villa: the incarnate fiends, the devils who do these crowning deeds of devilry will pay dearly for their infamy. Sinch cruel. diabolical deeds must be accounted for and reckoned with. In the face of such doings neutrality is hardly possible—" the neutrals )!

can no longer stand by never lifting voice or hand to stay such desperate, hellish methods for securing a small and temporary advantage.

" The Fumes of Hell."

"We may not retaliate—strong as the pro- vocation is we may not do to them as they ha N. e done to us, hut the penalty is sure; they who create the devilish atmosphere and let loose the fumes of hell will find in these their own cer- tain retribution.

"By the torpedoing of the Lusifania on Friday, May 7th, the deeper depth of the foul, deliberate . murder would appear to have been sounded. What further devil pranks .ea it Prussianism, in its desperation, devise for the gratification of its hellish spite and malignity ? The deeper depth than the wholesale murder of 1,409 innocent, defenceless people, some forty children among- them, must be deep in- deed—Prussianism for hate, cruelty. and bar- barity is unfathomable."

SUNDAY SCHOOL.

Vicar Of Sirelion On Its Importance.

The Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross, vicar of Stream, preaching at Holy Trinity Church, Warrington, on Sunday morning, on behalf of the Sunday schools of the parish, took for his text; "Fear not, little flock, for it is your Father's ,good pleasure to give you the king- dom." Sunday schools, he said, were the nur- series of the church; they paved the way for an earnest, useful, Christian ,life. Sunday schools appealed to. Parishioners as a whole, whose prayers and gifts should go to assist, to the best of their ability, the good work being done. All should give or their substance so that the build- ings might be furnished in a way to secure efficiency. Just as all could not fight at .the Front, all could not teach in the school; but there were very few who could not.render valu- able assistance in some way or other. The evening sermon was preached by the Rev. B. E. Taylor, curate-in-charge of All Saints', Newton-le-Willows. •

KING'S LEAD IS " SPLENDID " !

Temperance Reformers' Great Oppor- tunity, Says Burtonwood's lear.

" For the war-time, anyhow, the King has become a total abstainer," the Rev. A. M. Mitchell points out in the current issue of his Parish M agazine, and adds :—" Who, a year ago, ever dreamt of such a thing coming to pass? A king as total abstainer and asking the country to follow his example! It seems almost too wonderful to be true, but indeed it is a bright reality. The Royal lead is splendid; are we following where the King has gone before? Such a good, brave, high example is worthy our emulation; to do so is a stronger proof of loyalty. than to shout in church, on platform at meeting, God save the King!' The Cabinet is said to follow where the King has led the way. Now is the great opportunity of the Temperance reformer. 'Press the reform home. Public bodies should show their patriot- ism at " this time in this royal way. All are not too ready to do, so. 'At a recent meeting of our local Board of Guardians the suggestion to follow the King's good example was received with derisive laughter. At the same meeting another publican was added to the Assessment Committee—and yet through the Drink we may lose the war."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0267.txt | included=true | included =====
IiONA AA Ot 'LD THE APOSTLES FARE TO-DAY?

The Rev. L. J. T. Darwall, -vicar of. Walton, who preached at the evening service at St. Peter's Church on Sunday—the school anni- versary—said the slow 'growth of the Catholic Church in a land of heathenism was unlike any other story; the first spread of Christianity was a marvellous story. What would be the result now if twelve labouring men went forth from one of our country districts to preach a new religion, that sacred books must be put aside, that old traditions must be rejected, and a new god Worshipped—not to give up worship altogether, but to adopt fresh forms and a fresh object of worship? What 'would he their chance of success? Yet that was what practically happened 1,900 years ago. Everything seemed against the Apostles, yet what the world re- fused to hear the world was forced to hear, and the Gospel was established. They noticed the slow progress the Church seemed to make; they thought that religion was just holding its own, and no more; they saw that comparatively few people w.nt to church ri-gularly in proroi•- tion to the number who stayed away; but they knew that the Divine way was to carry on work by almost imperceptible progress.

SHORT SERMONS AND GABBLED

SERVICE‘;.

Rev. 11. Alitehell And Craze For

Wel ily In The Pulpit.

Asking the question as to *hether church services are too long, the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, in the current issue of his Parish Magazine, proceeds to answer it as follows:— Generally speaking it is so, Church services are too long, in the industrial and rural dis- tricts especially. A service one hour and a quarter long is amply sufficient, and should afford time for a sermon of half an hour's duration. The modern craze for short sermons is not good—it should not be encouraged. As a rule the service it is that needs curtailment— not the sermon. Short sermons and gabbled services do not tend to edification nor full con- gregations. Such is the teaching of experience. A shortened service, with more time given to teaching, would prove far more helpful to ordinary congregations, and be productive of more satisfactory results, from the standpoint of .Anglican Churchmanship. The Psalms are too long, the lessons are too long, and when there is an amalgamation of services, say Morning Prayer, followed by Holy Communion (or Ante-Communion), vain repetitions are not wanting. Why say the Lord's Prayer three times over 2 Why, again, 'recite two Creeds? Where music and singing are both deficient; when Psalms and canticles are nothing but a long-drawn drawl, elongated services become a weariness' to soul and body.

Why Not Select the Psalms? It may reasonably be asked, and is asked, " Why should congregations be expected to go through three long Psalms at any one service, just for the sake of getting through the Psalter in a month's time "Would it not be better to sing with the understanding one Psalm, or two short ones, suitable to the Sunday or the occasion, and in harmony with the Church teaching for the particular day?" The Psalms follow the days of the month, not the teachings of the Epistle and Gospel and other Scriptures appointed for the day. Should they not he chosen with the same care as hymns are chosen?

The Old Testament lessons are mostly inord- inately long, and, if read. with expression, they Anake a big demand on the service time. Some of them, moreover, as the record of vindictive- ness and slaughter, are not desirable reading in public; the indelicacy which SO often marks them ought not to be inflicted on mixed con- gregations,
