WOMAN'S WORLD.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

The Need for Economy.

The food supply of these islands may, in the near future, become a very serious ques- tion, and it is incumbent on etery housewife to practise a Wise economy in time. Waste not, want not, and take care of the pence, &c., are two proverbs that should be kept in mind by every man and woman to-day. The abund- ance of food of all kinds in the past has tended to make us a wasteful nation, and insistence on economy in the household has been con- sidered akin to meanness. The war has changed this, as well as many other notions.

Neglected Foods. Many so-called weeds are edible and were formerly used as food. The tender shoots of the nettle, for instance, are not only appetis- ing, but of great value medicinally. The stems of charlock and wild mustard are excel- lent if boiled. The Japanese eat with great relish the fronds of fhe bracken fern, which resemble asparagus. Sorrel used to be exten- sively eaten. It grows freely, almost every- where, and makes a piquant addition to an ordinary salad. The young leaves of the dandelion and those of the chicory plant are also valuable foods. . The leaves of beetroot are excellent served like spinach, and even flowers may be added • to the list of neglected foods. The blossoms of the nasturtium are by no means out of place in the'salad bowl; the leaves also may. be added, whilst both make appetising and wholesome sandwiches. Instances of waste and neglect of good food that Nature lavishly supplies free of cost might be multiplied indefinitely, to say noth- ing of the thousands of tons of mushrooms which are yearly left to rot on the ground.

lIOIIE NOTES.

Profitable Recipes and Useful

Suggestions.

Eggs an Casserole. Ingredients: 4oz. of grated cheese, 4 eggs, 1 shallot, 11oz. of butter, some crushed grape-nuts, salt, pepper, and chopped parsley. Method: Mince the shallot and fry it lightly in the butter; then pour it into a fireproof dish, and spread about so as to grease the dish well. Sprinkle thickly with cheese and crushed grape- nuts. Break the eggs carefully on to the cheese, sprinkle with pepper and salt, and cover with grated cheese and grape-nuts. Put some small pieces of butter on the top, bake in a moderate oven for about ten or twelve minutes, and serve immediately. Baked Souffle Potatoes.

Select potatoes which are of the same size. Wash and scrub them thoroughly, mid bake in a very hot oven. As soon as tender, cut the top from each and scoop the potato into a heated bowl. Mash smoothly, adding for each potato half a tablespoonful of butter and one tablespoon ful of cream. , Season lightly, and whip until light. For half a dozen potatoes, whip the whites of two eggs to a stiff, dry froth, cut them lightly into the mixture, and refill the shells. Arrange in a pan, and return to the oven until heated through and a pale brown on top. Rice and Oatmeal Pudding.

Put a teaspoonful of rice alkd a teaspoonful of coarse oatmeal in a saucepan with enough water to cover all, and simmer till the rice is half cooked. Add 2oz. of chopped suet, some grated cheese, and bake in a pie-dish. Baby's Cot.

A baby should never be allowed to sleep in the same bed as its mother or _nurse, says Maud, in " Farm and Home?' • There is always the danger of its being overlaid, and it is essential therefore that it should have its own little cot from the very first. A cradle should be provided and baby should sleep in it the greater part of the day. This should not be placed on the floor, bat either on a stand or on two chairs arranged in such a way that their backs prevent it from being pulled over by other children. An ordinary washing basket, a Japanese basket, or empty banana crate all make excellent cradles. Whichever is selected it should be lined with some washing material, this keeps out any draughts, and helps to maintain the warmth of the child. The mattress should be fairly thick and stuffed with fine hail; a frequent cause of chilliness is too thin a. mattress or too little clothing

The Summer Flounced Dress

The advent of the summer flounced frock will be hailed with delight by the.young girl, but it can also be arranged to suit women of all ages. Flounces of white darned net, lace, or embroidered lawn, chiffon and ninon are the best adapted for the youthful, whilst those of lace, in black, silvery white, dull gold, and other shades, are more suitable for the elderly. One very deep flounce starting from the waist, and reaching below the knee, with a narrower one below it, is as modish as the skirt con- sisting of three or more flounces of equal depth. Sometimes- the flounces of more sub- stantial materials such as baptiste are battle- mented, and as many as four of these appeared on one model. A jacket of taffetas, with dainty waistcoat, may be worn with this type of skirt; the corsage is -studiously simple, and very often picturesque. The upper one of two flounces is generally •wired invisibly, to give a slight crinoline effect. -White lace flounces are often made up on a coloured silk foundation, which shows through.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0275.txt | included=true | included =====
The Vogue of the Coatee.

Coatees vie with collars in variety of design anAl material. Russia provided the original idea of the loose coatee, which lends itself so admirably ,to summer materials, such as voiles, crepons, linens and niiion. Zouave boleros in taffetas gain in favour every day, for they are very original, and altogether feminine. They form an attractive accessory to the afternoon gown, and in many instances are sleeveless. The loose coatee is undoubtedly the most suitable garment for wearing with the full short skirt. It is often scalloped and braided, but no amateur should attempt either performance. A novel coatee of the Moyen Age type has a rucked front covering the hips, add a sash with ends falling in front. A very pretty high collar, quite one of the newest to be worn with a coatee or blouse, was finished at the back with a bow of ribbon, from which depended long streamers reaching to the waist.

under the child. The pillow should be well filled with down and fairly flat. The sheets must be of soft fine twill or cotton, but never of linen. The blankets must be warm and light. A woollen coverlet gives extra warmth in winter time. A small india-rubber hot water bottle covered in a flannel jacket is of great use, and helps to maintain warmth. A square of mackintosh should be used under the bottom sheet or blanket, but this should never be necessary after the first few iteeks, except in cases of illness. Tiny babies should at first sleep in blankets. Everything should be thoroughly warmed before the baby is put into its cot; this prevents him getting cold and ensures a sound sleep.

A Modern Girl.

" Did you break this vase, Priscilla?"

" It would be somewhat at variance with the truth, madam, personally to assume the fracture. The vase slipped from my fingers. It was the abrupt collision with the floor that caused its disintegration."

===== 0002601_19150522_art0281.txt | included=true | included =====
Charming Millinery.

There is no denying the charm of this season's millinery, inspired for the most part by the modes prevalent in Early Victorian days. Hats may be divided into two classes, the wide- brimmed and the very small brimless shapes that fit close down on the hair. The decora- tions of the former are flat, a single feather or mount completing the trimming; the latter have a wreath round the crown, or a cluster of flowers in front and generally a floating veil behind. White felt hats will be as popular this summer as last; they are to be had in the prettiest shades, and are very simply trimmed. Dunstable straws have come back again, and in large sailors of the Spanish type, have most gracefully curved brims. Some small hats have no trimming at all, unless the two enormous pins, placed on either side, may be so desig- nated. Fashionable Jewellery.

Rubies, emeralds and diamonds are not now considered in good taste. Leaders of fashion have laid them aside for the present, and the semi-precious stones are worn in their place. The deep blue ameythists of Achil Island, and the sea-green aquamarine, of Kerry, are much in evidence. Green gems are greatly favoured, and many women are wearing olivines, peridots and tourmalines.. Topazes aml zercons—the latter are brownish yellow in colour—are also sought after. Green jade bracelets are very fashionable, and much pink coral is seen. Garnets too, have been. revited. They are very handsome stones, and were greatly prized by our grandmothers. Those people who possess the sets formerly displayed in Continental watering places limy just now consider them- selves lucky.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0282.txt | included=true | included =====
The Patron Saint of London.

That London has a patron saint is a fact, of which most Londoners are ignorant, nor is his name even known, or familiar .to the inhabi- tants of the Metropolis. The /first Christian Cathedral in London was erected on the site of the Roman camp and altar to Diana, and St. Mellitus was its first bishop. As the patron saint of the City of London his name has lately been placed on a new banner, borne in processions on festival days in St. Paul's, and thus interest has been aroused in this bishop who lived in Saxon times. King Ethelbert, granted certain lands in Essex to the bishop, for the maintenance of the cathedral.

HINTs ON HEALTH.

Useful Information in Popular

Language.

The French Peasant and Effects of Drink.

A French (lector driven from his region of the North of France had to find a practice else- where, so he went West and is now practising as a country doctor. He found that the farmers and peasants had learnt how to distil spirits from any kind of vegetable matter, such as rotten potatoes, and the consequences were sometimes deplorable. The still was never idle, not even on Sundays. Every hoase had its own still. The war hindered it not at all. The smallest establishment gets 40 or 50 litres of potato eau de vie. jt was on offer everywhere, and no occasion to drink was neglected. The drinkers were unconscious of the, danger in- volved in its use. Ilpilepsy was one conse- quence, tuberculosis another; it was rare to see an aged person in that region. Suicides, violent deaths, accidents of all sorts that go with alco- holism were of daily occurrence. Influenza, else), easily complicated with pneumonia, is nearly always fatal to the hard drinker.. The doctor presages a speedy end to the race, once so frugal and industrious, unless the " powers " interfere to deprive these peasants,of their un- fortunate privilege of distilling their own poison. The Effect of Environment.

The Hampstead Garden Suburb is proving a desirable place of residence for children, so far as their health is concerned. Dr. F. W. Andrew, medical officer of health for Hendon—in which urban district the suburb is included—gives in his last annual report the results of his inspec- tion of the children in the elementary schools of the district. They prove the same tendency that has been. noted at Port Sunlight and at Bournville, for the children brought up amid natural conditions to be sturdier of limb and greater in stature than those who dwell in ordinary districts. Other factors than housing doubtless enter into the comparison, but the following figures with regard to the heights of boys in the garden suburb school and the over- age for all the schools in the district of Hendon is significant _

5-6 yrs. 6-7 yys. 7-8 yrs. ft. in. ft. in. ft. in. Garden suburb school 3 7 3 91 3 lit Hendon schools .

In the case of girls there is a similar increase of about one inch in height at each series of years; with regard to weight equally .satisfac- tory results are observed.—". The Medical Officer."

NO SCARECROWS NEEDED.

A farmer recently paid a visit to a neigh- bour, and as he passed along by the side of the fields he made a mental note of the fact that no scarecrows were visible. Meeting his neighbour almost immediately, he opened the conversation as follows :

"Good morning, Mr. Oates. I see you have no scarecrows in your field. How do you manage without them?" "Oh! well enough," was the innocent reply. "You see I don't need 'em, for I'm in the field all day myself."

JOHNNY'S KINDNESS,

I found him in the nursery This afternoon at two. The tears were rolling woefully From out his eyes of blue. He looked at me with angry frown, And cried in peevish tone, "Mother and Maud have gone to town, And left me here alone." "But, Johnny, dear," I kindly said, "You're very small to take; 'Come, share with me .a feast instead Of plum and currant cake!" But darker grew poor Johnny's gloom. He looked at me and said, "Make sister Maudie stay at home, And let me go instead. They only took me yesterday." (He •rubbed his tearful brow.) "Oh, take that nasty cake away! I do not want it now." He pushed aside the plums and all, However sweet and brown, - • And louder still I heard him bawl, " I want to go to town." But ere the clock was striking three I had a caller shy, He'd come to make it up with. me, And all his tears were dry. DEMOCRACY AND WAR.

New Union To Provide For International Peace.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0288.txt | included=true | included =====
SOME NOVEL PROPOSALS.

I.—No province shall be transferred from one Government to another without the consent by plebiscite or otherwise, of the population of such province. 2.—No treaty, arrangement or undertaking shall be entered upon in the name of Great Britain without the sanction of Parliament. Adequate machinery for ensuring democratic control of foreign policy shall be created. 3.—The foreign policy of Great Britain shall not be aimed at creating alliances for the pur- pose of maintaining the balance of power, but shall be directed to concerted action between the Powers, and the setting up of an Inter- national Council whose deliberations and decisions shall be public, with such machinery for securing international agreement as shall be the guarantee of an abiding peace. 4.—Great Britain shall propose as part of the peace settlement a plan for the drastic reduc- tion, by consent, of the armaments of all the belligerent Powers, and to facilitate that policy shall attempt to secure the general nationalisa- tion of the manufacture of armaments, and the control of the export of armaments by one country to another.

These are the four cardinal points in the policy of a new movement—the Union of Demo- cratic. Control—the inauguration of which is an outcome of the war. Under the auspices of the Warrington branch, a well-attended and animated- inectin; was, recently helti in the Friends' Meeting house, Buttermarket-street. Mr. J. A. Ransome (president) was in ;the chair, and an address was given by the Rev. Dfinnico (late of Warrington). Mr. H. Sherburn (secretary) was also present. The policy of the movement was discussed at length.

Nlll. CIIFIELD :AND GERMANY.

Democratic Control Of International

Affairs.

Mr. A. H. Cros4eld, who had received an invitation to attend, wrote a letter to Mr. Hey-wood explaining that it was impossible for him to be present, but expressing the hope that the following points might be emphasised in the speeches :- 1. That all Prussian militarism should be crushed, but no patched-up peace should be tolerated, but that the Allied Powers, which, after all, stand not only for justice to the smaller nations of Europe, and their protec- tion, but for civilisation and progress, should vindicate these great causes and establish their authority in such a way as to make it for. ever impossible for Germany and Austria to repeat so horrible a crime against humanity.

2. That the nations- who are guifiy: of this colossal crime should be punished for it, aml compelled to make such partial reparation as alone is possible, first to lielgium and next to others of the Allies.

3. That while punishment must be inflicted and reparation made, these nations should yet have their opportunity, in the course of gener- ations to come, to expiate their crime and atone for it so that in the future, under the wiser guidance of democratic rule and influ- ence, and changed in mind and spirit, they may yet be regenerated, and yet share in the useful and progressive work of civilisation. 4. That the futile attempt to maintain a balance of power in Europe, with all its vicious and fatal consequences, may give place to one powerful alliance, strong enough to guarantee the rights and independent exist- ence. of all nations.

s..lJiat • with a view to this being carried into effect the minciples of nationality would to the fullest extent that is practicable and possible be recognised, when the time comes to arrange the conditions of peace—a time when to no small extent it will certainly be necessary to re-draw the map of Europe. 6. If this policy is followed it will follow from it that secret diplomacy and secret nego- tiations would to a large extent disappear, while secret international treaties would be abolished altogether, democratic government exercising an amount of authority and control in foreign affairs beyond anything hitherto known in our political history, and in the political history of Europe. ORIGIN OF THE MOVEMENT.

The Chairman said that, to those men who had been working for good-feeling among. the nations, the war came as a terrible catas- trophe; and some of them—all very prominent and influential persons—decided to start this new movement. Owing to the condition of things; and the public .feeling, they had thought it wise to organise quietly—which had been ,well done in the various towns during the winter. Mr. liansome then read out the objects, and asked for discussion thereon. A Member: How would you propose to get the, other Powers to agree to this? (Hear, hear.)

Mr. Plinston: The principle of democratic control is that the people rule. If democratic control is obtained, the people can elect repre- sentation to the other Powers. Of course, after that was declared we should have to trult to their honour. That is the only way. I think the peoples of all the other Powers are satisfied in their bitn minds that war must not even be mentioned again, and the people will be in a large majority.

The Chairman: Now naiion can really want war. We went into it because we were bound by treaties.

THE CALL FOR EDUCATION.

Mr. Plinston : War brings out all the beast- lusts of a, people. How then are you going to get that ideal state? Only by education! It is the duty of us who are not taking part in the conilict to educate the people towards that end.

The Chairman: That is exactly the idea of the 'Union. There is no doubt that the more we can get the peoples of all countries to think, the sooner we shall put an end to war. 'HOW DEMOCRACY WILL GAIN POWER. Rev. H. T. Dixon (of Penketh) asked how they were going to get democratic control? What he was most concerned .about was that democracy was becoming . less and less to them. England never had been a democratic country, and they had scarcely a shred of real democracy left. The people had abso- lutely no power; and before they could do what was suggested—send a representative and " all that sort of thing "—they would in some way have 'to get in power. Personally he could not see any hope of getting that out of this war, unless the war was followed by a revolution. That was how the people must get in power; and then obtain education and enlightening in their own lands!

LIBERAL CLUB BOWLING M

The first monthly bowling main in connection with the Liberal Club was held on Saturday, and proved very suecessfnl. The prize-winners were: 1, E. Lunt; 2, J. Lawless; 3, T. O'Brien; 4, W. Bibby. The game between Thomas Shaw and Wm. Bibby fot the fourth prize was very ,close. They tied at 14 all, and it fell to Bibby to get in _

the winting wood. Also the game between T. O'Brien and J. Lawless was ken, Lawless just getting in the winning wood. The finalists were E. Lunt and J. Lawless. The former played a good game, and finished with the score of 15 to 10. The arrangements for the main were car- ried out by Messrs. Thomas Shaw, J. Collins and G. Marshall (secretary of the bowling section of tie club).

LOCAL BOWLERS WHO HAVE "JOINED UP."

The Warrington Bowling Clubs have contri- buted many a man to the ranks of the gallant Army who are upholding the national prestige on the field of war. From the Bull's Head

Club have gone :—W. Daniels, G. Hackett, J. Massey, H. Fox, W. Andrews, G. Shaw (No. 2), L. Hockenhall, H. Hockenhall, H. Rowley, H. Williams, J. Bate, E. Lunt, J. Simister, and C. Regan. Regan, as already reported in our columns, has been wounded, as, also, has Lunt, whilst Simister is missing.

ASSOCIATION PLAYERS

" Join Up."

On Monday, Denny Vernon and Percy- Chal- linor, two of the most popular junior players and members of the St. Barnabas' Football Club, along with another Fairfield Athletic footballer, Bate, joined the Royal Fusiliers and left Warrington Central Station by the 11.30 train en route for I,kndon. Several of their playing comrades accompanied them to the station to watch them off, and gave hearty cheers as the train departed from the plat- form. There are now twelve members' of the St. Barnabas' Football Club ih the ranks. amongst whom, unfortunately, Spruce and Hunt have lately been renorted as wounded. Of the Fairfield iithletic Football Club there are eight other members serving at the present time.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0313.txt | included=true | included =====
BRITISH COLUMBIA FOR THE SPORTSMAN.

British Columbia is called the "Sportsman's I Paradise." In this province is to be found a larger variety of game and fish than-in any other part of the continent. Grizzly bears i roam in the interior fastnesses, black bears can be found in all parts of the province, big- horn sheep, goat and caribou abound in the mountain regions, while deer of several varie- ties are to be found all over the province. Elk shooting may be had on the northern portion of Vancouver Island, while panthers are bagged on the coast and in the interior. Of birds there are five species of grouse, all kinds of wild fowl, and on the coast the pheasant affords splendid wing shooting. Fishing is equally good, salmon and trout being the prin- cipal objects of the sportsman's skill. The former are caught with spoon bait in the salt water, as British Columbia salmon do not %gaily rise te the fly.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0314.txt | included=true | included =====
NO PROFESSIONAL FOOTBALL.

Warrington Club Committee's War-Time Decision.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0315.txt | included=true | included =====
£3OO DEFICIT ON LAST SEASON.

I am sure that it is the unanimous wish of every member of the club that we should not, so long as the war lasts, engage in professional football So far as our committee are concerned, we have passed a resolution that no matter what may take place in the Northern Union, we will not be a party to professional football so long as the war lasts.

The foregoing statement—perhaps the most important from the point of view of public in- terest—was made by Councillor E. Osborne, the president 'of the Warrington rootball Club, at the annual meeting of that body, which was held in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street, on Thursday evening, and which, contrary to the usual experience, was very sparsely attended by the members, though the Chairman was sup- ported on the platform by the majority of the members of the committee and by other officials, including the Hon. Secretary (Mr. T. P. Brad- shaw) and the Hon. Treasurer (Mr. T. Pem- berton). The President, alluding to the unwontedly small attendance, said that it seemed as if the club had almost as much difficulty in raising a quorum as they had in raising a team, a remark which was received with laughter. Con- tinuing, he said that they met under circum- stances that evening that no one could possibly have anticipated at the last annual meeting of the club. The season just ended had been a most trying one for all concerned, and if it had not been for the loyalty of officials, committee, members and players he was sure they would have been in a much worse condition than the reports showed. Judging by the statements that had been made to him by the various representatives, he thought they would compare very favourably—financially at any rate—with most other clubs. Their position in the League Table wa,s not so good as last year, but when they sonsidered that, on many occasions the number of players had only just been sufficient to make up a team, owing to the players join- ing the Colours, they were not downhearted, but had reason to rejoice in the fact.

would be able to build up a team that should be capable of beating the best team in the Northern Union.

HONORARIA "AS USUAL "

It was decided to vote the officials the usual honoraria, viz., secretary 05; treasurer .05, and auditors 0 eaeh.

- AND SUBSCRIPTIONS WANTED "AS USUAL"

Mr. T. England proposed a vote of thanks to the committee for, their services during the past yearl and hoped that next year, the mem- bers wcArld be able to meet under happier auspices, and that the club would go on in a successful manner. He further hoped that none of the members would neglect to pay their sub- scriptions because of the altered conditions, but that they would loyally support the club. Mr. G. Wright, in seconding, thought the members ought to be as loyal to the club as the players who had gone to the Front had been to the country and should pay their subscrip- tion even if there was football or not. The President said that for a few months, at any rate during the beginning of the season, it might be hard on some members to continue their subscription, but he could assure them that if they came forward and gave them the same support as in the past, the committee would deal with the sum collected as carefully as possible. The money would not be squandered, and the matter was one which rested entirely with the members themselves. He hoped that they would come forward as one man and say "There's no football, but you need ray money and there it is !" On the other hand, if some of the members were hard hit, the coin- mittee did not want them to feel that they were out of it.

Mr. Pemberton said that it had been a very anxious time for the committee and officials, as well as a very disheartening time, but they had gone on with their work in very much the same manner as they would have done had the times been normal.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0316.txt | included=true | included =====
Finances Better Than Expected.

Financially, they had come out much better than was expected. The committee had anticipated a loss and set themselves quite early in the season to make as little .as pos- sible. By caiefully watching the pence they had made it very much less than at one time looked possible. Wages, refreshments, clothing. transfer fees, field expenses, printing, &c., were all considerably reduced. Of course, en the other side, the gate receipts and subscriptions were very much down also. The loss for the year as it appeared in the accounts was 2.521 10s-. 9d. That was after allowing for deprecia- tion of property £127 16s. 6d., Intermediate League £65 is., sd. and Schoolboys' League £l7 7s. id., a total 'of £2lO ss. The sum of £lO had since been received. The accounts would show a net loss of £3OO, which, considering all things, was a very creditable state of affairs. He moved the adoption of the reports, and con- gratulated the secretary and treasurer upon the very clear arid concise manner in which they had presented them to the meeting.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0317.txt | included=true | included =====
"THE BRIGHTEST SPOT IN THE REPORT.

Mr. T. England, in seconding, said that that was the first time in the history of the club that he was thankful that they had not won the cup or the League championship; he be- lieved that it would have been no honour and glory to have done so. To his mind the brightest spot in the report was that so many members, players and otherwise, had enlisted and gone to the Front, and in that connection

he felt sure that such men as Skelhorne, Naylor and Fish would give excellent accounts of themselves. (Applause.)_

The proposition was carried

THE OFFICERS RE-ELECTED EN BLOC. It was announced that the officers for the ensuing year bad been re-elected en bloc, as follows:—President, Councillor E. Osborne; lion. secretary, Mr. T. P. Bradshaw; hon. treasurer. Mr. T. Pemberton; committee, Messrs. F. Barber. T. Bunch. T. Barnes, J. F. Foster, W. Hill, W. Mason. J. T. Sampson and J.. Winstanley; auditors, Messrs.' W. Webster and George Gillin; trustees, Messrs. T. Locker and F. Barber.

PROOF OF THE MEMBERS' APPRECIATION. The President said that the fact that there had not been a single new nomination for any of the positions was in itself a proof of the appreciation of the members of the services which the committee and other officers had rendered during a very trying time. When things became normal again no doubt they would have the usual heated competition for places, and critics on the floor of the house would be as eager as ever to " put`the officials through it." (Laughter.) Well; the sooner such times came the better would the officers appreciate it. That night they felt that they were on different ground altogether and, whilst they realised that it was an honour to be re- elected, yet at the same time they realised that that re-election was due very largely to the state of affairs which the country was in at the pre- sent time. As to the programme for next season, he did not know whether they had all seen the newspaper with regard to racing in war time, but if they had they would have seen that the Government had asked for the general suspension of racing in Great Britain after the present week until the war was ove, and that the stewards of the Jockey Club, without a moment's hesitation, had complied with the request and, from Saturday, would not sanction any raaing meetings with the- solitary exception. of those at Newmarket.

No Professional Football in War Time. What was the position with regard to pro- fessional football? He was sure it was the unanimous wish of every member of the club that, so long as the war lasted, they should not engage in professional football. What had been suggested and would probably be brought about was that they should encourage the Schoolboys and Intermediate Leagues. If any football was played it should be played by them and not by men who were considered to .be fit for active. service. (Hear, hear.) This matter would be fully discussed at the annual meeting of the Northern Union, but he might say that from, the expressions of opinion he had heard with regard to it that the consensus of opinion seemed to be that they would be doing what was right, fair and honest to the fellows who had gone abroad by suspending professionalism during the continuation of the war.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0318.txt | included=true | included =====
Warrington Committee Against War-Time

Professionalism.

• So far as the Warrington committee were con- cerned, they had passed a .resolution that no matter..what might take place in the Northern Union they would not be a party to professional football so long as the war lasted. (Applause.) They, were quite determined about that.. One /natter Which. ..concerned them was the financial side of the question. Practically they would be. compelled to provide for about £3OO to meet rent, rates and taxes, the groundsmen's wages, cost of painting, &c. They had already in the bank £240, and they had a further £lO which. they had received from the League, making altogether £250, so that if no football took place. they would have to meet a liability of. probably, £5O. Well, that was not very much, and he was sure that the members of the War- rington Football Club would show their loyalty and that if it was found necessary to find that £5O they 'would give something towards it. (Hear, hear.) If it was found necessorY to fix a levy to raisq that £so—he did 'not know that it would be necessary—it would only be a small matter per, head, seeing that they had nearly a thousand Members.

They hoped 'that they would soon be able to welcome back the players who had gone to fight the country's battles, also the many members who were likewise doing their share, and that when .that happy re-union Wok place they

===== 0002601_19150522_art0319.txt | included=true | included =====
RUGBY FOOTBALL.

• Troubles The Future May Have hi

Store.

The Northern Union League will hold their annual meeting ia June, and in the meantime the question as to die advisability of proceed- ing with the game next season is being freely discussed. There is a general disposition not to prejudice the situation one way or the 'other by making premature resolutions, and it is prob- able an open mind- will be maintained until late in the summer, wheu. the actual state of things on the eve of the season will be tolerably well known.

Many serious points to be borne in mind in- clude the following: That if football is sus- pended the rents of the grounds will still have to be met; that many players have and will continue to join the colours; that if football is continued the support will be less even than during last season.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0320.txt | included=true | included =====
Grants to Necessitous Clubs.

The Northern Rugby League Committee met in Manchester on Tuesday, when tile treasurer reported that the Relief Fund had received the um of -QlB5 9s. id., which represented the con- tributions from players who had received their wages in full.

The committee decided to make grants to that amount to those clubs whose players had accepted substantial reductions of wages for dis- tribution amongst their players in proportion to the sacrifices they had made.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0321.txt | included=true | included =====
Claim Not a Good One.

At the subsequent meeting of the Union Com- mittee the Broughton Rangers Club made a claim against the Salford Club for loss of gate receipts on account of their match peing post- poned from Christmas Day to April 17. The committee decided that there were no grounds for a claim for compensation, but allowed the Broughton Club the sum of .R 7 19s. for expenses incurred in the preparation of the first match.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, MAY 22, 1915.

MR. CROSFIELD AND AUSTRALIAN EX-PREMIER

Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield and Miss Elliadi were present at the luncheon given in the Harcourt Room in the House of Commons this week in honour of the Hon. W. C. Watson, the ex-Labour Prime Minister of Australia. Mr. Watson made an admirable speech on that occasion, and both at the luncheon and at the great and representative gathering in the Guildhall the intense spirit of patriotism shown testified splendidly to Imperial solid- arity and power. While Mrs. Crosfield and Miss Truda Crosfield were busy all day at the meeting of the Women's National Liberal Federation on Wednesday, Mr. Crosfield and Miss Elliadi attended the memorable and very historic gathering in the Guildhall.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0340.txt | included=true | included =====
ASYLUM AS HOSPITAL.

The Winwick County Asylum has been trans- formed into a military hospital,, capable, if necessary, of accommodating no fewer than 3,000 men. Already a large number of patients have been received, included among them being a number of Australians, who received their injuries in the recent fighting in the 'Dar- danelles. The first batch of patients arrived on Friday of last week, and during the week there have been other arrivals, the total num- ber now quartered in the building—now tem- porarily known, by the way, as the Lord Derby Hospital—being about 400.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0341.txt | included=true | included =====
MUNICIPAL EMPLOYES AND WAR SERVICE.

Trades Council To Keep An Eye

On The Matter.

Mr. Charles Dukes called attention at a meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council to "the inducement tto get all municipal and urban councils to re- lease as many of their employes as possible, so that they may be moved to places where munitions of war are to be produced." Some Councils, he said, were making war service on the part of certain of their employes a matter of coercion without paying regard to the fact that if they were taken from their homes and their regular work, and set to producing war materials, the men "should be compensated to the degree of their loss." The men were being told that their services would be re- quired anywhere at the will of the Govern- ment, and he thought that the least that could be expected was that they should be treated as well as "the khaki dockers," and that men who were called upon for the special service suggested should receive, in addition to their wages, either a soldier's pay or the equivalent of their maintenance whilst divOrced from their homes and routine occu- pations. He also thought that the factories at present turning out the ordinary materials of commerce, but which were capable of adap- tation to the manufacture of war necessities, should be utilised before men were coerced into leaving their homes and their normal occupations to take up special war work.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0342.txt | included=true | included =====
No Coercion Locally.

So far nothing had been done locally in this matter of coercing municipal employes, but a census had been taken as to what the men in the employ of the Council could do and as to who, among them, could be spared. Up to the present, however, he had not heard of any men being transferred. The Council, he thought, should insist that men should not be transferred unless they were to receive an equivalent to the loss such transference would entail.

"Mayor Makes No Bones About It."

The Chairman (Mr. Hamblett) : The Mayor makes no bones about it. He says he will bring pressure to bear wherever it is possible. Indeed, pressure is being brought to hear in an ingenious manner, And it is up to as to watch this matter, very, very , closely. It was decided that in the event of any stepg being taken locally in the Matter the Exe'cu- tive Committee of the Trades Council should have power to take what steps they might consider necessary.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0343.txt | included=true | included =====
NO COMPULSION.

On making inquiries from the Town Clerk we find that no coercion is taking lace with the Corporation employes. The following circular has been posted in the Corporation Departments :

Government War Work.

The Government are urgently in need of men in connection with the output of munitions. All men who are desirous of assisting are 'requested to give in their names at once. All men will be reinstated after the war. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk.

In the event of men giving in their names, and it being found possible to release them, notification would be made to the Board of Trade, and the men would be at perfect liberty to acceptor refuse any job offered. Ile had already pointed out to . the Local Government Board the difficulty with reference to the mov- ing of a man, to another town, and it was only right that every man should be compensated to the degree of his loss; but in the case of the Cor- poration employes there wits no compulsion at all, every man was perfec ly free to make his own choice. Under the present system there could be no compulsory transference by the Corporation.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0344.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE MR. H. R. HOROBIN.

The death occurred on Wednesday of Mr. Henry Richard Horobin, of 51, Sankey-street. He was 66 years of age, and had not been in good health for a considerable time, suffering from heart and lung trouble. He came to War- rington over forty years ago, and after serving 38 years in the telegraphic department on Bank Quay Station, retired in 1902. His second son, Mr. Charles Horobin, was recently appointed .water engineer of the city of Lincoln. The youngest son is serving with the 18th Service Battalion of the King's Liverpool Regiment. The deceased leaves four sons and two daugh- ters. The funeral took place at the Cemetery, the service being conducted by the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton. The mourners included Messrs. MR. T. J. RIDGWAVS SON WOUNDED.

Local Australian In The Dardanelles.

News has been received by Mr. and Mrs. T. J. Ridgway, of Lyrum, that their younger son has been wounded in land fighting in the Dar- danelles. The news is communicated by the High Commissioner of Australia. Lieutenant Althorp Ridgway belongs to the Australian contingent—which he has only joined since the outbreak of war. In peace times he holds the highly important position of Assist- ant White House Engineer to the Common- wealth of Australia. He is brother of Captain T. Ridgway, of the Cheshires. It is not yet known how he has been wounded.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0347.txt | included=true | included =====
INSURANCE ACT CHANGES.

Scheme For Centralisation In The

Order.

In addition to the customary conference busi- ness of the routine character, proposals of an important character will be considered on the initiative of the Board of Management with regard to the centralisation in the Order for the purposes of National Insurance Act administration. These proposals have been instrumental in attracting the attendance of what will be a record number of delegates. - The scheme for centralising in groups has for its object the banding together of certain dis- tricts for mutual assistance, or for "spreading the risks," in dealing with the delicate claims of National Insurance; but it also involves, as a matter of course, an alteration in the proportion of funds allocated to general funds and retained by the branches. It was thought in the early stages of the consideration of the proposals, says the quarterly report of the Board of Management, that a majority of votes of the delegates at the A.M.D. cast in favour of the centralisation scheme, would be binding on all the branches. Regulations have, however, been recently issued by the Insurance Commissioners governing amalgamation and transfer of engagements under the National Insurance Acts, and by virtue of those regulations the Commissioners have intimated that every brartth having 50 or more insured members must be given the .option of deciding for itself whether or not it will transfer its engagements to the Order. Already a number of branches, Including some of the larger ones, have intimated that they are in favour of the proposals, and have passed the prescribed resolution transferring their engage- ments to the Order as from the Ist January next. On the other hand a number of branches have rejected the proposals, or at any rate have not passed the resolution.

As there is local option on the question it is suggested that those branches which do not at the moment favour the scheme of centralisa- tion should not attempt to prevent the setting up of 'machinery by the Order for the adminis- tration of the National Insurance Acts in respect of those branches that hold different views. Some provision will have to be made without delay for spreading the risks of the branches that have not a sufficient number of members to constitute an average, and the simplest way out of the difficulty is for those branches to transfer their engagements to the Order, and for the A.M.D. to permit them to do so.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0348.txt | included=true | included =====
The Opposition.

No great objection has been raised to the principle of centralisation; indeed it would be difficult to conceive arguments in opposition to proposals that offer greater security to the mem- bers for obtaining the full rate of benefits after the first valuation. What opposition there is appears to be based largely on the question of administration expenses, the pro- portion available for branches being neces- sarily somewhat reduced. In view of the vested interests that have been created, such opposition was, of course, not unexpected, and we are not disposed to regard it as altogether unreason- able. The result of the first valuation, how- ever, may be the means of awakening the mem- bers to a greater sense of their responsibilities, and if this be the case and a more lively in- terest is taken by them in the government of the Society in the future, • then we have no fear as to what their decision will ultimately be. The present constitution of the Order of Druids in common with that of other Societies of a similar type, provides for the branches being almost entirely self-governed. That the time has arrived when some extension or total change in the constitution should be made, at any rate in respect of National Health Insur- ance, in order to spread the risks of individuals over a wider membership, may be readily appreciated by a careful perusal of the report by the Order's actuaries on the last quin- quennial valuation of the Order. In a supplementary report issued by the Board of Management, it is stated that the Board un- hesitatingly aver that a bold step towards cen- tralisation of the business of the society under the National Insurance Act would be in the best interests of its members, who are insured under the Act. The existing branches of the society with their officials and staff assistants would continue to be utilised, but the.V would. under the centralised system. act as agents of the society itself in respect of National Health Insurance, rather than as separate entities.

KILLED WHILST WAVING TO COMRADE.

Private Henry Taylor, of No. 1 Company of the National Reserve, 2nd/4th South Lancs. Regiment, was buried with full military honours in Stockton Heath Churchyard on Tuesday. Private Taylor was killed at Honey. Surrey, on Friday of last week, whilst engaged in guarding the London. Brighton and South Coast Railway Bridge there. He was knocked down by a slow train whilst returning the salute of a waving comrade in a passing express. Death was instantaneous. The funeral, which was attended by a large number of comrades-in-arms, from various regiments, under Lieutenants C. L. Williamson and Bur- gess, of the Warrington Territorials, was of a very impressive nature.

\ AIIINISTERED ALIEN.

Case Against A Brazilian (ad Dismissed.

Miss Pulseria Maria De Nacimento, Brazilian, was summoned at a special sitting of the Daresbury Petty Sess:onal Bench, c Thursday, for being an alien, and residing within a .prohibited area without registering herself, at Walton Inferior, on May 19th. Mr. George Wallington, of Walton Inferior, was summoned at the same time for failing to give notice of the presence of an alien in his house- hold.

Mr. H. Roberts and Mr. J. A. Ransome were on the Bench.

Defendants pleaded guilty. A Prohibited Area.

Superintendent Owen said that Walton Inferior was a prohibited area, ,and any alien staying within that area was expected to regis- ter. It was also the duty ç the person with whom the alien lodged to give notice to the police. The female defendant was born in Brazil, and was therefore an American sub- ject. She came to reside at Walton Inferior on May Bth, but it was not until the previous day (Wednesday), when' Inspector Breese met her at Stockton Heath, that the police knew of her presence in the neighbourhood. Inspector Breese said that at 12 noon on Wed- nesday he was in Victoria-avenue, Stockton Heath, when he met the female prisoner. Noticing the foreign appearance, he stopped her and asked her where she came from. She replied "America," and added that she was staying with Mrs. Wallington, an was looking after the baby. Witness made further inquiries and visited Mrs. Wallington, at 98, Ellesmere- road, where she was staying With her father. Witness then saw Mi. Wallington, who said that his daughter was on a visit from America. He admitted that the nurse was not registered, and said that he did not know it was required. He thought it was only Germans and Turks who had to register. Inspector Owen said that he was informed that the female prisoner had been .travelling in England for some little time.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0355.txt | included=true | included =====
A Matter of Inadvertence.

Mr. Wallington said that he did not know that there was any necessity to have the nurse registered. She had been with his daughter in England for some time. They had stayed at Blackpool, Matlock and Southport, and no question had ever been raised as to the neces- sity of the girl being registered. It was purely a matter of inadvertence. The girl did not understand the matter at all. He did not sup- pose she could read any notice that she might have seen about registration. The Chairman (Mr. H. Roberts) said that the Bench quite accepted Mr: Wallington's explanation. It was not one of the serious cases of the kind, or else the Bench would have exercised its full powers. The case against the girl would be dismissed, and Mr. Wallington would be called upon to pay the costs-ss. Mr. Wallington: Thank you.

Temperance Meeting.—A. very interesting meeting was held in the Wesley Hall on Thurs- day evening, when the members of the Stockton Heath Band of Hope, under the direction of Mr. G. ===== 0002601_19150522_art0359.txt | included=true | included =====
GUARDIANS' DILEMMA.

The Education Of The Cottage

Homes' Children.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0360.txt | included=true | included =====
"A REVOLUTIONARY PROPOSAL."

A discussion took place at Wednesday's meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on the subject of the education of the children in the Cottage Homes. The problem hinged on the advisability or undesirability of having the children taught in the Council Schools instead of at the Homes. The principle of the Guardians surrendering part of their con- trol and the financial aspect were the chief points considered.

DIRECTOR OF EDUCATION INTER- VIEWED.

The controversy arose out of the following minute of the Cottage Homes Committee :7 " The Clerk reported that he had interviewed the Director of Education, Warrington, with regard to the amount the Guardians would have to pay if the children resident in the Cottage Homes were sent to the Council School, Oak- wood-avenue, for educational purposes. The Director had informed him that if there were, say, a hundred children in the Cottage Homes of school age, the amount the Guardians would have to pay would be about £l2O per annum. A return was presented showing the present number of children in the Homes to be as fol- lows: Between the ages of seven and fourteen, boys 33, girls 36; between fourteen and sixteen, boys 8, girls 12; under seven years of age, 27; total, 116. Of that number three are Roman Catholic children. It was decided, on the pro- position of Mr. Brooks, seconded by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, that the Board be recom- mended to accept the terms of the Warrington Education Authority as they affected the chil- dren between the ages of seven and fourteen, and that as soon as expedient all the children of those ages be sent to the Council School, Oakwood-avenue, Warrington. It was moved by Mr. Brooks and seconded by the Rev. A. M. Mitchell, that the Local Government Board be asked to sanction the proposal of the Board to appoint Mrs. Lawrance superintendent and matron of the Cottage Homes, at a salary at the rate of £75 per annum, together with emolut meats, as heretofore. On a vote being taken, eight were in favour, none against, and one did not vote. In view of the Roman Catholic children having been removed from the Cot- tage Homes, there is now no further need for the services of the Roman Catholic instructor, and the committee recommended that this office be abolished, and that the Rev. Father Miller be acquainted accordingly." Mr. Mather asked why the matron had not been recommended for an increase of salary, along with the other officials. Mr. Brooks said that Mrs. Lawrance had not asked for an advance in salary.

[The reader will recollect that on the death of Mr. Lawrance, the superintendent of the Homes, Mrs. Lawrance was re-appointed matron, with the additional title of superin- tendent. at a salary of £75 per annum. This appointment had to be endorsed by the Local Government Board before it became fully 'official.—Ed.]

===== 0002601_19150522_art0361.txt | included=true | included =====
FOLLOWING OTHER UNIONS.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell stated that other Unions had done what the Guardians had done in the matter of Mrs. Lawrance. As regarded the question of salary, if they made Mrs. Lawrance's salary £lOO it wouldn't be too much. She was a splendid woman for the position, a fact that was reflected in the high terms in which H.M. Inspector's reports on his visit to the Homes were couched. • The Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) remarked that the action of the Board in regard to Mrs. Lawrance was the only logical step following what they decided some time ago, viz., to send the children outside the Cottage Homes for educational purposes. It was a mistake to think it was going to b economical. But it was not merely a question. of finance; a matter of principle was involved. It meant that the Guardians were not going to carry out part of the duty which devolved upon them—the education of the children under their care. They would be carrying it out in a way, but it did not seem an altogether satisfactory way to leave the education of the children to persons over whom the Guardians had only slight and indirect control.

Mr. Mather said he thought that the fact that the majority of the children concerned were Warrington Children, made them quite eligible to attend the Warrington schools.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0362.txt | included=true | included =====
A DRASTIC CHANGE.

Mr. Billinge said it appeared to him that the whole thing was going to be a drastic change. He thought the members of the Board were not sufficiently informed to form a true opinion in the matter.

The Chairman: :You will be later on. The whole thing is unusual. It's a proposal to revolutionise the whole system. Mr. Mather expressed the opinion that the members should be supplied by the Clerk with particulars of the whole matter Mr. Shaw indignantly declared that it was monstrous that an intelligent body of men should be treated in the way Mr. Mather had suggested the committee should be treated, as though they were not capable of dealing with the business themselves.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said it had been calculated that the change would cost the Guardians £230.

The Chairman asked for .the source of the estimate.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell replied that it was calculated the previous day in committee. The Chairman _pointed out that a calculation made by a member or members of the com- mittee could not be accepted as official.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0363.txt | included=true | included =====
CATHOLIC CHILDREN ALREADY REMOVED.

Miss Broadbent said that the Catholic chil- dren had already been sent from the Homes to the Catholic schools, and added that despite the lamented death of Mr. Lawrance, everything had worked admirably at the Homes; it was only fair to Mrs. Lawrance to remember that. It was quite natural that the Board should want' to have full information on the subject. The matter then dropped, the understanding being that the members should be supplied with the full facts of ,the matter with a view to sub- sequent discussion.

===== 0002601_19150522_art0364.txt | included=true | included =====
"TALKING PICTURES" AT STAR

KINEMA.

At the Star Kinema this week, Warring- tonians have had the opportunity of witnessing performances that are unique locally—for their principal feature has been the kinetophone, that latest and most wonderful of. T. A. Edison's inventions. The kinetophone is the cinemato- graph and phonograph combined, with the result that the pictures " talk " in a wonderful way. There is no flaw in the workings; it is a distinctly new machine; and apparently there is no sound produced by human being or animal which cannot be truly reproduced on the kinetophone with ease. In short, Edison has revolutionised the "living picture" art. At each Kinema performance three separate kinetophonic films are shown.

The -other items in the programmes are tip- top." On the first three days two powerful dramas, "The Miser's Daughter" and "The Veteran" were shown, catering capitally with the topical touch. Beautiful coloured pictures of scenery, and stories of child-life, were given amid loud applause. The striking filmed story of "The Lusitania " is also being shown— backed by a specially strong war selection. to-day's programmes include "Lord Kitchener in France "—a film which will be- come historic, and which should be missed on no account. It is shown by authority of the War Office.

"4 Tillie's Punctured Romance" To Be Repeated. On the last three days of next week the big film which so recently packed the Star Kinema each time it was reeled off, viz., " Tillie's Punctured Romance," will be repeated. The leading line on the first three days will be "Beautiful Jim."

Life and Death. During the week ended May 15th, there wfs,o 32 births and 15 deaths in Warrington, excess of births over deaths being 17. In births there were two more males than fenial% and in the deaths three more males 0,93 females. The death-rate was 10.3 against It, in the corresponding week last year, and birth-rate 22.0 against 27.7. Three of the defil'" were from bronchitis. eSli During the week there were seventeen a- ni cases of scarlet fever, and the total nuixiber - cases of this disease in the hospital was 77*

nice. We are also well looked after. Fdo not know how long I shall be in this hospital, but I hope I shall be in England by the time you get this letter." • Joined Soon After the Outbreak. Private Howarth is a married man, and he resides with his wife at Long-row, Orford. He joined the Territorials on September Ist, and after undergoing his training, first in Scotland and then at Tunbridge Wells, he was drafted to the Front with the initial batch of men, on February 12th. Keen on Bowling. Private' Howarth was educated at Orford School. He was formerly employed at the Orford Tannery. He was very keen on bowl- ing, and had won several prizes. He wor- shipped at Orford Church, and was a member of the Young Men's Bible Class. He belongs to the Order of Oddfellows, and is a member of the Junior Conservative Club. Two of his brothers are with the Colours. One is.. in train- ing with the R.F.A., whilst another is serving at the Front in the same regiment and company as Private Howarth himself. A sten-brother—Corporal Higham, of Orford—was killed in action a short time ago. PRIVATE JOHN HENSHALL Hit In The Shoulder, But Doing Well. Last Saturday Private John Henshall, B Company, 4th South Lancashires, was wounded in the shoulder. He is a married man, and lives at 37, Bewsey-road. When the war broke out he was employed by Messrs. J. Cros- field and Sons in the glycerine department; and, being a Volunteer of seven years' standing, though he had been retired for four years, he rejoined and volunteered for active service. He was a member of the Liberal Club, and was well-known on the bowling green. Reported By Bosom Friend. Mrs. Henshall received the news of the casualty in the following letter, written by Private B. Watson, of the same company, and medical officer's servant :

DIED OF WOUNDS. SECOND-LIEUT. IVAN FULTON-SM ITH. A Good Officer Who Was Promoted From Sergeant. News was received yesterday by Mr. J. Fulton-Smith, of 20, Bold-street, Warrington, that his son, Second-Lieutenant Ivan Fulton- Smith of the 9th Royal Scots Regiment, had died in hospital on the 20th May. The intimation came in the form of a telegraphic message, merely stating that the gallant officer had died as the result of a wound received on the 23rd or 24th April. He was a widower.
